
=== PHYSICAL PAGE 1 ===
ORIUS
Observation–Reality Integrity for Universal Safety
Battery-First Unified Manuscript
Safety Under Degraded Observation
Pratik Niroula
A unified battery-domain manuscript.
ORIUS Safety Principle
“Safety is bounded by observation reliability.”
Revised March 2026

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 2 ===
Abstract
This manuscript develops a battery-first version ofORIUS (Observation–Reality Integrity
for Universal Safety), a framework for safe action under degraded observation in physical
AI systems. The central claim is that a controller can appear safe on observed state while
being physically unsafe on true state when the observation channel degrades. In the battery
domain, this hidden failure takes the form of dispatch decisions that appear feasible under
observed state of charge while violating the true battery envelope.
The manuscript uses grid-scale battery dispatch as the first complete validation surface because
the state, constraints, and telemetry pathways are interpretable, physically meaningful, and
experimentally reproducible. The battery-domain architecture centers onDC3S, a runtime
layer placed at the optimize-to-dispatch boundary.DC3S scores telemetry quality, inflates
uncertainty when reliability weakens, constrains the safe action set, repairs unsafe candidate
actions, and emits auditable runtime certificates. The evaluation framework,CPSBench,
maintains separate truth and observed trajectories so that hidden safety failure becomes
measurable rather than implicit.
In addition to the original core chapters, this expanded manuscript draft now includes a
battery-hardening block covering controlled fault-performance reporting, forty-eight-hour
operational fault traces, runtime latency and systems footprint, hyperparameter-surface
stability, subgroup calibration stress, regional per-balancing-authority interpretation, proxy
battery-aging economics, and a full hardware-in-the-loop validation architecture. These
chapters are intended to close the strongest remaining battery-domain gaps identified by the
broader thesis and GridPulse revision materials.
The result is a much stronger book-style battery manuscript base. It is still best understood
as an evidence-grounded advanced draft rather than a fully closed final manuscript, because
some of the extended hardening chapters define execution-ready evidence contracts that still
need final locked experimental runs before submission.
i

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 3 ===
Acknowledgments
This expanded draft exists as part of a larger research effort centered on certificate-aware
safety in physical AI systems. The battery-domain work was chosen as the first reference
domain because it provides a concrete, interpretable, and reproducible surface on which the
hidden gap between observed-state safety and true-state safety can be measured clearly.
The present manuscript is organized as a battery-first monograph with a deliberate hardening
path. Beyond the original conceptual, empirical, and theorem chapters, the current version
now includes dedicated chapters on fault-performance decomposition, latency, sensitivity
analysis, subgroup calibration, regional decomposition, lifecycle interpretation, and hardware-
in-the-loop validation design. Those additions are intended to make the thesis read more like
a final-year long-form manuscript and less like a scaffold.
The broader ORIUS research vision still extends beyond batteries into benchmark design,
temporal certificate validity, graceful degradation, composition across interacting agents, and
runtime deployment support. The battery-first structure of this file is meant to make those
later directions appendable without rewriting the core battery argument from scratch.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 35 ===
Part I
Why ORIUS Exists
1

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 36 ===
Chapter 1
Introduction
1.1 The hidden assumption in physical AI
Physical machine-learning systems do not act on the world directly. They act on what they
can observe about the world. That fact is obvious at the engineering level, but it is often
underappreciated at the safety level. In many cyber-physical pipelines, the controller is
designed as though the observation channel were a transparent and sufficiently trustworthy
window into the plant. Forecasts are generated, actions are optimized, feasibility is checked,
and safety is reported in the same representational space the controller observes. The hidden
assumption is that the observed state is trustworthy enough that “safe according to the
controller” remains equivalent to “safe in the physical world.”
This manuscript begins from the claim that this assumption is too strong. The battery
domain serves as the first complete validation surface because it provides an interpretable
plant, explicit operational constraints, and telemetry pathways that can be degraded in
realistic ways. The resulting evidence supports a broader scientific idea: physical AI safety
cannot be treated as independent of observation reliability.
This hidden assumption appears across many physical AI settings. In grid-scale battery
control, dispatch actions are chosen from telemetry streams representing state of charge,
power, forecasted demand, and renewable behavior. In autonomous vehicles, motion decisions
are formed from cameras, lidar, radar, GPS, and fused perception outputs. In industrial
automation, process controllers act on sensor values that are rarely perfect in deployment.
In each case, the controller’s decision quality depends not only on the quality of its policy,
optimizer, or predictor, but also on the trustworthiness of the information surface on which
2

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 37 ===
1.2. Why observed safety can differ from physical safety 3
those mechanisms operate.
The significance of this assumption becomes especially clear in the battery domain. A battery
controller may receive telemetry that appears plausible while already drifting away from the
battery’s true physical state. If the controller checks feasibility against the degraded observed
state, then it may approve an action that is internally feasible but physically unsafe. The
central motivation ofORIUS is that the observation channel is not a secondary monitoring
concern. It is part of the safety problem itself.
1.2 Why observed safety can differ from physical safety
Controllers ordinarily judge safety using the state representation currently available to them.
This is operationally natural. The true physical state is not directly accessible in real time.
The controller has measurements, estimates, filters, and telemetry contracts. The difficulty is
that physical constraints apply in the world, not in the controller’s internal representation.
Once the observation channel is degraded, those two levels can separate.
In the battery setting, the controller can appear observationally safe while being physically
unsafe. A charge or discharge action may look feasible under the observed state of charge
even though the true battery state is already too close to a constraint boundary. This changes
the meaning of safety evaluation. A system can satisfy all of its own observed-state feasibility
checks and still fail physically.
This distinction matters because it is not merely semantic. It determines whether the
controller’s internal feasibility test can be trusted. Standard robust and stochastic methods
often hedge uncertainty in future exogenous variables. They do not necessarily correct for
uncertainty in the current state measurement itself.ORIUS takes that missing layer seriously.
The battery-first manuscript is built around the idea that safety under degraded observation
requires the controller’s notion of feasibility to be repaired before unsafe actions reach the
plant.
1.3 The Observation-Action Safety Gap
This manuscript names the divergence between observed-state safety and true-state safety
the Observation-Action Safety Gap, or OASG. OASG exists when a controller evaluates
an action as safe using the observed state while that same action is unsafe with respect

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 38 ===
1.4. Why the battery domain exposed the problem first 4
to the true physical state on which real constraints apply. The concept is simple, but its
implications are broad. It means that a controller’s own safety logic can become misaligned
with the world it is supposed to protect.
At the level of universal structure, the true physical world is filtered through an observation
channel that can degrade under dropout, delay, spikes, staleness, drift, and packet reordering.
The controller sees only the observed state and optimizes against it. The resulting action is
then applied to the true physical world, where the actual consequence occurs.OASG is the
gap between the controller’s safety judgment and the plant’s physical safety reality.
This definition changes both diagnosis and solution. If the problem were merely poor
forecasting, then better prediction might suffice. If it were merely actuator disturbance, then
downstream robustification might suffice.OASG is different. It is a structural error at the
observation–decision boundary. It emerges because the controller’s action is evaluated in one
state space and executed in another.
1.4 Why the battery domain exposed the problem first
The battery domain exposed theOASG problem first because it combines hard physical
constraints, explicit control loops, and telemetry pathways that are easy to reason about and
degrade in realistic ways. Grid-scale battery energy storage systems operate under state-
of-charge, power, and ramp constraints that are physically meaningful and mathematically
explicit. A dispatch controller must repeatedly decide how much to charge or discharge based
on forecasts of load, generation, and price together with measurements of battery state and
operating conditions.
The control loop can be summarized as:
Forecast→Optimize→Dispatch→Measure→Monitor.
The hidden risk lies at the optimize-to-dispatch boundary. The optimizer computes an action
that is optimal relative to forecasts and observed telemetry, but what reaches the physical
battery may be optimal for the wrong state if the telemetry feeding the optimizer has drifted
from reality. Strong forecasting accuracy does not solve this. A forecast model may be
statistically strong and still contribute to unsafe action if the telemetry assumptions under
which uncertainty was calibrated no longer hold at runtime.
Battery also exposed the problem first because the corrective mechanism can be made explicit.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 39 ===
1.5. The ORIUS principle 5
A runtime system can compute a per-step reliability score, widen uncertainty when reliability
degrades, project unsafe candidate actions onto a tightened feasible set, and emit an auditable
runtime certificate. These design moves are concrete in the battery domain in a way that
is harder to achieve immediately in more complex domains such as surgery or autonomous
driving.
1.5 The ORIUS principle
The central principle of this work is concise:
Physical AI safety is bounded by observation reliability.
This sentence functions as the intellectual center of theORIUS program. It means that the
achievable safety of a physical intelligent system depends not only on the policy, optimizer, or
forecast model, but also on the trustworthiness of the observation channel available at decision
time. When observation quality is high, the controller can act with little conservatism. When
observation quality degrades, the uncertainty relevant to safe action must widen accordingly.
ORIUS operationalizes this principle by making observation quality a first-class control
input. In the battery domain, a quality engine computes a runtime score, a reliability-aware
uncertainty layer widens the state or forecast set when needed, and a safe action filter projects
candidate actions back into a feasible set defined over that widened uncertainty. The point is
not blanket conservatism. The point is conditional conservatism: when telemetry is clean,
the controller should impose little or no extra cost; when telemetry weakens, the protection
level should increase continuously and proportionally.
1.6 Research questions
This manuscript is guided by four battery-first research questions.
RQ1: How often do observed-state and true-state safety diverge under realistic telemetry
faults in battery dispatch?
RQ2: Can a runtime shield based on telemetry reliability scoring, uncertainty inflation,
and safe action repair close that gap without blanket conservatism?

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 40 ===
1.7. Contributions 6
RQ3: Which components of the battery shield are actually necessary for zero-violation
safety?
RQ4: How should the battery-domain result be lifted into a broaderORIUS framework
without overstating the current evidence?
These questions are intentionally ordered from diagnosis to intervention and then from battery
proof surface to framework generalization.
1.7 Contributions
This battery-first manuscript makes six central contributions.
First, it formulates the hidden gap between observed-state safety and true-state safety as a
first-class systems problem.
Second, it uses the battery domain as the first complete validation surface for that problem.
Third, it positionsDC3S as a runtime control-boundary architecture that converts telemetry
reliability into solver-usable uncertainty and safe action repair.
Fourth, it treats dual-trajectory evaluation as a necessary condition for measuring hidden
safety failures.
Fifth, it organizes the battery result as the empirical foundation of a largerORIUS framework.
Sixth, it provides a manuscript architecture that allows future work on certificate half-life,
graceful degradation, benchmark design, composition, and CertOS to be appended cleanly.
1.8 Contribution boundary
A strong manuscript must state not only what it contributes, but also what it does not yet
prove.
This battery-first manuscript does not claim full cross-domain empirical validation, field
deployment, or complete infrastructure maturity. It does not claim that the entire six-paper
ORIUS program has already been fully realized in hardware and multiple operational domains.
What it does claim is narrower and, for that reason, stronger: the battery domain reveals
a hidden observed-state safety illusion, the illusion can be measured, and a quality-aware
runtime shield can be designed to close it.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 41 ===
1.9. Organization of the manuscript 7
This boundary is a strength, not a weakness. It keeps the argument honest and reviewer-safe
while preserving a clean path to future domains.
1.9 Organization of the manuscript
The remainder of the manuscript proceeds from problem diagnosis to domain instantiation,
then from empirical evidence to theorem grounding, and finally from the battery foundation
toward the broaderORIUS program.
Part I establishes the problem and its conceptual foundation. Part II develops the battery-
domain control surface. Part III presents the empirical battery proof package. Part IV
organizes the battery-theorem validation layer. Part V lifts the battery result toward the
broader ORIUS agenda. The appendices collect notation, assumptions, proofs, artifact
manifests, and cross-domain templates.
Taken together, the manuscript is meant to do something specific. It is not a completed
universal systems book pretending all domains are already solved. Nor is it only a narrow
battery paper with no larger scientific ambition. It is a battery-first long-form foundation for
a broader theory of physical AI safety under degraded observation.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 42 ===
Chapter 2
Background and Related Work
2.1 Chapter purpose
This chapter situates the battery-domainORIUS manuscript within the broader literature
on control under uncertainty, uncertainty quantification, runtime safety, cyber-physical
monitoring, battery degradation, and benchmark design. The goal is not merely to survey
prior work, but to draw a careful boundary around what has already been established, what
remains unresolved, and where the present battery-first manuscript enters the literature. A
mature related-work chapter should answer three questions clearly: what foundations are
already strong, what gaps remain, and which of those gaps are directly closed by the current
thesis.
The central answer developed here is that modern work in battery dispatch, model predictive
control, conformal prediction, runtime assurance, and safety filters provides many of the
mathematical and systems ingredients used byORIUS, but does not directly resolve the
hidden case in which a controller appears safe on observed state while becoming unsafe in true
physical state under degraded telemetry. That unresolved gap motivates both the battery-
domain DC3S architecture and the battery-track CPSBench evaluation methodology
(Mayne et al., 2000; Rawlings and Mayne, 2009; Mesbah, 2016; Romano et al., 2019; Gibbs
and Candès, 2021; Sha, 2001; Leucker and Schallhart, 2009; Ames et al., 2019; Rosewater et
al., 2020; Niroula, 2026).
8

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 43 ===
2.2. Scope discipline for the literature review 9
2.2 Scope discipline for the literature review
The literature base relevant toORIUS is far larger than what a battery-first manuscript
should foreground in its main argument. For that reason, this chapter follows a deliberate
scope policy. The main body emphasizes literature that is directly load-bearing for the
battery-domain claims: battery dispatch, robust and stochastic control, conformal uncertainty
quantification, runtime assurance, control barrier functions, cyber-physical fault monitoring,
battery degradation, and battery benchmarking. Broader universal-autonomy references that
support later cross-domain ORIUS ambitions are retained, but are pushed into the extended
reading index and future-work discussion rather than being used to overstate the present
battery evidence base.
This decision is important for thesis integrity. The present manuscript aims to establish
a battery-domain proof surface that is strong enough to serve as the foundation for later
universalization, not to pretend that battery evidence alone already proves all cross-domain
claims. The thesis therefore treats universal ORIUS as a future-facing extension built on top
of a completed battery reference domain (Niroula, 2026a; Niroula, 2026b; Niroula, 2026c).
2.3 Battery dispatch and renewable-aware control
Battery energy storage systems are central to contemporary grid flexibility because they
shift energy in time, reduce peaks, absorb renewable variability, and provide reserve support.
At the operational level, battery dispatch is a sequential constrained control problem: at
each decision step, the controller chooses whether to charge, discharge, or hold, subject
to state-of-charge limits, power constraints, ramp limits, efficiency losses, and uncertainty
in load, renewable generation, and price (Mayne et al., 2000; Rawlings and Mayne, 2009;
Maciejowski, 2002; Mesbah, 2016; Kouvaritakis and Cannon, 2016; Rockafellar and Uryasev,
2000).
2.3.1 Deterministic dispatch
Deterministic dispatch assumes that point forecasts of load, renewable generation, and price
are sufficiently informative to drive a single optimization over the battery horizon. Its
practical appeal is simplicity, interpretability, and low computational cost. Many industrial
systems still rely on deterministic or lightly regularized deterministic dispatch because it is

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 44 ===
2.3. Battery dispatch and renewable-aware control 10
operationally transparent and easy to integrate with market logic (Rosewater et al., 2020;
Baldick et al., 2020; Watson et al., 2021).
The main limitation, from the perspective of this manuscript, is not that deterministic
dispatch ignores all uncertainty. Rather, it assumes that the state representation on which
feasibility is evaluated is reliable enough that standard constraints continue to mean what
they appear to mean. Under degraded telemetry, that assumption breaks. A deterministic
controller may remain internally coherent while being physically unsafe in the true battery
state. This is the exact hidden failure later quantified in the batteryORIUS experiments
(Niroula, 2026; GridPulse revision notes, 2026).
2.3.2 Robust and tube-based dispatch
Robust optimization and tube-based model predictive control extend deterministic dispatch
by hedging against future uncertainty. Tube MPC, min-max MPC, and scenario-based
robust methods all provide formal or semi-formal ways to preserve feasibility under bounded
or sampled disturbances (Mayne et al., 2005; Langson et al., 2004; Rawlings and Mayne,
2009; Kouvaritakis and Cannon, 2016; Mesbah, 2016; Camacho and Bordons, 2013). These
frameworks are foundational forORIUS because they supply the language of tightened
feasible sets, invariant tubes, and control under bounded uncertainty.
However, the battery literature usually applies these ideas to uncertainty in future exogenous
variables rather than uncertainty in the present observation channel. A controller can therefore
be robust over the wrong set if the current battery state estimate is already corrupted. This
is precisely where the battery-domainORIUS formulation diverges from standard robust
dispatch: it treats uncertainty about the present measurement surface itself as a first-class
control concern (Niroula, 2026; Niroula, 2026a).
2.3.3 Stochastic and risk-aware dispatch
Stochastic and risk-aware dispatch methods model future uncertainty through scenarios,
distributions, or tail-risk objectives. Expected-value planning, chance constraints, and CVaR-
style formulations make it possible to balance nominal value against rare high-cost outcomes
(Rockafellar and Uryasev, 2000; Mesbah, 2016; Shapiro et al., 2009; Rosewater et al., 2020;
Olson and Baldick, 2021). This line of work is deeply relevant because the battery thesis also
studies cost, carbon, peak shaving, and stochastic value of information.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 45 ===
2.4. The measurement blind spot in battery dispatch 11
But these methods still leave a critical gap. They can protect against future uncertainty
while assuming that the observation channel defining the current state remains trustworthy.
In the present thesis, that is the wrong assumption under degraded telemetry. Stochastic
dispatch is therefore necessary background, but not yet the solution to the observation-action
safety gap.
2.3.4 Battery operations, markets, and renewable coordination
The battery literature is also rich in practical operations work covering energy arbitrage,
renewable smoothing, frequency services, and degradation-aware scheduling (Lund et al.,
2015; Denholm et al., 2010; He et al., 2018; Parvania and Fotuhi-Firuzabad, 2014; Zhang et
al., 2018; Xu, 2018). These works matter because they show that battery dispatch is not a toy
safety problem. It is a real cyber-physical optimization problem in which sensing, forecasting,
degradation, and economics are coupled. That makes it a strong first domain for ORIUS: the
control problem is rich enough to matter, but structured enough to analyze rigorously.
2.4 The measurement blind spot in battery dispatch
One of the most important claims of this manuscript is methodological rather than purely
algorithmic. A recurring feature of prior battery-dispatch evaluation is that safety, feasibility,
and controller quality are often measured on the same state representation used by the
controller itself. Under clean-measurement assumptions, this is internally coherent. Under
degraded sensing, it becomes a structural blind spot. If the benchmark never maintains a
separate truth trajectory, it cannot detect whether the controller is physically unsafe while
appearing safe in observed state (Niroula, 2026; Niroula, 2026a; CPSBench-X draft, 2026).
2.4.1 Why the blind spot persisted
The blind spot persisted because most dispatch papers were not designed to study degraded
telemetry as a first-class scientific variable. Instead, they often treated sensing errors as
small noise, nuisance engineering, or preprocessing concerns. If the same simulator both
feeds the controller and judges its safety, the benchmark inherits the controller’s view of the
world. This makes observation-conditioned failure invisible by construction (Niroula, 2026;
CPSBench-X draft, 2026).

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 46 ===
2.5. Telemetry faults in cyber-physical systems 12
2.4.2 Why the blind spot matters beyond battery
Although the current manuscript remains battery-scoped, the underlying blind spot is not
battery-specific. Any cyber-physical controller acting through delayed, stale, reordered, or
spoofed measurements can inherit the same evaluation error if truth and observation are
not separated. The battery contribution is therefore not merely a domain case study; it is a
particularly clean witness of a more general problem, though the stronger universal claim is
reserved for future work (Niroula, 2026b; Niroula, 2026c).
2.5 Telemetry faults in cyber-physical systems
The thesis does not invent degraded telemetry as a concept. Missing data, latency, packet
disorder, drift, stale values, and spikes are well-known failure modes in cyber-physical systems,
industrial networks, and smart-grid telemetry (Sinopoli et al., 2004; Hespanha et al., 2007;
Mo and Sinopoli, 2010; Pasqualetti et al., 2013; Teixeira et al., 2015; Urbina et al., 2016).
What is novel in the battery-firstORIUS setting is the integration of these fault types into
one explicit control and evaluation framework.
2.5.1 Battery interpretation of the fault taxonomy
In the battery setting, dropout appears as missing BMS or SCADA updates, delay-jitter as
stale network measurements, spike faults as transient outliers, reorder as packet disorder,
and drift as slowly accumulating bias in state or exogenous channels. The battery paper and
the master ORIUS planning documents make these mappings explicit and use them as the
foundation for both benchmark design and runtime quality scoring (Niroula, 2026; Niroula,
2026b; GridPulse revision notes, 2026).
2.5.2 Fault monitoring in industrial and power systems
A large literature studies anomaly detection, fault diagnosis, and resilience in industrial
and power systems, including observer-based detection, residual monitoring, hybrid system
diagnosis, false-data injection detection, and smart-grid cyber-resilience (Blanke et al., 2006;
Chen and Patton, 1999; Pasqualetti et al., 2013; Teixeira et al., 2015; Mo and Sinopoli, 2010;
Smith, 2015). These works demonstrate that monitoring faults is feasible and often necessary.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 47 ===
2.6. Uncertainty quantification and conformal methods 13
What they typically do not provide is the battery-domain bridge from degraded telemetry to
adaptive safe-action restriction inside the same online control loop.
2.6 Uncertainty quantification and conformal methods
A second major pillar of related work comes from uncertainty quantification, especially
conformal prediction. Conformal methods are attractive because they provide finite-sample
predictive sets under weak assumptions. In battery control, that matters because interval
quality—not just point-forecast quality—changes which actions are admissible (Vovk et al.,
2005; Shafer and Vovk, 2008; Lei et al., 2018; Romano et al., 2019; Gibbs and Candès, 2021;
Zaffran et al., 2022; Fontana et al., 2023).
2.6.1 Conformalized quantile regression
Conformalized quantile regression combines learned lower and upper quantiles with conformal
calibration residuals, producing prediction intervals that are often sharper than generic sym-
metric conformal sets while retaining finite-sample coverage under exchangeability (Romano
et al., 2019; Sesia and Candès, 2020; Feldman et al., 2021). In the present manuscript, such
intervals are not merely reporting devices. They are control-relevant objects whose width
changes dispatch feasibility.
2.6.2 Adaptive conformal inference and time series
Adaptive conformal inference addresses distribution shift and online recalibration. For time-
series settings, this is essential because clean calibration assumptions weaken under temporal
dependence and changing regimes (Gibbs and Candès, 2021; Zaffran et al., 2022; Bostr"om
and Johansson, 2020; Fontana et al., 2023). The ORIUS move is to extend this logic by
making observation quality itself a runtime driver of uncertainty inflation rather than relying
only on residual-based adaptation.
2.6.3 Marginal versus conditional coverage
The most important limitation of standard conformal methods in this setting is that their
guarantee is usually marginal rather than conditional. Global coverage can look acceptable

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 48 ===
2.7. Runtime safety, shielding, and assurance 14
while coverage deteriorates exactly in the degraded-sensing regimes where safety is most
at risk. This issue is now widely recognized in the broader conformal literature, including
work on conditional, groupwise, and fairness-conditioned coverage (Romano et al., 2020;
Feldman et al., 2021; Wang et al., 2023; Zhou et al., 2024; Bostr"om and Johansson, 2020).
In the battery thesis, this limitation motivates reliability-conditioned diagnostics and later
extensions to subgroup calibration rather than allowing global PICP alone to stand in for
safety-ready uncertainty.
2.7 Runtime safety, shielding, and assurance
Runtime safety methods intervene at execution time when nominal controllers become unsafe
or untrustworthy. This broad family includes action shielding, runtime verification, Simplex-
style architectures, and certified fallback switching (Sha, 2001; Alshiekh et al., 2018; Leucker
and Schallhart, 2009; Schierman et al., 2015; Srivastava et al., 2017; Pajic et al., 2014). These
works are highly relevant becauseDC3S also sits at execution time.
2.7.1 Why prior runtime assurance is not enough
The problem for degraded sensing is that many runtime safety methods still evaluate safety
using the same state surface the controller sees. If that surface is already corrupted, then the
monitor, shield, or fallback trigger may inherit the same blind spot as the controller. The
battery-domain DC3S contribution therefore lies not just in restricting actions, but in first
redefining the uncertainty set using telemetry quality before the safe-action filter is applied
(Niroula, 2026; GridPulse revision notes, 2026).
2.7.2 How DC3S extends this literature
DC3S occupies a gap between conformal monitoring, runtime shielding, and fallback architec-
tures. It scores telemetry quality, inflates uncertainty in proportion to degraded trust, solves
dispatch against the widened set, projects unsafe candidates into the tightened feasible set,
and records a step-level certificate. This differs from classical monitors that merely observe,
and from classical safe-mode controllers that do not adapt their conservatism continuously to
observation quality (Niroula, 2026; Niroula, 2026a).

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 49 ===
2.8. Control barrier functions and state-dependent safety filters 15
2.8 Controlbarrierfunctionsandstate-dependentsafety
filters
Control barrier functions provide one of the strongest formalisms for enforcing forward
invariance of safe sets (Ames et al., 2017; Ames et al., 2019; Nguyen and Sreenath, 2016;
Coogan, 2020). If the barrier condition is evaluated on the correct state, strong safety
guarantees follow. This makes CBFs a natural comparison class forORIUS.
The ORIUS critique is not that CBFs are unimportant. It is that they require meaningful
access to the state on which the barrier condition is enforced. If the observed state drifts
from the true physical state, then a barrier certificate can appear satisfied in the wrong state
space. Robust and stochastic CBF variants mitigate bounded uncertainty (Xu et al., 2015;
Clark, 2021), but the present battery thesis focuses on a more basic structural issue: under
degraded telemetry, the controller may not know which state the barrier should be evaluated
on.
2.9 Information-theoretic and networked-control roots
The deepest theoretical roots ofORIUS lie in information and networked control. A controller
acts through a measurement channel; if that channel degrades, the controller’s knowledge
of the plant degrades as well. Classical work on partially observable decision processes,
networked control, packet-drop systems, and communication constraints already makes clear
that information quality limits achievable control quality (Kaelbling et al., 1998; Pineau et
al., 2003; Sinopoli et al., 2004; Hespanha et al., 2007; Cover and Thomas, 2006; Shannon,
1948).
This perspective matters because it shows thatORIUS is not merely a battery engineering
patch. It is a cyber-physical control thesis built on the insight that safe action capacity shrinks
when the information surface weakens. In the present battery manuscript, that principle is
instantiated through the observation quality score, reliability-conditioned interval inflation,
and safe-action filtering. The stronger universal claim that this becomes a domain-agnostic
law is treated as a future extension rather than a current empirical conclusion.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 50 ===
2.10. Battery degradation, aging, and asset health 16
2.10 Battery degradation, aging, and asset health
Battery safety is inseparable from degradation and asset health. A large literature studies
capacity fade, resistance growth, depth-of-discharge stress, lithium plating, thermal safety,
and degradation-aware control (Wang et al., 2024; Kucinskis et al., 2022; Marchegiani et al.,
2023; Liu et al., 2023; Silva et al., 2025; Zhang et al., 2018; Bloom et al., 2001). These works
matter for ORIUS because the battery thesis does not only reduce immediate violation risk;
it also motivates the claim that preventing true-state violations prevents avoidable stress on
expensive electrochemical assets.
At the same time, the current manuscript remains careful. The strongest evidence currently
available is a proxy asset-preservation model rather than a full electrochemical lifetime-
validation study. The literature on battery aging is therefore used to justify the physics
and economics of damage-aware interpretation, while the stronger electrochemical validation
claim is deferred to future experimental work.
2.11 Cyber-physicalsecurityandadversarialrobustness
Security becomes essential once the manuscript moves beyond noisy faults to adversarial
telemetry. The cyber-physical security literature on replay attacks, false-data injection
attacks, watermarking, and integrity monitoring is the natural foundation for the active
probing extension in the battery thesis (Mo and Sinopoli, 2010; Pasqualetti et al., 2013;
Teixeira et al., 2015; Urbina et al., 2016; Smith, 2015; Sinha et al., 2023).
These works show that passive anomaly detection can fail against carefully crafted attacks.
They also show that active challenge-response and watermarking ideas can expose hidden
manipulation by forcing the adversary to predict or reproduce a physical signature. This
literaturealignscloselywiththebattery-onlyadversarialchapteraddedlaterinthemanuscript,
where active probing is framed as a battery-domain physical challenge-response mechanism
rather than a purely statistical detector.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 51 ===
2.12. Evaluation protocols, benchmarking, and reproducibility 17
2.12 Evaluation protocols, benchmarking, and repro-
ducibility
A final literature strand concerns evaluation itself. Reproducibility, locked artifacts, bench-
mark traceability, and controller comparison protocols are essential if hidden safety failures
are to be made measurable and scientifically durable (Pineau et al., 2021; Pineau et al., 2020;
Sculley et al., 2015; Niroula, 2026a; CPSBench-X draft, 2026). The battery thesis responds
by enforcing dual-trajectory evaluation and artifact traceability. This is an important contri-
bution because it turns the hidden-gap claim from a simulation anecdote into a reproducible
benchmark object.
The broader universal benchmark vision belongs to later ORIUS work. In the present battery
manuscript, the defensible claim is narrower and stronger: the battery track provides a
rigorous first reference benchmark for observation-conditioned battery safety under degraded
telemetry.
2.13 What is integrated now versus deferred to future
work
The related-work review supports a strong battery-first claim. The literature already provides
foundations for constrained battery dispatch, uncertainty quantification, runtime assurance,
battery aging, fault monitoring, and cyber-physical attack detection. The present thesis
integrates those strands around one specific hidden failure mode: the gap between observed-
state feasibility and true-state physical safety under degraded telemetry. That integration is
the current contribution.
Broader universal references—autonomous driving, aerospace, surgery, and fully general
multi-agent certificate operating systems—are important for the future ORIUS program, but
they are not load-bearing for the battery evidence claim. To avoid overreach, those references
are cataloged in the extended reading index and deferred to the roadmap chapter rather than
being used to inflate the present battery contribution.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 52 ===
2.14. The novelty gap 18
2.14 The novelty gap
Taken together, the literature reveals a consistent blind spot rather than one missing algorithm.
Prior battery-dispatch methods are strong at modeling future uncertainty but usually assume
the measurement channel is trustworthy at decision time. Conformal prediction is strong
at finite-sample marginal uncertainty quantification but does not by itself guarantee safe
control under degraded observation. Runtime safety methods can restrict unsafe actions or
switch to fallback behavior, but they often evaluate safety using the same possibly corrupted
observation surface. Control barrier functions provide strong invariance guarantees, but only
when the state on which they are evaluated is the correct one.
The distinct contribution ofORIUS in the present manuscript is therefore not that each
individual ingredient is unprecedented. It is that they are integrated around one specific
hidden failure mode: the gap between observed-state feasibility and true-state physical
safety under degraded telemetry. The battery-domain thesis closes that gap empirically and
architecturally, while laying a disciplined foundation for later universalization (Niroula, 2026;
Niroula, 2026a; Niroula, 2026b).
2.15 Chapter summary
This chapter reviewed the literature most relevant to the battery-firstORIUS manuscript.
It showed that prior work in battery dispatch, robust and stochastic control, conformal
prediction, runtime assurance, control barrier functions, cyber-physical security, battery
aging, and reproducibility provides many of the pieces needed to reason about safety under
uncertainty. At the same time, it showed that these pieces do not by themselves resolve the
case in which the controller’s observation surface degrades at the moment of action, creating
a divergence between observed feasibility and true-state safety.
The next chapter therefore formalizes the battery-domain ORIUS problem itself: the
GridPulse system context, the true-versus-observed state separation, the battery dynamics
and feasible action set, the calibration gap, and the control problem thatDC3S is designed
to solve.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 53 ===
Chapter 3
Problem Formulation
3.1 Chapter purpose
The previous chapter positioned the manuscript against the broader literature and identified
the central novelty gap. This chapter formalizes the battery-domain control problem to
which ORIUS responds. The goal is to define the control setting precisely enough for real
experiments and real proofs while keeping the structure reusable for later domains.
3.2 GridPulse system context
The battery-domain ORIUS problem arises inside GridPulse, a forecast-to-dispatch stack for
grid-scale battery energy storage. The system can be summarized as a closed decision loop:
Forecast→Optimize→Dispatch→Measure→Monitor.
This ordering matters because the primary safety failure does not arise uniformly across
the loop. Instead, it appears at the optimize-to-dispatch boundary, where the optimizer
computes an action using forecasts and observed telemetry, but the physical battery executes
that action in the true world rather than in the controller’s observed world.
3.2.1 Why the optimize-to-dispatch boundary matters
The optimizer can compute an action that is mathematically correct with respect to forecasts
and observed telemetry while still being physically unsafe with respect to the true battery
19

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 54 ===
3.3. The hidden safety gap 20
state. Strong forecasting performance alone does not remove that risk if the battery state
estimate or uncertainty representation is no longer trustworthy. In theORIUS framing,
GridPulse is therefore not merely an economic optimization pipeline, but a safety-critical
cyber-physical workflow whose observation channel directly constrains the admissible control
space.
3.3 The hidden safety gap
The core structural issue inORIUS is that the controller acts onobserved state, while the
physical system evolves in thetrue world. The universal ORIUS problem structure can be
summarized as:
true physical world→degraded observation channel→observed state→controller
action→true physical consequence
The safety gap appears because the controller optimizes against observed state while safety
must ultimately be judged on the true physical consequence.
3.3.1 Battery-domain statement
In the battery domain, the hidden failure appears through state-of-charge. A battery may
look feasible under observed SOC while violating the physical SOC envelope under the true
battery trajectory. This is the battery-domain form of theOASG.
3.4 State objects and telemetry model
Let xt∈Sdenote the true physical state at timet and letot∈Odenote the observed state
at timet. The clean telemetry vector iszt∈Rd and the degraded telemetry vector is˜zt∈Rd.
Telemetry degradation is modeled through an operatorD such that
˜zt =D(zt).
The battery-domain fault surface includes dropout, delay-jitter, spike, stale, drift, and reorder
events. These are not only benchmark settings; they alter the information geometry of the

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 55 ===
3.5. Fault model 21
control problem.
3.4.1 Observation quality
Let wt∈[0, 1] denote the observation quality score. This scalar summarizes how trustworthy
the current observation channel is. In the battery implementation it is computed from
missingness, delay, out-of-order events, spike penalties, and optionally drift evidence.
3.5 Fault model
The ORIUS fault taxonomy includes six universal classes:
1. dropout,
2. delay-jitter,
3. spike,
4. stale,
5. drift,
6. reorder.
In the battery setting, these correspond to missing sensors, SCADA lag, outlier injection,
frozen readings, renewable or sensor bias, and packet disorder. Some are abrupt. Others are
gradual and more dangerous because they remain numerically plausible while drifting away
from physical truth.
3.6 Battery-domain instantiation
At each time stept, the controller observes degraded telemetry˜zt and chooses a dispatch
action from an admissible action space.
For clarity, the battery-domain ORIUS system distinguishes three action objects:
• the optimizer’s candidate action,a⋆
t,

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 56 ===
3.7. Battery dynamics and feasible action set 22
• the shield’s safe repaired action,asaf e
t ,
• the executed action,at, which equalsasaf e
t in the protected system.
This distinction matters because the optimizer can be internally correct while still proposing
an action that becomes unsafe once degraded observation is accounted for.
3.7 Battery dynamics and feasible action set
The true state-of-charge evolution follows the standard battery update:
SOC true
t+1 =SOC true
t + ηc[at]+−[at]−/ηd
Emax
∆t,
where ηc and ηd are charge and discharge efficiencies andEmax is rated battery capacity.
The feasible action region is constrained by:
• power limits,
• ramp-rate limits,
• next-step SOC limits.
A useful abstract version of the safe action set is
Asafe(Ut) ={a :f(x,a )∈Ssafe for allx∈Ut},
where Ut is the reliability-aware uncertainty set andSsafe is the physical safety set.
3.8 Dispatch objective
The battery paper uses a pragmatic engineering objective that balances cost, carbon exposure,
peak-demand penalty, and throughput degradation. In this manuscript, the key point is not
perfect market realism. The key point is that the controller is still trying to do useful work
under safety constraints.ORIUS does not replace the dispatch objective with a shutdown-
only policy. It constrains economically meaningful actions so they remain physically safe
when observation reliability weakens.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 57 ===
3.9. The calibration gap under degradation 23
3.9 The calibration gap under degradation
Base conformal uncertainty intervals are calibrated under a certain data regime. Under
telemetry degradation, the conformity-score distribution shifts because the model is no longer
seeing the same input-quality regime that supported the original calibration. The result is a
calibration gap: an interval that looks acceptable in aggregate can become under-covered
exactly when the controller most needs trustworthy uncertainty.
This matters because dispatch methods downstream assume that the uncertainty set they
receive is solver-usable and safety-relevant. If the interval under-covers when telemetry
degrades, then even a robust or risk-aware controller may become robust over the wrong set.
3.10 Baseline policies
The battery-domain empirical surface uses several baseline policy families:
1. deterministic dispatch without runtime uncertainty adaptation,
2. grid-only operation without battery dispatch,
3. simple heuristic battery control,
4. the protected DC3S controller.
These baselines isolate different failure modes and help show thatDC3S is best understood
not as a different battery objective, but as a control-boundary safety layer wrapped around
dispatch.
3.11 Research questions formalized
The problem formulation gives rise to four concrete questions:
Q1: How large is the gap between observed-state and true-state safety under realistic
telemetry faults?
Q2: Can reliability-aware uncertainty and safe action repair close that gap?
Q3: Which components of the runtime shield are load-bearing?

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 58 ===
3.12. Scope boundary 24
Q4: How should the battery result be lifted into a broader ORIUS framework without
overstating the evidence?
3.12 Scope boundary
The battery-domain problem formulation must also state what it isnot trying to do yet.
This manuscript is the first complete battery-domain validation surface, not the completed
universal ORIUS program. Hardware-in-the-loop validation, full certificate-lifecycle theory,
multi-agent composition, and five-domain empirical validation belong to later stages of the
research roadmap.
3.13 Dataset scope
The battery-domain problem is posed over a locked, reproducible data surface. The core
evaluation uses German and US grid data with load, renewable, and operational signals
suitable for forecast-to-dispatch experiments. This matters because the problem formulation
is not floating in abstraction. Every chapter that follows is tied to a specific, reproducible
empirical substrate.
3.14 Chapter summary
This chapter formalized the battery-domainORIUS problem. It placed the problem inside
the GridPulse closed loop, identified the optimize-to-dispatch boundary as the key safety
location, separated true state from observed state, defined degraded telemetry and observation
quality, instantiated the ORIUS fault taxonomy in the battery domain, and described the
dispatch sequence in which candidate actions can become unsafe under degraded observation.
The chapter also specified the physical battery dynamics, the feasible action region, the
comparative dispatch objective, the calibration gap under degradation, the baseline policy
family, the current scope boundary, and the dataset surface on which the battery problem is
actually studied. The next chapter develops the key empirical and conceptual consequence of
this formulation: the observed-state safety illusion.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 59 ===
Chapter 4
The Observed-State Safety Illusion
4.1 Chapter purpose
The previous chapter formally defined the battery-domain control problem under degraded ob-
servation. This chapter develops the central hidden failure that emerges from that formulation:
a controller can appear safe while being physically unsafe.
This chapter calls that failure theobserved-state safety illusion. The term is deliberate. The
controller’s safety logic is not necessarily broken in an internal sense. It may be fully consistent
with the information surface it sees. The illusion appears because the state representation on
which the controller evaluates safety is not always the same as the state that governs physical
consequence.
4.2 From partial observability to safety illusion
Every physical controller acts through a measurement channel. In clean conditions this is
ordinary and manageable. If observation quality is high, then the observed state is a useful
proxy for the true state, and safety checks performed on that proxy are often good enough
for practical control. The difficulty begins when the observation channel degrades. At that
point, the controller still acts on observations, but those observations no longer map cleanly
to physical reality.
The battery domain exposes this clearly because state of charge is bounded, interpretable, and
directly tied to physical feasibility. If a controller overestimates how much energy remains,
it may discharge too deeply. If it underestimates how close it is to the upper boundary, it
25

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 60 ===
4.3. Why the illusion arises in the GridPulse loop 26
may charge too aggressively. These are not soft statistical errors. They are concrete physical
violations.
Figure 4.1: Battery-domain illustration of the observed-state safety illusion. The controller
evaluates safety on observed state, while the physical consequence is determined by the true
battery state.
4.3 Why the illusion arises in the GridPulse loop
The GridPulse architecture is a closed decision loop:
Forecast→Optimize→Dispatch→Measure→Monitor.
The illusion does not live everywhere in this loop. It lives where candidate actions stop being
hypothetical and start affecting the physical battery.
4.3.1 The optimize-to-dispatch boundary
The optimizer computes an action that is correct relative to the state and uncertainty
representation it has been given. But the battery receives that action in the true physical
world, not in the controller’s observed world. If telemetry degradation has already created a
meaningful gap between observed and true state, then the optimizer can produce an action
that is internally feasible but physically unsafe.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 61 ===
4.4. Formal statement of the illusion 27
4.3.2 Why good forecasting does not solve it
This point is easy to misunderstand. The problem is not simply that forecasts can be wrong.
Even strong point-forecast accuracy does not remove the risk if the telemetry assumptions
under which uncertainty was calibrated have already broken down at runtime. The controller
may know the future well enough and still misunderstand the present badly enough to make
unsafe decisions.
4.4 Formal statement of the illusion
The cleanest battery-domain formalization is to distinguish two safety predicates for a
candidate actiona⋆
t:
• observed-state safety, which evaluates whether the candidate appears feasible and safe
in the controller’s observed state representation,
• true-state safety, which evaluates whether the same candidate is actually safe in the
physical battery state.
The observed-state safety illusion occurs when a candidate action is safe under observed-state
evaluation but unsafe under true-state evaluation. In battery terms, it means the controller
sees enough feasible SOC margin to approve an action even though the physical battery does
not actually have that margin.
A useful indicator form is
OASGt = 1 [Safeobs(a⋆
t ) = 1 ∧Safetrue(a⋆
t ) = 0].
4.5 Battery-domain realization under telemetry faults
The observed-state safety illusion does not appear because the battery controller is irrational.
It appears because telemetry degradation increases the distance between observed state and
true physical state.
In the battery setting, the relevant fault surface includes dropout, delay-jitter, spikes, stale
readings, drift, and reorder events. Some faults are abrupt, such as hard dropout or extreme

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 62 ===
4.6. Why standard evaluation misses the problem 28
spikes. Others are gradual and more dangerous because they remain numerically plausible
while drifting away from reality.
4.5.1 A simple battery narrative
Suppose the observed SOC remains frozen at a value that appears comfortably inside the
safe interval. The optimizer therefore sees room to discharge further. In the physical battery,
however, SOC has already moved closer to the lower boundary. The proposed discharge is
feasible in the observed representation and infeasible in the true plant. The optimizer is not
“wrong” according to the data it sees. The data is the problem.
4.6 Why standard evaluation misses the problem
A major reason this hidden failure was not discovered earlier is that ordinary BESS evaluation
protocols do not maintain a separate truth trajectory. If the simulator only exposes the
controller’s observed state and then also uses that same state to judge safety, then it cannot
detect the illusion by construction. The controller and the benchmark share the same mistaken
representation.
This is why the battery-first evaluation philosophy behindCPSBench matters so much.
Hidden failure becomes measurable only when the evaluator is allowed to see what the
controller may not.
4.7 CPSBench and dual-trajectory evaluation
CPSBench exists to make the illusion measurable and reproducible. Its core principle is
simple:
The controller should act on observed state, but the evaluator should judge safety
on true state.
In the battery track, this is implemented by running a physical truth trajectory in parallel
with the controller-facing observed trajectory. The controller never receives the true state
directly. The evaluator measures whether the resulting action is safe on the true battery
trajectory.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 63 ===
4.8. Why the illusion demands a runtime shield 29
Definition 4.1 (Dual-trajectory evaluation). Dual-trajectory evaluation is a benchmark
protocol in which the controller receives only degraded observed telemetry, while a separate
physical simulator evolves the true system state under the same executed actions, and safety
is measured on the true state rather than the observed one.
4.8 Why the illusion demands a runtime shield
Once the observed-state safety illusion is visible, the design requirement becomes clear. A safe
battery controller cannot trust observed-state feasibility alone. It needs a runtime mechanism
that accounts for how much the observation channel should be trusted at each step.
This is the design role ofDC3S. Rather than relegating telemetry reliability to a monitoring
dashboard, the controller uses reliability scores to widen uncertainty, repair unsafe candidate
actions, and emit a step-level audit record. The guiding idea is conditional conservatism:
permissive when the observation channel is trustworthy, protective when it weakens.
4.9 Chapter summary
This chapter established the first empirical and conceptual foundation ofORIUS: a battery
dispatch controller can appear safe while being physically unsafe. The hidden failure arises
because the controller evaluates actions on degraded observed state while safety must be
judged on the true physical battery state. Under realistic telemetry faults, those two surfaces
can diverge enough to create a measurable safety gap.
The chapter also showed why prior evaluation protocols failed to expose the problem and why
dual-trajectory evaluation is necessary. Finally, it clarified why the hidden failure cannot be
solved by cleaner prose or better dashboards alone. Once the controller’s safety judgment is
shown to be unreliable under degraded observation, a runtime control layer becomes necessary.
The next chapters develop that battery-domain runtime system in detail.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 64 ===
Part II
Battery Domain: The First Full
Validation
30

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 65 ===
Chapter 5
GridPulse System Context
5.1 Chapter purpose
This chapter grounds the battery-domainORIUS thesis in the actual GridPulse system
rather than in an abstract control sketch. The purpose is threefold. First, it explains why
grid-scale batteries are a scientifically strong first domain for a manuscript on safety under
degraded observation. Second, it specifies GridPulse as a closed decision architecture in
which observation, forecasting, optimization, dispatch, and monitoring interact continuously.
Third, it identifies the exact architectural location at which the hidden safety failure becomes
physically consequential: theOptimize–Dispatchboundary.
A final-year research manuscript should not treat the battery system as a toy plant. It should
show that the domain is operationally serious, structurally rich, and still mathematically
tractable. Grid-scale batteries satisfy all three criteria. They expose hard physical constraints,
depend strongly on telemetry, admit interpretable safety variables, and yet are simple enough
that the relationship between sensed state, candidate action, and physical consequence can
be written explicitly. That combination is why the battery domain is the right place to build
the first complete ORIUS reference implementation.
31

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 66 ===
5.2. Why grid-scale batteries matter 32
5.2 Why grid-scale batteries matter
5.2.1 Operational importance
Grid-scale battery energy storage systems provide temporal flexibility to modern power
systems. They are used for arbitrage, peak shaving, renewable smoothing, reserve support,
congestion relief, and resilience-oriented backup. Unlike a generator, which primarily trans-
forms energy from one form to another, a battery shifts energy in time. That time-shifting
ability gives the operator leverage over both economics and stability. The same property,
however, also creates risk: a control mistake at one hour changes what is physically feasible
at the next.
This temporal coupling is one of the reasons the battery domain is stronger than a static
decision problem. A poor decision does not merely reduce revenue. It also changes the
plant’s future reachable set. A true-state violation can create asset stress, reduce remaining
flexibility, and alter the validity of future control decisions. In ORIUS terms, the battery is
therefore a physically meaningful substrate for studying how degraded observation changes
safe action capacity.
5.2.2 Why safety matters in this domain
Battery dispatch is safety-critical because the plant operates under hard physical limits. State
of charge must remain within an admissible window, power commands must remain within
inverter and cell limits, and ramp transitions must remain inside acceptable operational
envelopes. If the controller issues commands against an incorrect state estimate, the plant
can be pushed into overcharge, over-discharge, or repeated excursions near unsafe boundaries.
These are not merely accounting mistakes. They are physically meaningful errors.
The thesis therefore treats battery safety at two levels. The first is direct physical safety:
remain inside the true state-of-charge, power, and transition limits. The second is operational
integrity: preserve useful work without achieving performance by silently violating the physical
plant. The battery paper’s hidden-gap result is important precisely because it shows that a
controller can appear safe on the observed surface while violating the true physical boundary
underneath it.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 67 ===
5.3. GridPulse as a closed decision loop 33
5.2.3 Why batteries are a good firstORIUS domain
The battery domain is not claimed to be the final universal proof surface. It is claimed to be
the best first one. Several properties make it especially suitable:
1. The main safety state variable, state of charge, is bounded and interpretable.
2. Telemetry faults can be injected and studied realistically.
3. The plant can be simulated with enough fidelity to support proof-oriented reasoning.
4. The economic objective is nontrivial, so safety is studied in tension with useful work
rather than in a shutdown-only setting.
5. A later hardware-in-the-loop path is realistic because battery hardware is accessible
compared with many other physical AI domains.
These features make the domain mathematically clean enough for theorem writing and
operationally rich enough for manuscript-level systems evaluation.
5.3 GridPulse as a closed decision loop
5.3.1 The five-stage loop
Across the paper, thesis, and update materials, GridPulse is described as the following closed
loop:
Forecast→Optimize→Dispatch→Measure→Monitor
The loop should be read as a recurrent decision architecture rather than as a one-shot pipeline.
Forecast Produce point forecasts and uncertainty intervals for load, wind, solar, price,
and related operational variables.
Optimize Compute a candidate battery action that trades off cost, carbon exposure, and
peak-related penalties subject to operational constraints.
Dispatch Send the selected action to the battery plant or plant emulator.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 68 ===
5.3. GridPulse as a closed decision loop 34
Measure Record realized battery evolution, telemetry state, and any fault process acting
on the observation channel.
Monitor Update reliability, drift status, residual behavior, and audit information for the
next cycle.
This decomposition matters because it lets the manuscript identify where particular errors are
born and where they can still be corrected. Forecasting errors originate upstream. Telemetry
faults enter through the observation channel. The physically decisive moment occurs when a
candidate action leaves the optimization stage and becomes an actual plant command.
Figure 5.1: Artifact-backed GridPulse/DC3S pipeline illustration used in the battery-first
thesis build. The control story is not merely forecast-to-optimization; it is forecast-to-
optimization-to-safe execution under runtime observation uncertainty.
5.3.2 Why the loop is a safety argument
A manuscript-level treatment should not describe this loop only as software architecture. It is
also a causal safety argument. Forecasts influence the candidate action. Observation quality
influences whether forecast uncertainty should be trusted. The shield influences whether the
candidate may safely cross into execution. Measurement closes the loop by revealing whether
the plant remained safe and whether the current belief surface remained trustworthy. In
other words, GridPulse is the concrete battery-domain embodiment of the ORIUS principle
that physical AI safety is bounded by observation reliability.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 69 ===
5.4. The Optimize–Dispatch boundary as the true risk location 35
5.4 The Optimize–Dispatch boundary as the true risk
location
5.4.1 Why forecasting alone is not enough
A common misunderstanding is to interpret the battery thesis as a stronger forecasting paper.
That interpretation is incomplete. Better forecasts improve economic dispatch, but they do
not by themselves guarantee safe dispatch when the present observation channel is degraded.
A system can forecast tomorrow well and still misunderstand the battery’s current state
badly enough to issue an unsafe action. This is why the thesis repeatedly argues that the
decisive safety problem is not only uncertainty about the future; it is also uncertainty about
whether the controller is seeing the present correctly.
5.4.2 The last safe point of intervention
The candidate action generated by the optimizer is still virtual until it is dispatched. After
dispatch, the plant has already received the command. At that point, logging, alarms, or
after-the-fact dashboards cannot undo the physical consequence. The last safe point of
intervention is therefore the boundary between optimization and execution. This is why
DC3S is placed there.
Formally, this boundary is where three things come together:
a) the candidate action is already optimized against an observed state surface,
b) the true plant will execute that action in the physical world,
c) the controller still has one final chance to account for degraded telemetry before the
command becomes physical.
That triad is what makes the boundary the correct control-theoretic location for ORIUS.
5.4.3 The architectural thesis claim
The architectural claim of the manuscript can be stated clearly:

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 70 ===
5.5. Operational telemetry pathways 36
Thehiddensafetyfailureinbatterydispatchisbornwhentheoptimizer’scandidate
is validated against observed state but executed on the true plant; therefore the
shield must act at the execution boundary.
This claim is stronger than a software design preference. It is the systems interpretation of
the OASG.
5.5 Operational telemetry pathways
5.5.1 Telemetry as state, context, and metadata
Battery telemetry matters in at least three ways.
1. Plant state:it provides the controller’s estimate of battery state, especially state of
charge and recent operating condition.
2. Context: it provides recent load, renewable, and price context that shapes both
forecasting and dispatch.
3. Meta-reliability: the telemetry stream itself carries evidence about missingness, age,
ordering, spikes, and drift, all of which determine how much the controller should trust
the state and forecast surfaces built from it.
The third pathway is the one most neglected in conventional pipelines. Many systems use
telemetry as data but ignore telemetry as evidence about its own reliability. ORIUS makes
that evidence first-class through the observation-quality score.
5.5.2 The fault taxonomy in operational terms
The ORIUS battery fault taxonomy can be read operationally as follows:

5.6. Why measurement quality enters the control problem 37

These are not artificial benchmark categories. They are compact representations of realistic
ways in which operational telemetry can become less trustworthy.
5.6 Why measurement quality enters the control prob-
lem
5.6.1 The standard engineering response
In many practical systems, measurement degradation is handled outside the controller.
Operators may receive alerts. A dashboard may flag stale data. A monitoring layer may
record packet loss. The control law itself, however, often remains unchanged. This is
acceptable only if observation degradation is not safety-relevant. The battery thesis is built
on the opposite conclusion: once degraded observation changes whether an action is truly
safe, measurement quality must enter the control law.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 72 ===
5.7. Interfaces and implementation boundary 38
5.6.2 From measurement quality to safe control
The ORIUS logic is simple:
1. candidate actions are chosen based on a belief about the state,
2. degraded telemetry weakens the reliability of that belief,
3. weakened belief should enlarge the uncertainty set,
4. enlarged uncertainty should contract the safe action set,
5. if the candidate leaves that tightened set, it must be repaired before execution.
This is why the quality scorewt is not a cosmetic diagnostic. It is a control variable. It
directly changes what the controller is allowed to do.
5.6.3 A battery-specific interpretation
In battery terms, a lowerwt means the controller should trust the observed SOC less. That
forces the uncertainty set around the true SOC to widen. A wider true-SOC uncertainty set
shrinks the set of charge/discharge actions that can be guaranteed safe. Therefore a lower
wt leads naturally to either more conservative dispatch or more frequent repair. This is the
control-theoretic heart of the battery adapter.
5.7 Interfaces and implementation boundary
5.7.1 Why this chapter matters for the later theorem layer
The theorem chapters later in the manuscript depend on this systems chapter. They need an
explicit story for:
• where the candidate action comes from,
• where the shield sits,
• why observation reliability is logically upstream of safe execution,

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 73 ===
5.8. Chapter summary 39
• and why the battery domain is an acceptable reference domain for the first ORIUS
proofs.
Without this chapter, the formal battery theorems could look detached from the real system.
With it, the theorems can be read as formalizations of a concrete architecture rather than
abstractions floating above it.
5.8 Chapter summary
This chapter placed the battery-domain thesis inside the GridPulse system context. It argued
that grid-scale batteries are a particularly strong first ORIUS domain because they combine
interpretable physical safety, operational significance, and strong dependence on telemetry.
It then described GridPulse as a five-stage closed loop, identified the Optimize–Dispatch
boundary as the true risk location, interpreted telemetry as both state and reliability evidence,
and explained why measurement quality must enter the control law rather than remain outside
it.
The next chapter builds directly on this architectural foundation by specifying the data
surface, telemetry streams, dataset scope, artifact boundary, and evidence contract on which
the battery-domain thesis rests.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 74 ===
Chapter 6
Data, Telemetry, and Dataset Scope
6.1 Chapter purpose
A final-year research thesis must make the empirical substrate of its claims explicit. The
battery-first ORIUS manuscript is not built on abstract symbols alone; it is built on concrete
datasets, telemetry surfaces, locked artifacts, and reproducible evaluation contracts. This
chapter therefore defines the battery-domain data surface on which the rest of the manuscript
depends.
The goals are fivefold. First, the chapter identifies the regional datasets used in the locked
battery evaluation. Second, it distinguishes truth trajectory, observed telemetry, and degraded
telemetry at the data level rather than only in the abstract. Third, it explains the operational
meaning of the main channels: load, solar, wind, price, and battery state. Fourth, it clarifies
why telemetry faults should be treated as ordinary control conditions rather than edge
cases. Fifth, it states the artifact boundary: which surfaces are locked evidence, which are
engineering proxies, and which remain extension targets.
6.2 Dataset regions and evidence surface
6.2.1 German dataset scope
The battery thesis uses a German regional data surface centered on OPSD-style hourly grid
data. In the locked profile documented across the thesis and supporting files, the Germany
dataset contains approximately 17,377 hourly rows. The central targets are load, wind,
40

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 75 ===
6.2. Dataset regions and evidence surface 41
and solar, with aligned exogenous and calendar features used in forecasting and dispatch.
Germany is important not only because it supplies a stable empirical surface, but also because
the battery paper reports its strongest direct economic gains there. This makes Germany
the most persuasive region for demonstrating that safety-preserving control can still preserve
useful work.
6.2.2 United States dataset scope
The locked US dashboard profile centers on an EIA-930 MISO-based hourly surface of
approximately 13,638 rows, with release-family materials also referencing PJM and ERCOT.
The thesis is explicit that the present US result compresses multiple balancing-area effects
into a single higher-level surface, and that this masks structural heterogeneity that a stronger
final manuscript should eventually unpack. That limitation does not invalidate the battery
result; it simply means the regional interpretation chapter must remain disciplined about
what is truly shown versus what is still aggregated.
6.2.3 Why the two-region design matters
Using both Germany and the United States matters for two reasons. First, it shows that
the battery safety story is not tied to a single local data surface. Second, it reveals that
economic improvement and safety preservation need not move identically across regions. The
battery manuscript already reports stronger direct gains in Germany and more modest but
still positive stochastic value in the US. This asymmetry is part of the scientific value of the
thesis because it shows the safety logic is structurally stronger than any one regional cost
benefit.
