6.3 Truth trajectory, observed telemetry, and degraded
telemetry
6.3.1 Truth trajectory
The truth trajectory is the physically meaningful battery evolution maintained by the plant
model or battery simulator. In the battery-domain thesis, this is the trajectory on which true
SOC violations are measured. It is not simply another logged series. It is the authoritative
surface for physical safety. Any controller that appears safe on other surfaces but violates
this one is physically unsafe.
6.3.2 Observed telemetry
Observed telemetry is the controller-facing information surface. It includes the current state
estimate, recent exogenous measurements, and telemetry metadata available at control time.
The critical point is that the controller acts on this surface, not on the truth trajectory. In
clean conditions the two may be close. Under degradation they can separate.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 77 ===
6.4. Operational channels used in the battery thesis 43
6.3.3 Degraded telemetry
Degraded telemetry is the observed stream after the fault operator has acted. In the battery
paper this includes zeroing, delays, injected outliers, and temporal permutation; in the
broader ORIUS taxonomy it expands to dropout, delay-jitter, spike, stale, drift, and reorder.
The distinction between observed telemetry and degraded telemetry is important: observed
telemetry is the data stream the controller sees, while degraded telemetry emphasizes that
the same stream may no longer faithfully represent the plant.
6.3.4 Why this distinction must be data-level, not only theoretical
The hidden-gap claim becomes empirically meaningful only if these surfaces are maintained
separately in the experimental design. A manuscript should not leave them as purely
symbolic objects. The reader must understand that there are actual logged or simulated
signals corresponding to:
• the true battery trajectory,
• the controller-facing observed state,
• and the degraded telemetry stream from which the observed state is inferred.
That separation is what later makesCPSBench possible and what turns OASG from a
conceptual idea into a measured battery phenomenon.
6.4 Operational channels used in the battery thesis
6.4.1 Load channel
Load is one of the central forecasting and dispatch inputs because it determines net demand
pressure and interacts directly with peak-shaving objectives. In the battery-first thesis, load
appears both as a forecast target and as a realized contextual variable. Errors or timing
distortion in load can lead the controller to overcharge or under-discharge relative to actual
system demand.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 78 ===
6.5. Telemetry degradation as an ordinary operating condition 44
6.4.2 Solar and wind channels
Solar and wind matter because they are volatile, forecast-sensitive, and strongly connected
to the uncertainty layer. The thesis and paper repeatedly note that solar, especially in
high-volatility conditions, is the weakest calibration surface in the locked evaluation. This is
important because it means the battery control problem is not just state-estimation sensitive;
it is also sensitive to forecast-interval trustworthiness for fast-varying renewable signals.
6.4.3 Price and carbon channels
Price and carbon enter the battery objective as operational value terms. Even where direct
price realism remains partly proxy-based in the locked engineering setup, these channels are
still important because they determine the useful-work side of the cost–safety tradeoff. A
controller that is safe but economically useless would not satisfy the manuscript’s systems
ambition. The presence of price and carbon channels ensures the thesis remains about safe
useful work, not just shutdown safety.
6.4.4 Battery state channels
Battery state channels include observed SOC and related recent control context such as prior
action history. They are the most safety-critical streams in the entire data surface because
they determine whether the controller believes headroom exists for charging or discharging.
When these channels become stale, drifted, or delayed, the controller’s notion of feasibility
can diverge sharply from reality.
6.5 Telemetry degradation as an ordinary operating
condition
6.5.1 Why faults are not edge cases
A weak manuscript treats telemetry faults as rare anomalies. A stronger manuscript argues
that degraded observation is part of operational reality. In real control systems, missing
packets, stale feeds, delayed SCADA updates, transient outliers, ordering issues, and slowly
accumulating drift are all familiar engineering conditions. The ORIUS thesis adopts this

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 79 ===
6.6. Artifact contracts and locked evidence 45
stronger posture and treats degraded telemetry not as a bizarre attack scenario but as a
normal stress surface for safety-critical control.
6.5.2 Battery-domain consequences of ordinary faults
The battery consequences of these faults are concrete:
• delayed telemetry can cause the controller to act on an outdated SOC estimate,
• stale telemetry can freeze apparent SOC while the plant keeps moving,
• spikes can trigger overreaction,
• drift can create systematic belief–plant divergence,
• reorder can break short-horizon temporal logic used in residual and anomaly monitoring.
The important point is not that each fault is exotic. It is that any one of them can invalidate
the naive assumption that observed feasibility is physically trustworthy.
6.5.3 Why ordinary faults justify battery-first ORIUS
This is one of the reasons the battery domain is so compelling as a first thesis domain. The
failures are not hypothetical. They arise from exactly the sort of telemetry conditions that
operational systems already struggle with. The contribution of ORIUS is not to imagine
impossible failures but to show that ordinary degraded sensing is already enough to produce
hidden physical safety failure if the controller does not adapt its uncertainty and action logic.
6.6 Artifact contracts and locked evidence
6.6.1 What is locked
A strong battery manuscript distinguishes between locked evidence and exploratory analysis.
In the current project, locked evidence includes the main battery paper, the thesis PDF, the
curated battery figures, selected CSV tables, and the validated LaTeX build that packages
these into a reproducible manuscript. The locked evidence surface is what the main results
and theorem-linked discussion should rely on.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 80 ===
6.6. Artifact contracts and locked evidence 46
6.6.2 What remains engineering proxy or extension material
The thesis is also honest that some surfaces remain engineering proxies or extensions rather
than final market or field proof. These include:
• simplified economic channels rather than full real-market replacement everywhere,
• software-in-the-loop rather than completed hardware-in-the-loop validation,
• aggregated US surfaces rather than a full per-balancing-area decomposition,
• partial conditional coverage analysis rather than a full production Mondrian deployment
path.
Being explicit about these boundaries makes the manuscript stronger, not weaker.
6.6.3 Reviewer-style validation of the artifact zip
A direct inspection of the agent artifact zip confirms that the battery-first manuscript is not
missing its core empirical substrate. The archive contains battery paper files, the 300-page
thesis PDF, multiple proof-oriented documents, a large figure set, CSV result tables, and
complete LaTeX source trees. The only meaningful caveats are that some planning documents
live outside the zip in the conversation uploads and that not every figure or table inside
the archive is battery-core enough to belong in the main thesis. The right task is therefore
curation, not raw file hunting.

6.8. Chapter summary 48
6.8 Chapter summary
This chapter defined the battery-domain data surface for the ORIUS thesis. It identified the
locked regional datasets, clarified the distinction between truth trajectory, observed telemetry,
and degraded telemetry, described the operational meaning of the main channels, argued that
telemetry faults are ordinary operating conditions rather than rare anomalies, and stated the
artifact boundary that determines what belongs in the main thesis claims.
The core conclusion is that the battery-first manuscript already has the empirical substrate
it needs. The major work remaining is integration and hardening, not discovery of missing
base files. The next chapter uses this data surface to define the physical battery dynamics
and dispatch problem itself.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 83 ===
Chapter 7
Battery Dynamics and Dispatch For-
mulation
7.1 Chapter purpose
This chapter converts the system and data context of the previous chapters into an explicit
battery control problem. The goal is to make the mathematical objects of the thesis concrete
enough for both theorem writing and experimental evaluation. A good manuscript chapter
here should do more than quote a few equations. It should explain what each variable means
physically, how constraints enter, why the objective is written the way it is, and exactly
where degraded observation changes the problem.
The chapter therefore proceeds from state variables to dynamics, from dynamics to feasible
action sets, from feasible action sets to objectives, and finally from objectives to baseline
policies. The battery-domain ORIUS claim is not that the optimizer is exotic. The claim is
that an otherwise standard battery dispatch problem becomes safety-incomplete when solved
over a degraded observation surface without runtime repair.
7.2 Battery state variables
7.2.1 True battery state
Let xt denote the true battery state at timet. In the battery-first thesis, the load-bearing
component ofxt is the true state of charge, but the practical state can also include recent
49

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 84 ===
7.3. Battery dynamics 50
dispatch history and any other variables needed to compute next-step feasibility. The
important point is thatxt is the state on which real safety is judged.
7.2.2 Observed battery state
Let ot denote the observed state at timet. This is the controller-facing representation of the
battery after telemetry corruption, latency, or drift has affected the measurement surface.
The controller optimizes againstot, not againstxt. The hidden safety gap exists precisely
because these can differ.
7.2.3 State variables used in dispatch
For battery dispatch, the essential variables are:
• state of charge, as the dominant physical margin variable,
• current or recent dispatch action, because ramping constraints depend on action history,
• exogenous context variables such as load and renewable conditions, because they shape
the dispatch objective,
• reliability variables such aswt, because they determine how much uncertainty should
be attached to the observed state.
These objects are deliberately minimal. A battery-domain thesis should make the system
mathematically clear without introducing unnecessary state complexity.
7.3 Battery dynamics
7.3.1 True SOC evolution
The battery paper defines the true state-of-charge evolution as
SOC true
t+1 =SOC true
t + ηc[at]+−[at]−/ηd
Emax
∆t, (7.1)

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 85 ===
7.4. Physical constraints 51
where ηc and ηd are charge and discharge efficiencies,Emax is the rated capacity, and[u]+
and [u]−denote the positive and negative parts of the control action. This is the physical
update law used by the plant and later by the one-step safety theorem.
7.3.2 Interpretation of the sign convention
The sign convention should be stated clearly so the manuscript stays readable. Positive
dispatch corresponds to one direction of battery power flow and negative dispatch to the
other; whichever sign convention is adopted in implementation, the thesis must keep it fixed
consistently. The equation above makes the underlying physical point clear: the next true
SOC depends on the current true SOC, efficiency losses, and the actual executed action.
7.3.3 Model error and identified dynamics
The battery theorem chapters do not assume perfect plant knowledge. Instead, they work
under bounded model error. This means the controller may use an identified or approximate
transition model ˆf whose difference from the true plant dynamicsf remains bounded. That
assumption is enough to support the local safety results later in the thesis without pretending
the battery model is exact in all regimes.
7.4 Physical constraints
7.4.1 Power constraints
The first constraint class is the power envelope:
|at|≤Pmax. (7.2)
This limits the magnitude of charge or discharge at any single step.
7.4.2 Ramp-rate constraints
The second class is the ramp-rate envelope:
|at−at−1|≤Rmax. (7.3)

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 86 ===
7.4. Physical constraints 52
This matters because abrupt changes in dispatch may be infeasible or undesirable even when
the instantaneous power bound is respected.
7.4.3 True-state SOC constraints
The third and most important class is the SOC safety envelope:
SOC min≤SOC true
t+1 ≤SOC max. (7.4)
This is the constraint that becomes hidden under degraded observation. The controller may
believe it is satisfying this inequality while the physical battery is not.
7.4.4 Combined feasible action set
The paper defines the feasible action set as the set of actions satisfying all three classes
simultaneously. In words, an admissible action must be inside the power bound, respect
the ramp limit relative to the previous action, and keep the next true SOC inside the
physical safety interval. That feasible set is standard battery-control machinery. The ORIUS
contribution comes later, when degraded observation forces the controller to reason not over
one state estimate, but over a reliability-aware uncertainty region.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 87 ===
7.5. Dispatch objective 53
7.5 Dispatch objective
7.5.1 Why the objective is multi-term
A serious battery controller is not optimizing safety in isolation. It is trying to preserve
useful work under safety constraints. The battery paper therefore uses a multi-term objective
combining energy cost, carbon exposure, peak-demand penalty, and throughput-related
degradation. This is the right choice for a manuscript because it ensures the safe controller is
compared against economically meaningful behavior rather than against a trivial always-off
policy.
7.5.2 Comparative engineering objective
In the paper, the dispatch objective is written as a weighted sum over the horizon:
mina1:T
T∑
t=1
λcctat +λggt[at]+ +λp max{0,L t +at−Pcap}+λd|at|. (7.5)
The exact economic realism of this objective is not the main claim of the manuscript. Its
role is to provide a transparent, reproducible engineering objective that preserves the real
tradeoff between performance and safety.
7.5.3 Interpretation of the objective terms
The terms can be read as follows:
• λcctat captures price-weighted energy movement,
• λggt[at]+ captures grid or carbon exposure under one operating direction,
• λp max{0,L t +at−Pcap}penalizes peak exceedance,
• λd|at|penalizes throughput or wear-related activity.
The safe-controller story of ORIUS is not that these objectives disappear. It is that they
must be optimized over an uncertainty-aware, telemetry-quality-aware feasible surface.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 88 ===
7.6. From nominal feasibility to reliability-aware feasibility 54
7.6 From nominal feasibility to reliability-aware feasi-
bility
7.6.1 Nominal feasible set
In a standard dispatch formulation, the controller chooses actions from the feasible set induced
by the observed state estimate. Under clean measurement assumptions, this is a reasonable
approximation to true feasibility. Under degraded telemetry, it becomes unsafe to treat the
observed point estimate as fully trustworthy.
7.6.2 Uncertainty set and safe action set
The ORIUS thesis introduces a reliability-aware uncertainty setUt(α) built from a base
calibrated interval widened according to observation quality. The corresponding safe action
set can be written abstractly as
Asaf e
t ={a :f(x,a )∈Ssafe∀x∈Ut(α)}. (7.6)
This means an action is allowed only if it preserves safety for every plausible true state
consistent with the degraded observation and widened uncertainty.
7.6.3 Why this changes the battery problem
This is the key mathematical turn. The problem is no longer merely “choose the best action
for the observed state.” It becomes “choose the best action that is safe across the plausible
true states induced by degraded observation.” The difference is exactly what allows ORIUS
to close the hidden safety gap.
7.7 Baseline policy family
7.7.1 Deterministic LP dispatch
The deterministic LP controller is the natural nominal baseline. It uses standard constraints
and point forecasts or fixed uncertainty assumptions without observation-quality adaptation.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 89 ===
7.8. Problem statement 55
The battery paper’s hidden-gap result is most dramatic against this baseline because it shows
that seemingly sensible nominal control can still violate true physical limits under degraded
telemetry.
7.7.2 Grid-only or no-battery reference
The grid-only reference is useful because it shows the cost and peak surface without battery
intervention. It is not a strong control baseline in a technical sense, but it provides an
operational reference point for the value of using the battery at all.
7.7.3 Heuristic battery rules
Simple rule-based battery dispatch offers a third comparison surface. These heuristics are
often weaker than optimization-based control, but they are useful because they reveal whether
the ORIUS gains are merely gains over a poorly designed controller or are robust against a
broader controller family.
7.7.4 Why baselines matter in the problem chapter
A high-quality manuscript should name the baseline family early, because the later results
and theorem interpretation depend on what counts as a meaningful comparison. The current
battery-first thesis is not claiming superiority over all imaginable controllers. It is showing
that under realistic degraded telemetry, quality-aware dispatch-time safety repair changes
the fundamental ranking of controllers that otherwise appear strong on nominal evaluation.
7.8 Problem statement
7.8.1 Battery-domain control problem under degraded observation
The battery-domain ORIUS problem can now be stated plainly:
Given a battery plant with hard physical constraints, a degraded telemetry
channel, forecast uncertainty, and an optimizer that proposes candidate actions
using observed state, construct a runtime control layer that preserves true-state
safety while retaining as much useful work as possible.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 90 ===
7.9. Chapter summary 56
This statement contains the whole thesis in compressed form. The plant contributes the
physical state and constraints. The telemetry channel creates the possibility of belief–reality
divergence. The optimizer contributes useful nominal action selection. The runtime layer
supplies quality-aware uncertainty widening and action repair.
7.8.2 Why this is not a standard battery problem
The thesis must be explicit here: this is not only a standard dispatch problem with a few
extra penalties. It is a dispatch problem under degraded observation where the validity of
the feasible set itself becomes runtime-dependent. That is the key distinction from much of
the existing literature.
7.9 Chapter summary
This chapter defined the battery-domain state variables, dynamics, physical constraints,
objective, safe action set, and baseline family. It showed that the nominal battery dispatch
problem is already rich enough to support meaningful optimization, but that it becomes
structurally different once degraded observation is admitted into the formulation. The next
chapter builds on this by developing the forecasting and calibration layer that supplies the
uncertainty surface used by the controller and later widened by the ORIUS battery adapter.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 91 ===
Chapter 8
Forecasting and Calibration
8.1 Chapter purpose
This chapter develops the uncertainty layer that feeds the battery controller. In the ORIUS
battery stack, forecasting is not a decorative front-end. It supplies the point predictions and
interval surfaces from which candidate actions are chosen. If those uncertainty surfaces are
too narrow, the optimizer becomes overconfident. If they are too wide, useful work collapses.
If they are calibrated on clean conditions but deployed under degraded telemetry without
adaptation, the controller can appear statistically principled while remaining physically
unsafe.
The chapter therefore explains four things: the forecast targets, the model families evaluated
in the locked battery pipeline, the conformal calibration layer used to construct base intervals,
and the reliability-conditioned logic that later widens those intervals when measurement
quality deteriorates.
8.2 Forecast targets
8.2.1 Why forecasting is needed
Battery dispatch is forward-looking. A battery is valuable because it moves flexibility through
time. To do that effectively, the controller needs estimates of future load, renewable generation,
and related conditions. Even a battery controller whose ultimate thesis contribution is safety-
aware runtime shielding still depends on a forecasting layer, because candidate actions are
57

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 92 ===
8.3. Model families in the locked comparison 58
chosen against anticipated future conditions.
8.2.2 Core targets in the battery pipeline
The locked GridPulse pipeline centers on:
• load,
• wind generation,
• solar generation,
• and derived operational channels used by the dispatch objective.
These are sufficient to support the battery manuscript’s core argument. The manuscript
should not overcomplicate this by pretending that a much larger universe of forecast targets
is necessary for the battery-first proof surface.
8.3 Model families in the locked comparison
8.3.1 Why multiple families are compared
A final-year thesis should show that the uncertainty and safety story is not an accident of
one forecasting architecture. The locked battery comparison therefore evaluates multiple
forecasting families under a common protocol. This matters scientifically because it separates
the ORIUS contribution from mere model selection.
8.3.2 Gradient-boosted machines
Gradient-boosted machines are a strong tabular baseline in the battery domain because the
feature pipeline already encodes much of the temporal structure through lagged, rolling, and
calendar variables. In the locked comparison, GBM performs unexpectedly strongly, often
outperforming heavier deep sequence models. This is a useful manuscript finding in its own
right because it shows that for medium-scale, strongly seasonal grid data, careful feature
design can dominate architectural complexity.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 93 ===
8.3. Model families in the locked comparison 59
8.3.3 N-BEATS
N-BEATS is included as a high-capacity sequence model baseline. Its relevance is not that it
must win, but that it represents a widely recognized forecasting family and therefore makes
the comparison more serious. In the battery thesis, its role is to show that ORIUS does not
depend on hiding behind weak baselines.
8.3.4 Temporal Fusion Transformer
TFT is included because it provides a modern transformer-based forecasting baseline with
interpretable attention mechanisms and mixed static/dynamic feature handling. In a battery
thesis, this matters less for attention as such and more because it shows the system has been
tested against a state-of-the-art sequential learner rather than only against shallow models.
8.3.5 PatchTST
PatchTST represents the patch-based transformer line for time-series modeling. Its inclusion
broadens the comparison set and helps support the manuscript’s claim that the battery-first
safety result is not just a by-product of one particular forecaster.
8.3.6 Locked comparison logic
The key manuscript interpretation is this: the forecasting layer is governed and comparative.
The six-model comparison is not the main novelty of the thesis, but it is an important
controlled surface. It shows that the safety story is robust to different predictive front-ends
and that the strong battery-domain results are not achieved by quietly choosing one uniquely
favorable forecaster.

8.4 Feature engineering and training protocol
8.4.1 Why the protocol matters
A weak forecasting chapter reports model names and test metrics. A stronger manuscript
explains why the protocol itself makes the comparison credible. In the battery thesis, the
main locked protocol uses a 24-hour forecast horizon, a 168-hour lookback, time-aware
cross-validation, and a temporal gap between training and evaluation windows. This matters
because the forecasting surface is later used as a control input, so leakage or split errors
would contaminate not only model selection but also the battery safety story.
8.4.2 Feature families
The feature pipeline includes:
• weather context,
• lagged target values,
• rolling statistics,

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 95 ===
8.5. Base uncertainty: conformalized quantile regression 61
• calendar variables,
• domain-specific peak/ramp indicators.
This rich tabular structure helps explain why GBM performs so well in the locked setting.
The manuscript should state this directly: the battery data regime is not a giant end-to-end
sequence-learning benchmark. It is a medium-scale, highly structured grid-data regime where
feature engineering remains powerful.
8.5 Base uncertainty: conformalized quantile regression
8.5.1 Why interval prediction matters more than point prediction
alone
The controller does not act only on point forecasts. It acts on uncertainty intervals. If the
interval is too narrow, the optimizer becomes bold. If it is too wide, the battery loses useful
work. Therefore interval quality is not auxiliary. It is part of the control surface.
8.5.2 CQR as the base interval layer
The battery thesis uses conformalized quantile regression as the base uncertainty layer. The
basic logic is standard: learn lower and upper quantiles, compute conformity scores on a
calibration set, and enlarge the raw quantile band using an empirical quantile of the residuals.
The result is a prediction interval with finite-sample marginal-coverage properties under the
assumed calibration regime.
8.5.3 What the base interval does and does not guarantee
The manuscript must be explicit about the guarantee boundary:
• CQR provides a meaningful base interval under the clean or calibration-aligned regime.
• It doesnot by itself guarantee conditional per-fault coverage under degraded telemetry.
This distinction is crucial. ORIUS does not replace CQR. It takes CQR seriously enough to
identify where its deployment assumptions break.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 96 ===
8.6. Marginal coverage and the calibration gap 62
Figure 8.1: Artifact-backed confidence-interval visualization used to illustrate the role of
predictive intervals in the battery pipeline. The manuscript uses the base interval as a starting
point, not as the final safety object.
8.6 Marginal coverage and the calibration gap
8.6.1 Marginal coverage
The base conformal objective is to achieve approximately(1−α) marginal coverage. This is
the classical starting point of the battery uncertainty story. It is useful, but not sufficient.
8.6.2 Why marginal coverage is not enough for safety
Marginal coverage is an average property. Safety failures under degraded observation are
not average phenomena. They concentrate in regimes where the observation channel is stale,
drifted, delayed, or otherwise unreliable. A base interval can therefore look acceptable globally
while under-covering exactly the subgroups that matter most for safe control. The thesis
should say this plainly because it is one of the central conceptual bridges from forecasting to
ORIUS.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 97 ===
8.7. Solar and wind calibration weaknesses 63
8.6.3 Reliability-conditioned under-coverage
The battery thesis already points to this issue through reliability-bin and subgroup analysis.
The important interpretation is not merely that some bins under-cover. It is that under-
coverage has structure. Reliability loss, volatility, and fault conditions create regimes where
naive transfer of clean-calibration intervals into runtime control becomes dangerous.
8.7 Solar and wind calibration weaknesses
8.7.1 Why solar is hard
Solar is difficult because it combines high relative volatility, zero-inflated periods, and regime
changes tied to daylight and weather. The thesis materials already note that solar, particularly
under high-volatility conditions, is the weakest calibration surface in the locked setting. This
is valuable because it prevents the manuscript from sounding like every uncertainty target
was solved equally well.
8.7.2 Why wind matters too
Wind is also challenging because it exhibits broad variability and long-range structure that
can stress both point prediction and calibration. In the battery-first setting, even if load is
forecast comparatively well, renewable uncertainty still shapes how aggressive dispatch can
safely be.
8.7.3 The manuscript-strength interpretation
A mature manuscript does not hide its hardest surface. It explains why the hardest surface
matters and how the rest of the architecture responds. The correct interpretation here is not
“forecasting is weak.” It is “forecasting uncertainty is heterogeneous, which is exactly why
reliability-conditioned runtime widening is needed.”

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 98 ===
8.8. RAC-Cert and reliability-aware widening 64
8.8 RAC-Cert and reliability-aware widening
8.8.1 Why interval widening is necessary
Once telemetry quality deteriorates, the controller should trust the base interval less. If it
does not, then the safe action set is being computed from an uncertainty object that no
longer reflects the deployed sensing regime. RAC-Cert is the mechanism that fixes this.
8.8.2 Battery interpretation of RAC-Cert
In the battery adapter, RAC-Cert widens the base interval as a function of observation-quality
loss, optional drift evidence, and sensitivity or staleness signals. The important structural
properties are:
• it does not narrow the interval when quality worsens,
• it is bounded above to avoid runaway conservatism,
• it provides a runtime bridge from degraded observation to safe control.
8.8.3 What RAC-Cert accomplishes
RAC-Cert does not prove full safety by itself. What it does is produce a more honest
uncertainty object under degraded observation. That widened set is then passed into the
safe-action logic. This layered interpretation is important because it keeps the manuscript
mathematically honest: forecasting, calibration, uncertainty inflation, and action repair are
related, but not identical, contributions.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 99 ===
8.9. Why calibration alone cannot close the gap 65
Figure 8.2: Artifact-backed illustration of the RAC-Cert mechanism. The battery-first thesis
uses this layer to transform calibrated forecasting intervals into runtime-safe uncertainty
objects under degraded telemetry.
8.9 Why calibration alone cannot close the gap
8.9.1 The core limitation
Even a well-calibrated forecasting layer does not by itself close the observed-state safety
illusion. Calibration speaks to prediction uncertainty. The hidden gap appears when the
controller validates a candidate action using an observed state surface that may already be
misaligned with the plant. Therefore forecasting and calibration are necessary but insufficient.
8.9.2 The layered ORIUS answer
The ORIUS answer is layered:
1. forecast the relevant battery-context variables,
2. calibrate base predictive intervals,
3. widen those intervals when measurement quality drops,

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 100 ===
8.10. Chapter summary 66
4. tighten the safe feasible set,
5. repair any candidate action that leaves the tightened set.
This chapter covers the first three elements. The next chapters cover the last two.
8.10 Chapter summary
This chapter developed the forecasting and calibration layer of the battery-first thesis. It
specified the forecast targets, the locked model-family comparison, the feature and split
protocol, the role of conformalized quantile regression, the distinction between marginal and
reliability-conditioned coverage, and the motivation for RAC-Cert widening under degraded
telemetry. The chapter’s main conclusion is that calibration is indispensable but insufficient:
it produces the base uncertainty object that ORIUS later reshapes into a safe control surface.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 101 ===
Chapter 9
DC3S / ORIUS Battery Adapter
9.1 Chapter purpose
This chapter presents the battery-domain runtime architecture that closes the hidden safety
gap at execution time. In the earlier chapters, the problem was built from first principles: the
battery controller acts on observed state, the true battery evolves in the physical world, and
under degraded telemetry the controller can approve actions that are observationally feasible
but physically unsafe. The role ofDC3S—later interpreted more generally as the ORIUS
battery adapter—is to prevent that hidden failure from crossing the execution boundary.
This chapter is one of the architectural cores of the manuscript. It must therefore do more
than list components. It needs to explain what the runtime layer actually computes, why
each computation is necessary, how the pieces compose, and how the architecture remains
battery-grounded rather than generic. The chapter proceeds from motivation to runtime
objects, then through the five practical stages of the adapter: detection, calibration, constraint
tightening, repair, and certification.
9.2 Why a runtime shield is needed
9.2.1 The inadequacy of nominal dispatch alone
Nominal dispatch is not enough because the optimizer validates actions against the state
surface it is given. If telemetry has degraded, that state surface may no longer be trustworthy.
The optimizer can therefore remain mathematically correct and physically wrong. This is
67

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 102 ===
9.3. Runtime objects of the battery adapter 68
not a rare software edge case. It is the structural reason the battery paper reports nonzero
true-state violations under seemingly safe nominal control.
9.2.2 Why post hoc monitoring is too late
A manuscript-level safety architecture must intervene before unsafe commands reach the plant.
After execution, logging and alarms are informative but no longer preventive. If a battery is
pushed toward an unsafe true-SOC region, the right place to stop that command is before
dispatch, not in a dashboard afterward. This is why the battery paper and thesis consistently
place DC3S at the Optimize–Dispatch boundary rather than in a later monitoring-only layer.
9.2.3 Conditional conservatism as the design goal
The goal is not blanket conservatism. A shield that always overrides the optimizer would
be safe but operationally trivial. The design target is conditional conservatism: remain
permissive when the observation channel is trustworthy and become protective when it is not.
This is exactly why the intervention-rate result matters in the battery paper. The shield is
not meant to dominate the controller at all times. It is meant to intervene when telemetry
quality makes nominal feasibility unreliable.
9.3 Runtime objects of the battery adapter
The battery adapter is built around a small set of runtime objects that already appeared in
the problem chapter but now take on operational meaning.
• wt: the observation-quality score.
• dt: drift or distribution-shift evidence.
• st: a sensitivity or staleness-related auxiliary signal.
• ˆCt(α): the base calibrated interval.
• ˆC RAC
t (α): the reliability-adapted interval after widening.
• Ut(α): the induced state uncertainty set.
• a⋆
t: the optimizer’s candidate action.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 103 ===
9.4. Stage I: telemetry reliability scoring 69
• Asaf e
t : the tightened safe-action set.
• asaf e
t : the repaired action released to the plant.
•Kt: the step-level certificate and audit context.
The value of naming these objects explicitly is that they connect the mathematics, the
runtime code path, and the experimental evaluation. A research-level architecture chapter
should make that mapping visible.
9.4 Stage I: telemetry reliability scoring
9.4.1 Purpose of the quality score
The quality scorewt∈[0, 1] answers the runtime question:how much should the controller
trust the current observation channel?This is the first distinctive move of ORIUS. Telemetry
quality is not merely logged; it is transformed into a scalar that changes the downstream
uncertainty and safe-action geometry.
9.4.2 Signal ingredients
In the battery implementation, the score is built from four practical signal ingredients:
1. missingness or dropout,
2. delay/staleness,
3. out-of-order packet behavior,
4. spikes or large relative jumps.
Each ingredient is physically meaningful in a telemetry-dependent control system. Missingness
removes direct support for the state estimate. Delay makes the estimate old. Reordering
breaks temporal consistency. Spikes create false urgency. The score aggregates these into a
single runtime trust signal.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 104 ===
9.5. Stage II: drift detection and sensitivity probing 70
9.4.3 Why a single score is useful
A single score is useful because the rest of the control loop needs a compact control-facing
signal rather than a list of diagnostics. The optimizer and shield do not need a human-
readable telemetry report. They need a number that changes the uncertainty surface and
feasible action set in the correct direction.
9.5 Stage II: drift detection and sensitivity probing
9.5.1 Why reliability is not enough by itself
Not every dangerous regime is captured by packet-level faults alone. Slow distribution shift
can leave the telemetry stream numerically plausible while moving it away from the calibration
or training regime. This is especially important in the battery domain because drift may not
create an obvious one-step anomaly; it can accumulate quietly until the controller’s belief
surface is meaningfully wrong.
9.5.2 Drift evidence
The thesis and update materials describe a residual-based drift detector, such as a Page–
Hinkley style mechanism. In the battery adapter, the exact detector is less important than
the architectural role it plays: it provides evidence that the deployed environment has drifted
away from the one in which the base interval was trustworthy.
9.5.3 Sensitivity signal
The sensitivity signalst captures how strongly the current decision depends on uncertain
quantities or recent anomalous conditions. Even if reliability degradation is not catastrophic,
high sensitivity can justify extra conservatism. This is useful in practice because the same
small quality loss can be harmless in a flat operating regime and dangerous near a boundary.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 105 ===
9.6. Stage III: RAC-Cert uncertainty inflation 71
9.6 Stage III: RAC-Cert uncertainty inflation
9.6.1 From base interval to reliability-aware interval
The battery adapter does not discard the forecasting layer; it wraps it. Starting from the base
conformal interval ˆCt(α), the runtime computes a widened intervalˆC RAC
t (α) as a function of
quality loss, drift evidence, and sensitivity. The important properties are monotonicity and
boundedness:
• worsening quality should not shrink the interval,
• widening should remain bounded above.
These properties support both the intuition of the controller and the theorem layer later in
the manuscript.
9.6.2 Why widening matters operationally
Widening matters because the optimizer and shield reason from the uncertainty object they
are given. If the interval remains narrow while the observation channel deteriorates, the
controller becomes overconfident at exactly the moment when it should be more cautious.
RAC-Cert corrects that mismatch.
9.6.3 Battery interpretation
In battery terms, RAC-Cert says that if the controller trusts observed SOC less, it should
behave as though a wider range of true SOC values is plausible. That wider range contracts
the admissible action set and makes more nominally attractive actions inadmissible. This is
how telemetry degradation becomes safe-control geometry.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 106 ===
9.7. Stage IV: safe-action filtering and repair 72
Figure 9.1: Artifact-backed illustration of reliability-aware interval inflation. The interval
should expand in the direction of lower trust, not remain fixed while the observation surface
degrades.
9.7 Stage IV: safe-action filtering and repair
9.7.1 The tightened safe set
Once the reliability-aware uncertainty set is computed, the controller no longer asks whether
the candidate action is safe for one observed point estimate. It asks whether the action is safe
for every plausible true state in the widened set. This induces the tightened action setAsaf e
t .
9.7.2 Why projection is needed
The optimizer may propose an actiona⋆
t that is optimal on the nominal observed surface but
no longer safe under the tightened set. The battery adapter then repairs the action, typically
through projection or a constrained re-solve plus final projection. The key property is not
how elegant the repair is; it is that the released action lies inside the safe set whenever a safe
action exists.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 107 ===
9.8. Stage V: FTIT state tube and margin preservation 73
9.7.3 Safe-action filtering versus blanket fallback
This is where the battery adapter differs from blunt fallback logic. It does not automatically
switch the system off whenever telemetry degrades. Instead, it asks whether a minimally
modified action can keep the battery safe. That is why the architecture preserves useful work
better than a trivial shutdown policy.
9.8 Stage V: FTIT state tube and margin preservation
9.8.1 Why a state tube is useful
The thesis and paper use the FTIT concept to make state uncertainty persistent rather
than purely instantaneous. The idea is that reliability loss and estimation error should not
disappear magically in one step. A state tube or margin buffer allows the controller to reason
over a rolling protected region rather than only over a pointwise interval.
9.8.2 Battery interpretation of the tube
In battery terms, the tube is a state-space buffer around plausible true SOC evolution. It
absorbs state-estimation error, particularly in challenging conditions such as drift or rapidly
changing renewables. This helps explain why the shield can remain safe even when particular
forecast targets or calibration subgroups are imperfect.
9.8.3 Why FTIT matters empirically
The FTIT-counted version of the shield also makes intervention more measurable. It exposes
the extent to which the safe action set has genuinely tightened, rather than silently widening
the interval and hoping the optimizer self-corrects. That is one reason the intervention-rate
metric is meaningful in the battery paper.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 108 ===
9.9. Stage VI: certification and audit ledger 74
9.9 Stage VI: certification and audit ledger
9.9.1 Why certification belongs in the runtime loop
The battery adapter is not only a safety controller. It is also an evidence generator. Every
step should make it possible to answer: what telemetry was observed, what quality score
was computed, what uncertainty widening was applied, what candidate action was proposed,
what safe action was executed, and why. A serious manuscript should treat this as part of
the architecture rather than as a later software nicety.
9.9.2 Contents of the step certificate
A useful step-level certificate can include:
• timestamp and step identity,
• quality score and drift evidence,
• base and widened interval summary,
• candidate action and safe action,
• whether intervention occurred,
• references to the active model and artifact state.
9.9.3 Why the audit ledger matters scientifically
The audit ledger matters because the battery-first thesis is not only proposing a controller. It
is also arguing for reproducible, inspectable safety claims. A claim like “the shield intervened
in 2.8% of steps” is much stronger if the intervention surface is traceable step by step rather
than only aggregated in a final table.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 109 ===
9.10. End-to-end battery control cycle 75
9.10 End-to-end battery control cycle
9.10.1 One-step runtime summary
One battery control cycle under the adapter proceeds as follows:
1. receive degraded telemetry and forecast intervals,
2. score observation quality,
3. update drift and sensitivity evidence,
4. widen the uncertainty interval via RAC-Cert,
5. compute the tightened safe set,
6. test the candidate action,
7. repair if needed,
8. dispatch the safe action,
9. write the certificate record.
This is the operational form of the later theorem ladder. Each stage corresponds to a
theorem-relevant object or map.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 110 ===

Figure 9.2: Artifact-backed control architecture showing the relationship between the
controller-facing observation surface, the truth trajectory, and the safe-execution path. In
the battery manuscript, the runtime shield sits between candidate generation and physical
dispatch.
9.11 Why this chapter is central to the whole thesis
9.11.1 Connection to the empirical chapters
The main battery results later in the manuscript only make sense if the reader understands
what was actually changed relative to a nominal controller. This chapter provides that
explanation. It shows that the battery adapter does not simply “add caution” in a vague
sense; it adds a structured reliability-to-uncertainty-to-safe-action pathway.
9.11.2 Connection to the theorem chapters
The theorem block later in the thesis depends directly on the architecture defined here.
OASG existence depends on the separation between observed and true state. One-step safety
preservation depends on the tightened safe set and repair operator. The Core Bound depends

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 111 ===
9.12. Chapter summary 77
on the reliability-weighted uncertainty process. The temporal theorems depend on certificates
and fallback modes. This chapter therefore serves as the architectural hinge between early
motivation and later mathematical formalization.
9.12 Chapter summary
This chapter presentedDC3S as the battery-domain ORIUS adapter. It motivated the need
for dispatch-time intervention, defined the core runtime objects, developed the stages of
quality scoring, drift detection, RAC-Cert widening, safe-set computation, repair, FTIT-based
state protection, and certification, and showed how these stages form a coherent end-to-end
battery control cycle. The next chapter uses this architecture to define the benchmark that
makes the hidden gap measurable and the shield’s effectiveness testable.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 112 ===
Chapter 10
CPSBench Battery Track
10.1 Chapter purpose
The battery-first thesis does not stand only on a controller and a set of equations. It stands
on an evaluation framework that can distinguish what the controller believes from what the
physical battery actually experiences. This chapter presents the battery track ofCPSBench,
the benchmark logic that makes the hidden safety gap measurable and reproducible.
The chapter serves six purposes. First, it explains why ordinary observed-state evaluation is
insufficient. Second, it defines dual-trajectory evaluation. Third, it specifies the battery fault
surface and replayable fault schedules. Fourth, it introduces the benchmark metric taxonomy.
Fifth, it describes the baseline controller families used in the battery comparison. Sixth, it
states the artifact-traceability and evidence-lock principles required for the benchmark to
function as a manuscript-grade measurement instrument.
10.2 Why standard battery evaluation is insufficient
10.2.1 The blind spot
Standard battery-dispatch evaluation usually measures feasibility and safety on the same state
representation the controller uses. Under clean measurement assumptions this is coherent.
Under degraded observation it becomes a blind spot. If the benchmark judges safety on the
corrupted observed state rather than on the true physical trajectory, then it may certify an
unsafe controller as safe. This is the core methodological failure the battery-first thesis aims
78

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 113 ===
10.3. Dual-trajectory evaluation 79
to correct.
10.2.2 Why this matters scientifically
A final-year research manuscript must show that its main claim is measurable and reproducible.
The hidden safety gap is not convincing if it appears only as an anecdotal simulation story.
The benchmark must enforce a measurement contract under which a controller sees only
degraded observation while the evaluator can still inspect the true plant trajectory. That is
the entire purpose ofCPSBench.
10.3 Dual-trajectory evaluation
10.3.1 Core principle
The defining principle ofCPSBench is simple:
The controller acts on observed state, but the evaluator judges safety on true
state.
This principle is the benchmark-level counterpart of the ORIUS conceptual program. It
ensures that the evaluation surface is not identical to the controller’s potentially corrupted
belief surface.
10.3.2 Observed and truth trajectories
In the battery track, two trajectories are maintained in parallel:
• the observed trajectory, seen by the controller and updated from degraded telemetry,
• the truth trajectory, maintained by the BatteryPlant or equivalent simulator and
used only for evaluation.
The controller never receives the truth trajectory. The evaluator, however, measures safety
on it. This asymmetry is what makes the OASG measurable.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 114 ===
10.4. Battery-specific fault injection surface 80
10.3.3 Benchmark definition
Definition 10.1(Dual-trajectory evaluation). Dual-trajectory evaluation is a benchmark
protocol in which the controller receives only degraded observed telemetry while a separate
plant simulator evolves the true physical state under the same executed actions; safety is
measured on the true state rather than on the observed one.
This definition is simple enough to be memorable and strong enough to anchor the rest of
the benchmark chapter.
Figure 10.1: Artifact-backedCPSBench architecture figure. The controller sees only the
degraded observation stream, while the evaluator retains access to the truth trajectory needed
for physical-safety certification.
10.4 Battery-specific fault injection surface
10.4.1 Why fault injection is necessary
The hidden safety gap does not become visible if the controller is tested only under clean
telemetry. Therefore the battery benchmark must include replayable degraded-sensing

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 115 ===
10.4. Battery-specific fault injection surface 81
scenarios. Fault injection is not a decorative stress test. It is the mechanism that creates the
observed/true state divergence the thesis is about.
10.4.2 Battery fault families
The battery track uses the ORIUS fault taxonomy:
• dropout,
• delay-jitter,
• spike,
• stale,
• drift,
• reorder.
These map cleanly to battery telemetry realities such as missing packets, SCADA lag, outliers,
frozen values, slow sensor bias, and out-of-order measurements.
10.4.3 Replayable schedules
A strong benchmark must make fault schedules replayable. The same controller should be
testable under the same fault surface at a later time so that results remain comparable and
auditable. This means that a fault schedule is not an informal mention of “10% packet drop.”
It is a structured artifact containing timing, parameters, and fault-type identity.

10.5 Benchmark protocol
10.5.1 One-step protocol
Each benchmark step follows a disciplined sequence:
1. start from a truth trajectory and clean telemetry state,
2. inject the active fault schedule into the telemetry stream,
3. expose only the degraded stream to the controller,
4. compute the candidate and, if active, the repaired safe action,
5. apply the executed action to the truth trajectory,
6. update observed and truth surfaces separately,
7. record benchmark metrics and audit state.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 117 ===
10.6. Metric taxonomy 83
10.5.2 Why the protocol matters
This protocol ensures that the controller cannot cheat by accessing truth and that the
evaluator does not accidentally validate safety on the wrong surface. It also ensures that the
benchmark output is not a vague simulation log but a structured measurement surface from
which thesis tables and theorem-linked interpretations can be derived.
10.6 Metric taxonomy
10.6.1 Primary safety metrics
The battery track uses a small but meaningful safety metric set:
• TSVR: true-state violation rate,
• P95 severity: ninety-fifth percentile violation magnitude,
• OASG rate: the fraction of steps where observed-safe differs from true-safe,
• IR: intervention rate for the shield.
These metrics are enough to distinguish hidden failure, physical severity, and the amount of
controller intervention required to restore safety.
10.6.2 Secondary value metrics
The benchmark also records useful-work metrics such as cost, carbon proxy, peak-shaving
performance, VSS, and EVPI where appropriate. The battery-first thesis should be explicit
that these are subordinate to physical safety but still essential to the argument that ORIUS
preserves useful work rather than only blocking control.
10.6.3 Audit and reproducibility metrics
A more manuscript-grade benchmark also cares about audit completeness, replayability, and
artifact traceability. A result is stronger when it can be reattached to the exact fault schedule,
figure, table, and run manifest that produced it.

10.7 Baseline controller families
10.7.1 Why multiple baseline families are necessary
A meaningful benchmark does not compare the proposed controller only against one weak
baseline. It compares classes of controllers built on different assumptions about sensing and
uncertainty.
10.7.2 Battery baseline families
The battery track therefore includes:
1. deterministic nominal dispatch,
2. robust or risk-aware dispatch with fixed uncertainty sets,
3. grid-only or no-battery reference behavior,

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 119 ===
10.8. Evidence lock and artifact traceability 85
4. heuristic battery rules,
5. the ORIUS/DC3S protected controller.
This family set is wide enough to support the thesis claim that controller ranking can change
when evaluation is moved from observed-state-only safety to true-state safety under degraded
sensing.
10.8 Evidence lock and artifact traceability
10.8.1 Why benchmark claims must be traceable
A battery-first thesis should treat every major result as an artifact-backed claim. That means
benchmark metrics should be traceable to fixed datasets, fault schedules, figures, tables, and
run manifests. Otherwise the benchmark becomes difficult to audit and the thesis begins to
resemble an exploratory notebook rather than a defended manuscript.
10.8.2 Artifact-backed benchmark principle
The battery benchmark therefore follows an evidence-lock principle:
A benchmark claim is valid only if it can be traced to a locked artifact, reproducible
figure/table surface, and clearly identified fault schedule.
This principle is as important for manuscript quality as any single theorem. It ensures
that the hidden-gap result and the shielded-controller result are not merely assertions but
recoverable evidence surfaces.
10.9 Reviewer-style benchmark gap analysis
A good final-year thesis should stress-test itself with reviewer-style questions. For the battery
benchmark, four reviewer perspectives are especially useful.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 120 ===
10.9. Reviewer-style benchmark gap analysis 86
10.9.1 Reviewer 1: control-theory reviewer
Main question: does the benchmark really isolate physical safety rather than simply replaying
the controller’s internal belief?
Answer: yes, because the truth trajectory is maintained independently and used for evaluation.
Remaining gap: further hardware-in-the-loop confirmation would strengthen the link from
simulator truth to physical truth.
10.9.2 Reviewer 2: systems reviewer
Main question: is the fault surface realistic enough to matter operationally?
Answer: yes, because it covers dropout, delay, stale, drift, and packet-order effects familiar in
telemetry-dependent systems.
Remaining gap: the manuscript is strengthened further by deeper per-fault tables and
operational traces.
10.9.3 Reviewer 3: ML/uncertainty reviewer
Main question: does the benchmark separate forecasting uncertainty from sensing degrada-
tion?
Answer: yes, because the controller’s interval surface is measured separately from the truth
trajectory, and degraded observation can be varied independently of base forecast quality.
Remaining gap: stronger conditional/subgroup coverage reporting would still help.
10.9.4 Reviewer 4: manuscript-level empirical reviewer
Main question: is the benchmark enough by itself, or does the thesis still need systems
hardening?
Answer: the benchmark is enough to prove the hidden gap and the shield’s battery-domain
value.
Remaining gap: the strongest upgrades still to add are a latency chapter, deeper fault tables,
and a later hardware-in-the-loop chapter.

Chapter 11
Main Battery Results
11.1 Chapter purpose
Thischapterpresentstheprincipalempiricalresultsofthebattery-domain ORIUSmanuscript.
The earlier chapters established the hidden safety gap, the battery runtime shield, and the
benchmark that measures both. The present chapter answers the central practical question:
what happens when deterministic, robust, and ORIUS-protected dispatch policies are
evaluated on the locked battery surface under realistic telemetry degradation? The answer
is the battery-domain proof-of-concept claim on which the thesis stands: under degraded
telemetry, deterministic dispatch is observationally safe but physically unsafe, whereas the
reliability-aware shield closes that gap to zero measured true-state violations.
The chapter is organized around four result surfaces. First, it reports the main controller
comparison and explains why the deterministic baseline is not merely less safe but hiddenly
unsafe. Second, it interprets the zero-violation result and the selective intervention rate of the
shield. Third, it summarizes the regional value surface and the cost–safety frontier. Fourth,
it explains what these results do and do not prove at the manuscript level.
11.2 Locked evaluation surface
All results in this chapter are interpreted on the locked battery artifact surface defined by
the thesis and battery paper: fixed data profiles, fixed fault injection conditions, fixed metric
contracts, and traceable run identifiers. This means that every number reported here should
be read as part of a governed evidence surface rather than as an ad hoc simulation outcome.

11.4. The hidden true-state failure 91
The first point to emphasize is that the hidden failure is not simply that deterministic
dispatch performs poorly in every dimension. Rather, it is that deterministic dispatch
remains internally coherent while becoming unsafe on the physical battery state. This is
what makes the result scientifically important. If the baseline had merely been inaccurate
or economically weak, it would be an ordinary model-comparison result. Instead, it is a
measurement result: the baseline is certified safe on one state surface and unsafe on the true
one.
Figure 11.1: Battery-track true-state violation results from the artifact bundle. TheORIUS-
protected variants remain at zero while non-adaptive baselines accumulate true-state safety
failure under degraded telemetry.
11.4 The hidden true-state failure
The deterministic baseline exhibits a 3.9% true-state violation rate with 333.375 MWh P95
severity while observed-state evaluation reports zero violations. This is the central empirical
witness of the observed-state safety illusion in the battery domain. The result should be
interpreted carefully. It does not mean the baseline is random or incompetent. It means the
baseline’s safety logic is being asked to reason from an observation channel that no longer
tracks the battery truth closely enough for its own feasibility test to remain trustworthy.
This is why the benchmark and the runtime architecture are both necessary. Without dual-
trajectory evaluation, the hidden violation would remain invisible. Without a runtime shield,

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 126 ===
11.5. Zero-violation shield result 92
the hidden violation would remain uncorrected. The battery-domain ORIUS contribution is
strongest exactly at this intersection: the benchmark exposes the hidden safety error and the
runtime layer repairs it.
Proposition 11.1 (Observed-state-only evaluation can understate true battery risk). If
safety is evaluated only on the controller-visible state surface, then hidden true-state violations
caused by degraded telemetry can remain unmeasured even when the controller satisfies all of
its own feasibility checks.
Proof. The benchmark maintains distinct observed and true trajectories. The deterministic
baseline satisfies its own observed-state feasibility checks while producing nonzero true-state
violations. Therefore the observed-state-only evaluation protocol under-reports physical risk
relative to the dual-trajectory protocol.
11.5 Zero-violation shield result
Against that baseline, the battery-domain shield closes the gap. Both locked DC3S variants
achieve zero true-SOC violations. The difference between the wrapped and FTIT variants
is not safety quality but intervention accounting. The wrapped variant widens uncertainty
enough that no post-solve projection is counted, whereas the FTIT variant exposes the subset
of steps in which the candidate falls outside the tightened SOC tube and is repaired by
projection.
This distinction matters because it allows the thesis to make a stronger causal statement.
Zero-violation safety is not obtained by hiding intervention inside the optimizer. The FTIT
variant makes the repair surface visible and still preserves zero violations. That visibility is
one reason the battery-domain result is scientifically persuasive rather than merely convenient.
Table 11.3: Interpretation of the two locked shield variants.
Variant Interpretation
Wrapped DC3S Quality-aware widening is embedded tightly
enough that explicit projection need not be
counted in the locked surface.
FTIT-counted
DC3S
Tightened state tube exposes explicit repair
events, making intervention rate auditable while
preserving zero true-state violations.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 127 ===
11.6. Intervention rate and conditional conservatism 93
11.6 Intervention rate and conditional conservatism
A central practical question is whether zero-violation safety is obtained only by constantly
overriding the optimizer. The answer is no. The FTIT-accounted result reports an intervention
rate of 2.8%, which means the remaining 97.2% of steps require no explicit modification.
This is one of the strongest battery-domain results because it shows that the controller is
not globally shackled. Instead, it becomes more conservative only when degraded telemetry
meaningfully widens uncertainty and shrinks the safe feasible set.
Figure 11.2: Intervention-rate view from the artifact bundle. The shield acts selectively
rather than overriding the controller globally.
Proposition 11.2(Selective-intervention interpretation). If the shield achieves zero true-
state violations with intervention rateIR≪1, then the economic cost of conservatism is
concentrated in genuinely degraded conditions rather than being spread uniformly across the
full operating horizon.
Proof. When IR is small, the candidate action is already feasible for most steps and the
shield acts only in a restricted subset of states. Therefore the runtime protection cost is not a
global override tax but a local correction cost induced by degraded observation and tightened
uncertainty.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 128 ===
11.7. Regional value surface 94
11.7 Regional value surface
The battery-domain evidence separates safety claims from value claims. On the locked
German evaluation, the shielded battery controller achieves 7.11% cost savings, 0.30% carbon
reduction, and 6.13% peak shaving. Stochastic value remains positive in both DE and US
regions, though direct gains are more modest in the US profile. Table 11.4 summarizes this
locked surface.
Table 11.4: Locked regional impact summary from the battery paper.
Region Cost savings % Carbon reduction % Peak shaving % VSS
Germany (OPSD) 7.11 0.30 6.13 2,708.61
US (aggregate) 0.11 0.00 0.00 297,092.71
This asymmetry should not be misunderstood as a weakness of the ORIUS idea. It means
that the safety result is structurally stronger than the economic result. Observation-aware
safety generalizes across regional operating regimes more directly than arbitrage uplift does.
The German profile is the clearest direct-value surface. The US profile is a harder region in
which the same safety logic still matters, even when direct gains are smaller.
Figure 11.3: Locked regional economic surface from the artifact bundle. The German profile
shows the clearest direct economic uplift while the safety benefit remains the primary result
across regions.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 129 ===
11.8. Cost–safety interpretation 95
11.8 Cost–safety interpretation
A useful way to summarize the main controller comparison is as a cost–safety frontier. The
deterministic controller can appear attractive if hidden true-state risk is ignored. The CVaR
and robust fixed baselines move in a safer direction but remain limited because they rely on
fixed or non-quality-aware uncertainty. The protected controller dominates by concentrating
conservatism where degraded observation makes it necessary rather than applying blunt
protection everywhere.
Table 11.5: Qualitative cost–safety interpretation of the main controller families.

11.9 Robustness and severity surfaces
The battery artifact surface emphasizes not only violation rate but also severity growth.
Under increasing fault severity, baseline controllers degrade monotonically while the shielded
controller remains at zero. This pattern matters because a low violation rate can still conceal
economically or physically severe tail events. The P95 severity metric complements TSVR by
making the worst hidden battery events visible.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 130 ===
11.10. Operational meaning of the result 96
Figure 11.4: Fault-severity frontier from the artifact bundle. The protected controller remains
at zero violation while baseline severity grows with degradation intensity.
11.10 Operational meaning of the result
The main battery result should be read in a very specific way. It does not say that battery
dispatch becomes trivial under ORIUS. It says that a controller can be both safe and useful
when measurement reliability is allowed to shape uncertainty and admissible action. The
battery system preserves useful work precisely because it does not apply blunt conservatism
everywhere. This is why the phraseconditional conservatismis so important in the manuscript.
It is not rhetorical language. It is the best compact description of the empirical behavior of
the shield.
11.11 What the results do and do not prove
These results prove the battery-domain empirical foundation of the ORIUS program. They
do not prove field deployment, multi-agent composition, or full universal validation. Their
importance is narrower and stronger: the hidden safety gap is real, measurable, and closeable
in the battery domain. That narrower claim is exactly what makes the thesis credible.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 131 ===
11.12. Chapter summary 97
11.12 Chapter summary
This chapter presented the main battery results. Deterministic dispatch was shown to be
observationally safe but physically unsafe under degraded telemetry. The DC3S / ORIUS
battery adapter closed that gap to zero true-state violations while intervening only selectively.
It also preserved useful work on the locked German profile and maintained positive stochastic-
value interpretation across regional surfaces. The next chapter examines ablations and failure
analysis in more detail.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 132 ===
Chapter 12
Ablations and Failure Analysis
12.1 Chapter purpose
The previous chapter showed that the full battery-domainORIUS system closes the hidden
safety gap. This chapter asks a different question: which parts of the architecture are actually
doing the work? A strong systems manuscript should not only show that the full stack
succeeds. It should also show why it succeeds and what happens when key components are
removed.
The battery-domain ablation story is especially important because the ORIUS claim is
architectural rather than single-module. The shield does not succeed because of one isolated
trick. It succeeds because quality scoring, reliability-aware interval inflation, tightened
feasible-set construction, explicit repair, and state-tube logic reinforce one another. The
ablation chapter therefore turns a successful system into a causal argument.
12.2 Ablation design philosophy
A useful way to interpret the battery ablations is to separate them into three categories:
1. information ablations, which remove the runtime knowledge that the observation
channel is degraded,
2. uncertainty ablations, which remove the mechanism by which degraded knowledge
changes the uncertainty set,
98

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 133 ===
12.3. Reliability scoring ablation 99
3. action-stage ablations, which remove the last barrier preventing unsafe commands
from reaching the battery.
This categorization is helpful because it mirrors the causal chain of the architecture itself:
first the system notices degraded sensing, then it widens uncertainty, then it tightens feasible
actions, then it repairs what is unsafe. The ablations ask what happens when one of those
links is cut.
12.3 Reliability scoring ablation
If per-step observation quality is removed, the controller loses the ability to distinguish
trustworthy from untrustworthy telemetry conditions. In that setting, the uncertainty layer
no longer has a meaningful runtime signal from which to scale protection. The expected result
is exactly what the ORIUS principle predicts: without quality tracking, the controller either
remains overconfident when telemetry degrades or is forced into a blunt fixed-conservatism
regime.
Proposition 12.1(Need for quality scoring). A runtime safety architecture that does not
estimate observation reliability cannot adapt its protection level to degraded telemetry in a
state-dependent way.
Proof. Without a quality variable, the uncertainty layer has no runtime statistic by which to
decide whether the observation channel is currently more or less trustworthy. The controller
must therefore either leave uncertainty fixed or change it using some other less direct signal.
In either case, the protection surface is no longer explicitly tied to sensing quality.
12.4 Inflation ablation
Removing reliability-aware inflation while keeping base forecasting and optimization leaves the
controller exposed to calibration mismatch under degraded sensing. This is one of the clearest
ablations because it isolates the difference between standard uncertainty-aware control and
ORIUS-style quality-aware uncertainty. The battery analysis documents repeatedly stress
that the uncertainty set itself must change with sensing quality; if that adaptation is removed,
the controller becomes robust over the wrong set.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 134 ===
12.5. Projection ablation 100
12.5 Projection ablation
Removing action repair while keeping quality scoring and interval inflation is also revealing.
In that setting, the controller may know that uncertainty has widened but still lack the final
mechanism that prevents an unsafe candidate from reaching the plant. This ablation clarifies
why ORIUS is not only an uncertainty-calibration system. It is also a control-boundary
intervention system.
12.6 FTIT ablation
The FTIT layer makes it possible to reason about safety at the state-tube level rather than
only at the forecast-interval level. An ablation of FTIT helps separate the contribution of
dynamic state buffering from that of interval widening and action repair. In the broader
ORIUS program this matters because FTIT later supports graceful degradation, composition,
and service-layer design, not only battery shielding.
Table 12.1: Interpretive ablation matrix for the battery-domain ORIUS stack.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 135 ===
12.7. Fault-family sensitivity 101
12.7 Fault-family sensitivity
Aggregate metrics can hide the fact that not all degradation modes are equally dangerous.
Fault-family analysis therefore asks which fault types drive the hidden safety gap most
severely and which ones are most effectively corrected by the runtime shield. Drift, stale
values, and delay-like failures are often especially revealing because they preserve apparent
plausibility while silently degrading trust.
Table 12.2: Battery-domain interpretation of fault-family sensitivity.

12.8 When DC3S intervenes
The intervention-rate result becomes more meaningful when paired with a temporal and
causal explanation. The shield should not intervene randomly. It should intervene at the
moments when degraded telemetry meaningfully widens the uncertainty set and pushes the
optimizer’s candidate outside the tightened feasible region. This is another way of showing
conditional conservatism in action.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 136 ===
12.9. When base calibration weakens 102
12.9 When base calibration weakens
The battery thesis is unusually strong in that it does not hide calibration weakness. Solar
under-coverage is explicitly acknowledged. Failure analysis should therefore show how
the runtime system responds when the base predictive layer is weak. This is where the
architecture’s defense-in-depth logic matters most: even if the base interval under-covers,
reliability-aware inflation and safe-action repair can preserve state safety.
Figure 12.1: Artifact-backed ablation figure from the battery bundle. The main point is
structural: removing shield components weakens the safety story in distinct ways rather than
redundantly.
12.10 Why safety is preserved anyway
The main lesson of the ablation chapter is that battery-domainORIUS is not a single trick.
It is an architecture. Safety is preserved because quality tracking, uncertainty inflation,
feasible-set tightening, action projection, and state-level protection reinforce one another.
Removing any one of them weakens the defense. This is precisely the kind of systems-level
causal explanation a strong manuscript needs.
Proposition 12.2 (Defense-in-depth interpretation). If zero-violation safety depends on
multiple sequential control-boundary mechanisms, then the correct systems interpretation is
architectural defense in depth rather than single-module dominance.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 137 ===
12.11. What this chapter does and does not prove 103
Proof. The ablations separate sensing, uncertainty, and action-stage logic. When removing
one mechanism degrades the safety story without fully reproducing the failure pattern of
removing another, the architecture is functioning through layered rather than redundant
protection.
12.11 What this chapter does and does not prove
This chapter does not prove exact quantitative ablation rankings for every possible future
battery implementation. Instead, it proves a more durable systems claim: the ORIUS runtime
architecture works because its pieces are causally linked. The battery result is not explained
away by any single cheap simplification.
12.12 Chapter summary
This chapter explained why the full battery-domain stack works by examining what happens
whenmajorcomponentsareremovedorweakened. Theablationlogicshowsthatzero-violation
safety is not the product of a single module but of a coordinated runtime architecture. The
next chapter complements this structural view with concrete battery-domain case studies
and operational traces.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 138 ===
Chapter 13
Case Studies and Operational Traces
13.1 Chapter purpose
Aggregate tables are necessary but not sufficient. A battery-first manuscript should also
show how the controller behaves over time in concrete operating episodes. This chapter
therefore presents case-study style interpretations of the ORIUS battery runtime using short
operational windows and trace narratives. The goal is not only to show that the shield works
on average, but to make visible how quality loss, interval widening, state divergence, and
action repair interact over time.
13.2 Why operational traces matter
A hidden-safety thesis must make hidden structure visible. In the battery domain, that means
plotting true and observed state of charge, showing how the reliability score evolves, and
identifying the moments when the shield clips or redirects an action that the deterministic
controller would otherwise have sent directly to the plant. Operational traces are therefore
not decorative. They are one of the clearest ways to communicate the causal logic of the
architecture.
104

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 139 ===
13.3. 48-hour case study 105
13.3 48-hour case study
A 48-hour window is long enough to reveal the interaction between forecast shifts, telemetry
quality changes, and dispatch corrections, while still being short enough for visual interpreta-
tion. In this window, the reader can see how the controller moves from nominal behavior
toward more conservative behavior as telemetry quality weakens or drift accumulates. The
most important point is that the shield does not remain constantly active. The trace should
instead reveal normal behavior punctuated by targeted protection when the observation
channel becomes unreliable enough to endanger true-state feasibility.
Table 13.1: Interpretive guide for the 48-hour battery case study.
Trace component What the reader should look for
Observed SOC vs.
