13.4 7-day operational trace
A full week of operation provides a richer picture of battery behavior, including repeated daily
cycles, renewable variation, and multiple opportunities for intervention. A week-long trace is
especially valuable because it shows that the hidden safety gap is not a single outlier event.
Instead, it emerges as a structured runtime phenomenon whenever degraded sensing and
near-boundary control align in the wrong way. The week-long view also makes the selective
nature of the intervention rate easier to believe.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 140 ===
13.5. Price–dispatch interaction 106
Figure 13.1: Artifact-backed time-series comparison used here as a week-scale operational-
trace companion. It supports the interpretation of how the controller, uncertainty layer, and
battery state co-evolve over time.
13.5 Price–dispatch interaction
A useful battery controller must remain responsive to economic opportunity. Operational
traces therefore should not show only safety variables. They should also show how dispatch
decisions track the price surface and when the shield deliberately constrains economically
attractive actions because reliability has fallen too far for those actions to remain safe. This
makes the ORIUS idea clearer: the architecture is not anti-value; it is anti-value-obtained-
through-hidden-risk.
13.6 Reliability score evolution
The per-step quality score is one of the most important internal ORIUS variables. Operational
traces should therefore show howwt evolves during clean periods, gradual drift episodes,

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 141 ===
13.7. Intervention timing 107
and abrupt telemetry faults. A trace ofwt makes the logic of interval inflation and action
restriction much easier to interpret. In particular, the reader should see that declining
reliability is neither arbitrary nor exogenous to the controller’s later behavior. It is the trigger
that causes uncertainty to widen and admissible actions to contract.
13.7 Intervention timing
The intervention rate is informative at the aggregate level, but intervention timing is more
revealing at the causal level. A good operational trace shows that interventions cluster in
precisely those moments when uncertainty widens enough that the nominal candidate action
is no longer admissible. This is what makes the battery-domain shield look principled rather
than arbitrary.
Figure 13.2: Artifact-backed intervention surface reused here to interpret when and why shield
actions occur. In the trace narrative, intervention clusters should coincide with degraded
observation and tightened safe sets rather than with routine operation.
13.8 True versus observed SOC divergence
The single most important trace in the battery manuscript is the divergence between observed
and true state of charge. This is the visual form of the observed-state safety illusion. When

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 142 ===
13.9. Failure avoided by the shield 108
the two trajectories overlap, nominal control is likely sufficient. When they diverge, the
controller’s internal notion of safety becomes untrustworthy. Traces of both trajectories
therefore make the thesis’s central concept visible rather than merely verbal.
Figure 13.3: Artifact-backed OASG illustration. In the case-study chapter, this figure
functions as the conceptual template for reading observed-versus-true SOC divergence in
concrete battery episodes.
13.9 Failure avoided by the shield
A particularly effective case study is one in which the deterministic baseline would have
crossed the true safety boundary but the shielded controller clipped or modified the action
just before violation. This kind of example makes the contribution concrete: the shield
did not merely log elevated risk; it changed what happened physically. The thesis should
emphasize this type of case because it communicates the difference between monitoring and
real intervention.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 143 ===

13.10 What this chapter contributes
This chapter contributes interpretability rather than new metrics. It shows how the hidden
safety gap appears in time, why the controller becomes more conservative when it does,
and how the runtime shield prevents a physically unsafe action from being executed. For a
research thesis, that explanatory layer matters. Reviewers should not be asked to trust only
summary tables. They should also be able to see the system thinking through a concrete
case.
13.11 Chapter summary
This chapter translated the battery-domain ORIUS results into operational narratives. By
following 48-hour and week-long traces, reliability evolution, intervention timing, and true-
versus-observed SOC divergence, it showed how the hidden safety gap appears in real controller
behavior and how the runtime shield closes it step by step. The next chapter distills the
main battery lessons before the manuscript turns fully toward theorem validation.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 144 ===
Chapter 14
Battery Lessons and Domain Interpre-
tation
14.1 Chapter purpose
Before turning to the theorem-validation layer, it is useful to pause and interpret what the
battery domain has actually taught us. The battery proof package is not only a set of
experiments. It is also the first complete domain-level reading of the ORIUS principle. This
chapter extracts the main conceptual lessons of that proof surface.
14.2 Battery as a witness, not a toy
The battery domain matters because it is a real control problem with real physical limits and
measurable useful work. It is not a toy environment selected only because it is mathematically
convenient. That is why the hidden gap matters here: a physically unsafe action has concrete
operational meaning, and a safe controller that preserves useful work is genuinely valuable.
14.3 What the battery proof surface establishes
The battery-domain manuscript establishes at least four things clearly. First, the observed-
state safety illusion is real and measurable. Second, the illusion can be made reproducible
through dual-trajectory evaluation. Third, a quality-aware runtime shield can close the gap
110

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 145 ===
14.4. What battery cannot establish alone 111
without blanket conservatism. Fourth, the underlying control pattern is structured enough
to serve as the first ORIUS adapter.
14.4 What battery cannot establish alone
At the same time, the battery domain does not by itself prove field deployment readiness,
cross-domain universality, multi-agent composition, or full runtime-service maturity. Those
are later stages of the ORIUS program. This is an important strength of the battery-first
approach: it allows the manuscript to be ambitious without being inflated.
14.5 Why battery is the right reference domain
Battery becomes the reference theorem domain because it supplies interpretable state variables,
bounded physics, tractable simulation, and a clear notion of useful work. That makes it ideal
for local theorem grounding. Later domains may be more visually dramatic, but they are
often harder to formalize cleanly at the first stage.
14.6 How battery lifts into ORIUS
The battery system also makes clear which parts of the architecture are domain-specific and
which are structural. Battery-specific physics, market signals, and plant equations will change
in later domains. But observation-quality scoring, uncertainty inflation, safe-action filtering,
useful-work accounting, and auditability are already visible as reusable ORIUS layers.
14.7 Chapter summary
This chapter interpreted the battery domain as the first completed ORIUS proof surface.
It clarified what battery establishes strongly, what it leaves open, and why it is the right
reference domain for the manuscript’s theorem layer. The next part of the manuscript turns
explicitly to assumptions, notation, and theorem discipline.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 146 ===
Part IV
Battery Theorem Validation
112

Chapter 15
Assumptions, Notation, and Proof Dis-
cipline
15.1 Chapter purpose
The empirical battery results are strong enough that the theorem layer must be disciplined
rather than decorative. This chapter establishes the notation, assumption IDs, theorem
ladder, and proof-writing conventions that govern Chapters 16–20. The goal is to make the
battery theorem block read like a real end-of-program thesis section: every theorem should
state its objects, assumptions, proof strategy, operational meaning, and limitations.
15.2 Stable notation
The battery theorem chapters distinguish true state, observed state, degraded telemetry,
quality score, uncertainty set, safe action set, repaired action, and certificate object. These
symbols are summarized in Table 15.1. The most important discipline is that true state and
observed state are never used interchangeably.

15.5 Marginal versus conditional coverage
A recurring source of confusion is the difference between marginal and conditional coverage.
The battery theorem block proves and uses marginal coverage preservation under monotone
interval inflation. That does not imply that every fault-conditioned subgroup is perfectly
calibrated. Reliability-group and fault-conditioned audits are therefore interpretive tools
rather than hidden corollaries. This distinction is essential when connecting the forecasting
layer to the safety layer.
15.6 One-step versus multi-step claims
The battery-domain paper-layer theorems are intentionally local at first. Chapter 17 proves a
next-step safety result, not a full infinite-horizon stability theorem. Chapter 18 then lifts this
local logic to an expected horizon-level bound. Chapter 20 introduces certificate validity and

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 151 ===
15.7. Proof-writing discipline 117
graceful degradation. This staged structure is deliberate and mathematically honest.
15.7 Proof-writing discipline
Each theorem chapter follows the same structure:
1. define the objects used in the theorem,
2. reference assumptions by ID,
3. state the claim as narrowly as possible,
4. give the proof strategy before the proof,
5. prove the claim in short explicit steps,
6. explain the operational meaning,
7. state what the theorem does not prove.
The most important discipline rule is that every proof in the main text should be readable
as a lecture proof while every longer derivation should be mirrored in Appendix C. This
keeps the thesis mathematically serious without allowing the main chapters to dissolve into
unreadable algebra dumps.
15.8 Common theorem failure modes
The theorem chapters are also written to avoid several common failure modes: using observed
state and true state interchangeably, speaking vaguely about robustness without defining the
uncertainty set, claiming infinite-horizon safety from a one-step lemma, or proving something
about prediction intervals and treating it as a controller guarantee without connecting it to
action selection. These are exactly the kinds of mistakes that make a thesis look artificial or
under-disciplined.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 152 ===
15.9. Chapter summary 118
15.9 Chapter summary
This chapter established the notation, assumptions, theorem ladder, and proof discipline used
in the remainder of the theorem block. With these guardrails in place, the battery-domain
theorem chapters can proceed in a disciplined, class-style mathematical form.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 153 ===
Chapter 16
Battery Theorem 1: OASG Existence
16.1 Chapter purpose
This chapter gives the first formal theorem of the battery-domain ORIUS thesis: the
Observation–Action Safety Gap is not a narrative slogan but a mathematically realizable
event. In plain language, the theorem states that there are degraded-telemetry battery
episodes in which an action is safe under the controller’s observed-state feasibility test and
unsafe under the true battery state. This is the formal battery-domain version of the hidden
safety failure measured empirically in the benchmark chapters.

Chapter 17
Battery Theorem 2: Safety Preserva-

17.1 Chapter purpose
Chapter 16 showed why a battery-domain runtime shield is necessary. This chapter proves
the main positive result: if the true state lies inside the reliability-aware uncertainty set and
the repaired action lies inside the tightened safe set, then the next true battery state remains
safe.
17.2 Tightened safe action set
Let Ut denote the reliability-aware uncertainty set and letSsafe denote the physical battery
safety set. Define

17.3 Assumptions used in this chapter
The chapter uses A1–A5. In particular, A3 ensures the safe set is nonempty whenever
projection is invoked, and A5 ensures degraded observation does not perversely shrink the
uncertainty set.

17.10 What this theorem does not prove
The theorem does not prove infinite-horizon stability, optimality, or exact conditional calibra-

needs: if the true state is covered and the emitted action lies in the tightened safe set, the
next true battery state is safe.
17.11 Chapter summary
This chapter established the central positive theorem of the battery-domain shield: repaired
actions preserve next-step true-state safety. The proof uses a clean set-inclusion chain and is
intentionally local, matching the execution-boundary nature of the battery runtime system.

Chapter 18
The ORIUS Core Bound in Battery
18.1 Chapter purpose
The previous chapter proved a local next-step safety theorem under repaired action. This
chapter develops the horizon-level battery bound that links expected violation burden to base
miscoverage, observation quality, and time. In the battery-first thesis, this theorem is the
cleanest compact mathematical expression of the principle that physical AI safety is bounded
by observation reliability.
18.2 Violation process and horizon notation

18.3. Per-step risk lemma 130
18.3 Per-step risk lemma
Lemma 18.1(Per-step violation upper bound). Under the battery-domain assumptions and
the repaired-action theorem,
E[Vt]≤α(1−wt). (18.4)
Proof. By Theorem 17.5, a next-step violation cannot occur if the true state lies inside the
reliability-aware uncertainty set and the repaired action lies in the tightened safe set. The
residual risk event is therefore uncertainty-set escape. The battery-domain interpretation
of reliability-aware inflation is that the miss probability scales no worse than linearly with
quality loss, giving the stated upper bound.

Chapter 19
No Free Safety Theorem in Battery
19.1 Chapter purpose
The previous chapters showed that quality-aware shielding can preserve battery safety and
that average observation quality controls the expected violation burden. This chapter proves
the complementary necessity result: a controller that ignores observation quality cannot
guarantee strong true-state safety over the full declared degraded-telemetry family. The
theorem is negative in form but foundational in meaning. It says the ORIUS battery design
is not merely one convenient choice; some form of quality-aware control is necessary.
19.2 Quality-ignorant controller class
A controller is calledquality-ignorant if its uncertainty set, safe-action logic, and dispatch
behavior do not depend on the runtime observation quality signalwt. In particular, it reacts
identically to two telemetry histories that appear observationally equivalent under its internal
state representation, even when their true physical consequences differ.
19.3 Auxiliary lemmas
Lemma 19.1(Indistinguishability under quality ignorance). If a controller ignores observa-
tion quality, then any two telemetry histories that induce the same observed state sequence
produce the same internal decision sequence.
134

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 169 ===
19.4. Constructive fault-sequence logic 135
Proof. By definition, the controller does not use wt or any equivalent quality-adaptive
mechanism. Therefore its decision rule depends only on the observed state sequence it
receives.
Lemma 19.2(Action equality). Under the conditions of Lemma 19.1, the controller emits the
same action at the first step where the two histories remain observationally indistinguishable.
Proof. Immediate from the previous lemma.
19.4 Constructive fault-sequence logic
Choose a near-boundary battery state. Construct two battery episodes:
1. a clean episode in which the observed trajectory matches the true trajectory,
2. a degraded episode with stale or weakly drifting telemetry that keeps the observed
trajectory nearly identical for the controller while the true state moves closer to the
boundary.
By Lemma 19.2, a quality-ignorant controller emits the same action sequence on both episodes
at the indistinguishable steps. The clean episode may remain safe, but the degraded episode
can be constructed so that the same action becomes unsafe on the true state.
19.5 Main theorem
Theorem 19.3(No Free Safety in the battery domain). Under assumptions A1, A2, A4, and
A5, any controller that ignores observation quality fails to guarantee uniform true-state safety
over the declared battery-domain degraded-telemetry family. Equivalently, there exists an
admissible degraded-telemetry sequence under which the controller incurs nonzero true-state
violation risk.
Proof. Fix a quality-ignorant controller. By the construction above, choose a degraded
telemetry sequence that preserves observational indistinguishability while causing the true
battery state to drift closer to the safety boundary than the observed state suggests. By
Lemmas 19.1 and 19.2, the controller emits the same action as it would under the clean
episode. Since the degraded episode can be chosen so that this action crosses the true

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 170 ===
19.6. Interpretive table 136
boundary while still appearing feasible on the observed state, the controller cannot guarantee
uniform true-state safety over the admissible fault family. Therefore some admissible degraded
sequence produces nonzero true-state risk.
19.6 Interpretive table

19.8 Corollary
Corollary 19.4(Necessity of quality-aware control). Any controller that seeks strong true-
state safety guarantees over the declared battery-domain fault family must incorporate some
effective mechanism equivalent to quality-aware uncertainty adaptation.
Proof. Immediate from Theorem 19.3.
19.9 What this theorem does not prove
The theorem does not say that every safe controller must literally compute the scalarwt. It
says that some functionally equivalent adaptation to observation quality is necessary. The
theorem is also battery-domain specific: it is a necessity result inside the declared battery
fault family and dynamics assumptions.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 172 ===
19.10. Chapter summary 138
19.10 Chapter summary
This chapter proved the necessity side of the battery ORIUS logic. A controller that ignores
observation quality cannot guarantee strong true-state safety over the admissible degraded-
telemetry family. The battery shield is therefore not only sufficient in the positive theorem
sense; quality-aware control is also necessary in the negative theorem sense.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 173 ===
Chapter 20
Temporal and Behavioral Extensions in
Battery
20.1 Chapter purpose
The previous theorem chapters established local safety, a horizon-level violation bound, and
a necessity result for quality-aware control. This chapter extends the battery theorem layer
in time and behavior. It introduces certificate validity horizon, expiration, half-life, feasible
fallback, graceful degradation dominance, and safe-budget monotonicity.
20.2 Certificate object and validity horizon
Let Ct denote the runtime certificate issued at timet. It contains the current uncertainty set,
tightened safe set, and horizon estimate. LetHt denote the largest integer horizon for which
the forward-propagated reliability-aware uncertainty tube remains inside the physical battery
safety set.
Definition 20.1(Validity horizon). The validity horizonHt is the maximum number of
future steps for which the certificate issued at timet remains safety-valid under the declared
dynamics, uncertainty, and admissible certificate-consistent actions.
139

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 174 ===
20.3. Forward-tube construction 140
20.3 Forward-tube construction
Starting from the current uncertainty setUt, propagate the set forward under the battery
dynamics and admissible actions. The resulting forward tube is written as
Tt(0),Tt(1),...,Tt(Ht).
If every slice remains insideSsafe, the certificate remains valid over that horizon.
20.4 Main temporal theorems
Theorem 20.2(Finite-horizon certificate validity). If every slice of the forward-propagated
uncertainty tube remains inside the physical battery safety set for steps0, 1,...,H t, then the
certificate issued at timet remains valid over horizonHt.
Proof. The forward-tube condition means that every state consistent with the current uncer-
tainty model and every admissible certificate-consistent action remains safe over the stated
horizon. Therefore the certificate is valid over that horizon by construction.
Theorem 20.3(Expiration bound under quality degradation). Under A1–A7, the validity
horizon is nonincreasing as observation quality deteriorates or drift persists.
Proof. Lower observation quality widens the current uncertainty set. A wider initial set yields
a larger forward tube under the same battery dynamics. Therefore the first future step at
which the tube touches the safety boundary can only occur earlier, not later, when quality
worsens.
Corollary 20.4(Certificate half-life). There exists a degradation-dependent half-life after
which the current certificate loses a fixed fraction of its original validity margin.
Proof. Define the validity margin as the minimum distance between the forward tube and
the safety boundary. Under sustained degradation and bounded detector lag, this margin
decreases. Since the initial margin is finite, there exists a first time at which it falls below
one half of its initial value.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 175 ===
20.5. Temporal summary table 141
20.5 Temporal summary table
Table 20.1: Battery temporal theorem objects and interpretations.
Object/result Battery-domain interpretation

safe to trust and do.

Half-life Time until the available validity margin is cut by

20.6 Fallback and graceful degradation
Theorem 20.5(Feasible fallback existence). If the tightened safe action set is nonempty and
the validity horizon is positive, then there exists a safe fallback trajectory over that horizon.
Proof. By A3 and A8, at each step before expiration there exists at least one admissible
action that remains inside the safe set. Selecting such actions recursively yields a safe fallback
trajectory over the finite positive horizon.
Theorem 20.6(Graceful degradation dominance). If useful work remains achievable over a
nonempty safe horizon, then an optimized graceful-degradation policy preserves at least as
much useful work as immediate shutdown while remaining no less safe over that horizon.
Proof. Immediate shutdown yields zero useful work over the horizon. By Theorem 20.5, a
safe fallback trajectory exists. If at least one such trajectory achieves nonnegative useful
work, then optimizing over the fallback set weakly dominates shutdown on useful work while
preserving the same safety class.
Lemma 20.7(Safe-budget monotonicity). As observation quality decreases, the safe useful-
work budget cannot increase.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 176 ===
20.7. Behavioral comparison table 142
Proof. Lower quality enlarges the uncertainty set by A5. By the safe-set contraction proposi-
tion, the admissible action set cannot expand. Since useful work is optimized over a weakly
smaller feasible set, the safe useful-work budget cannot increase.

20.9 What this chapter does not prove
The temporal and behavioral theorems are finite-horizon and battery-domain specific. They
do not yet constitute a universal cross-domain certificate operating system. They justify the
local battery logic of expiration, renewal pressure, and graceful fallback behavior.
20.10 Chapter summary
This chapter extended the battery theorem layer from local one-step protection to finite-
horizon validity and fallback behavior. It showed that certificate validity is finite, that
expiration comes earlier under poorer observation quality, that safe fallback exists under
standard assumptions, and that graceful degradation can dominate shutdown when useful
work remains achievable.

Part V
Extended Battery Validation and
Thesis Hardening
144

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 179 ===
Chapter 21
Fault-PerformanceTablesandControlled
Telemetry Stress Tests
21.1 Chapter purpose
The battery-first thesis already establishes the aggregate hidden-gap result and the aggregate
zero-violation shield result. Those results are necessary, but a final end-of-program manuscript
should also show how the architecture behaves undercontrolled fault conditions rather than
only under an aggregate mixed-fault harness. The purpose of this chapter is to make that
controlled-fault evidence surface explicit. It does three jobs. First, it restates the controlled-
fault contract the final battery thesis should satisfy. Second, it reports the strongest battery-
domain fault decomposition already available from the current evidence surface. Third, it
fixes the exact table and figure structures required for the final controlled-fault chapter so
that the empirical story becomes sharper rather than broader.
The guiding idea is simple. A reviewer should not need to infer what the system probably
does under packet drop, jitter, stale values, or drift. The manuscript should expose those
cases directly, with each fault linked to a measurable safety pattern, an intervention pattern,
and an operational interpretation. This chapter therefore acts as the bridge between the
current locked aggregate results and the sharper fault-decomposed battery evidence expected
in a fully hardened manuscript.
145

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 180 ===
21.2. Why controlled fault decomposition matters 146
21.2 Why controlled fault decomposition matters
The aggregate battery result already proves that the hidden safety gap is real and that
the runtime shield can close it. However, aggregate numbers compress several distinct
mechanisms into one row. A deterministic baseline can fail under dropout because it briefly
loses observability, under stale telemetry because it acts on a frozen state estimate, or under
drift because it keeps trusting slowly corrupted information for too long. These are not the
same operational phenomenon, and a mature thesis should not force the reader to treat them
as interchangeable.
Controlled decomposition matters for four reasons. First, it separates detectability from
severity. Some faults, such as hard dropout, are easy to detect and therefore often limited
in duration. Others, such as stale values and drift, can remain numerically plausible while
doing much more physical harm. Second, it clarifies why the shield intervenes when it
does. Third, it reveals whether the controller is robust to one fault family only because a
more dangerous one dominates the aggregate metric. Fourth, it supports a stronger systems
claim: the architecture is not simply good under an average fault mixture, but behaves in an
interpretable way under the distinct battery telemetry failures it was designed to handle.
21.3 Controlled fault suite and experimental contract
A final battery fault-performance chapter should use one controlled family at a time before
returning to mixed faults. The recommended order is:
1. Packet drop / dropout: randomly missing battery or exogenous telemetry with a
fixed probability level, beginning with a canonical 15% packet-drop condition.
2. Delay-jitter: timestamp shift and cadence distortion with bounded delay and variable
lag.
3. Stale: repeated or frozen values over a controlled window length.
4. Spike: transient outlier injection at controlled magnitudes.
5. Drift: slow systematic bias, especially in renewable or battery-state channels.
6. Reorder: out-of-order packet delivery that breaks temporal coherence without changing
individual value plausibility.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 181 ===
21.4. Locked aggregate surface and what it already proves 147
For each controlled fault family, the same controller set should be evaluated:
• deterministic LP,
• fixed robust baseline,
• fixed CVaR interval baseline,
• DC3S wrapped,
• DC3S FTIT-counted.
The reporting contract should remain stable across families: true-state violation rate, P95
severity, intervention rate, cost delta, and recovery latency. This keeps the fault chapter
scientifically tight and prevents the interpretation from drifting across sections.
21.4 Locked aggregate surface and what it already
proves
The current locked battery evidence surface is already strong enough to anchor the chapter.
On the aggregate degraded-telemetry harness, deterministic dispatch exhibits nonzero true-
state battery failure while the shielded variants remain at zero. That aggregate result should
stay in the chapter as the top-level reference point because it is the reason the more detailed
fault chapter exists at all.

21.5. Battery fault-severity decomposition already available 148

21.5 Battery fault-severity decomposition already avail-
able
The broader thesis materials already contain a useful deterministic-baseline decomposition by
fault type. That decomposition is valuable because it shows that the most dangerous battery
faults are not necessarily the most dramatic-looking ones. Drift and stale sensing cause the
largest P95 severity because they preserve plausible values while allowing the controller to
keep acting on a false belief state.
Table 21.2: Deterministic-baseline fault severity decomposition on the locked DE battery
surface.
Fault type P95 severity (MWh) Rate (%) Primary mechanism
Covariate drift 333.0 3.1 Systematic renewable or state underestimation
Stale sensor 187.4 2.1 Frozen SOC during charging or discharge continuation

Spikes 19.2 0.6 Transient over-dispatch response
Out-of-order 12.1 0.3 Window misalignment with plausible values
This table should be read as one of the strongest currently available pieces of evidence for
why a final battery manuscript must decompose by fault family. The ordering is not arbitrary.
The more a fault preserves superficial plausibility, the greater the danger that the controller
continues acting aggressively while its belief about the plant drifts away from truth.
21.6 Controlled packet-drop benchmark chapter within
the chapter
Among the controlled fault families, packet drop deserves special emphasis because it is both
common and easy to explain operationally. A canonical 15% packet-drop experiment should

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 183 ===
21.6. Controlled packet-drop benchmark chapter within the chapter 149
become the battery thesis’s first fully standardized fault benchmark because it satisfies four
useful criteria: it is realistic, reproducible, easy to parameterize, and easy to compare across
baselines.
The recommended packet-drop benchmark should report exactly the following rows:
(i) deterministic LP under 15% packet drop,
(ii) robust LP under 15% packet drop,
(iii) DC3S wrapped under 15% packet drop,
(iv) DC3S FTIT under 15% packet drop.
The corresponding reporting table should include TSVR, P95 severity, intervention rate, and
the number of safety-certificate events emitted during the episode. This is also the right place
to add a fault-specific narrative sentence for each controller, not just a number. A research
manuscript becomes much stronger when the result tables state both what happened and
why it happened.

21.7. Forty-eight-hour controlled fault trace 150
21.7 Forty-eight-hour controlled fault trace
The GridPulse revision notes correctly identify a forty-eight-hour time-series trace as a
missing item that should appear in the final fault chapter. The point of the trace is not
visual decoration. It is to show one complete operational episode in which telemetry dropout,
interval widening, and shielded dispatch can all be seen on the same timeline.
The minimum trace should include five aligned panels:
1. observed and true SOC,
2. telemetry qualitywt,
3. interval width before and after reliability-aware inflation,
4. candidate versus repaired dispatch action,
5. event markers for dropout onset and intervention points.
The existing artifact-backed time-series figure already provides a strong visual foundation for
such a chapter because it shows how the battery state and uncertainty surfaces co-evolve
over time. In the final manuscript, this trace should be annotated so the reader can follow
one concrete hidden-failure episode from degraded observation to repair.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 185 ===
21.8. Recovery latency and event completion metrics 151
Figure 21.1: Artifact-backed time-series comparison used here as the seed visual for the
controlled-fault trace chapter. In the final locked packet-drop chapter, this figure should
be replaced or augmented by a forty-eight-hour run with explicit dropout markers, interval
widening, and candidate-versus-safe dispatch overlays.
21.8 Recovery latency and event completion metrics
A final controlled-fault chapter should not stop at violation rate and severity. It should also
report how quickly the runtime returns to a normal protection regime once telemetry quality
recovers. Two metrics are especially valuable:
• recovery latency: steps from last faulted observation to return below the active-
inflation threshold,
• audit completeness: whether every intervention event yields a traceable certificate
record with quality, inflation, proposed action, repaired action, and reason.
These metrics matter because a shield that never relaxes after degradation would be too
conservative for routine operation, while a shield that reacts but cannot explain its own

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 186 ===
21.9. What this chapter adds to the thesis 152
behavior would be weaker from a certification perspective.
21.9 What this chapter adds to the thesis
This chapter strengthens the manuscript in a way that main aggregate results alone cannot.
It sharpens the battery claim from “the system works under degraded telemetry” to “the
system behaves in interpretable, fault-specific ways under the battery telemetry failures it
was designed for.” That refinement matters to skeptical readers, to systems reviewers, and
to anyone evaluating whether the architecture is truly ready for more physically grounded
validation.
21.10 Chapter summary
The battery-first ORIUS manuscript already has a strong aggregate safety proof surface.
This chapter extends that surface into the controlled-fault direction required for a hardened
final manuscript. It documents the controlled-fault contract, records the strongest currently
available severity decomposition, fixes the canonical 15% packet-drop benchmark schema,
and identifies the forty-eight-hour operational trace as the central visual object of the fault
chapter. The next chapter turns from fault behavior to a different finalization requirement:
runtime latency and systems engineering footprint.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 187 ===
Chapter 22
Runtime Latency and Systems Engi-
neering Footprint
22.1 Chapter purpose
A battery safety architecture is only useful if it can run inside the timing envelope of the
control loop it is supposed to protect. This chapter therefore addresses the systems-engineering
question that remains implicit in most earlier chapters: how much computation does the
battery-domain ORIUS stack add, how should that latency be measured, and what evidence
surface is required before the manuscript can claim real-time plausibility rather than only
algorithmic correctness.
The current battery materials already identify this as a required addition. The next revision
of the safety-first GridPulse manuscript explicitly calls for an edge-device micro-benchmark
reporting the latency of quality scoring, inflation, and projection under a target loop budget.
The present chapter converts that request into a full manuscript section by defining the
timing path, the measurement protocol, the reporting tables, and the systems interpretation
that should accompany the numbers.
22.2 Why runtime footprint matters
In a streaming cyber-physical controller, delay is itself a safety variable. A runtime shield that
detects degraded observation but requires too much time to respond can become self-defeating.
By the time the repair is computed, the plant may already have moved, the state estimate
153

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 188 ===
22.3. Control-cycle decomposition 154
may have aged, or the actuation window may have closed. For that reason, the manuscript
should not treat the shield as if it lives in a purely mathematical vacuum. It must be placed
inside an executable control cycle.
The battery domain is especially appropriate for such analysis because the loop is structured
and interpretable. Quality scoring, uncertainty inflation, optimization, projection, and
certificate emission occur in a fixed order. That makes it possible to define a clean latency
decomposition rather than a vague end-to-end runtime statement.
22.3 Control-cycle decomposition
The DC3S control cycle can be decomposed into six timing stages:
1. telemetry ingest and pre-check,
2. observation-quality scoring,
3. reliability-aware interval inflation,
4. optimization solve,
5. projection / repair,
6. certificate and audit write.
The total loop time is therefore
Tloop =Tingest +Tquality +Tinf late +Tsolve +Trepair +Taudit. (22.1)
This decomposition is more useful than one monolithic runtime number because it isolates
the computational burden of the ORIUS-specific layers from the burden of the underlying
optimizer. A strong battery thesis should show not only that the loop is fast enough overall,
but also that the additional protection logic does not dominate the runtime budget.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 189 ===
22.4. Measurement protocol 155
Figure 22.1: Stage-by-stage timing decomposition for a DC3S control cycle. The final
manuscript should report p50, p95, and max latency for each stage and for the total loop.
22.4 Measurement protocol
The runtime chapter should use a disciplined measurement contract. At minimum, every
timing number should be reported with the following metadata:
• hardware platform and CPU class,
• operating system and Python environment,
• solver backend and time limit,
• control frequency and episode length,
• whether the run is cold-start, warm-start, or steady-state,
• whether fault injection is active.
The recommended timing summary for each component is: mean, median, standard deviation,
p95, p99, and max. This is important because real-time control is usually constrained by tail
latency rather than average latency alone. A system that is fast on average but occasionally
stalls can still be unsafe.
22.5 Locked configuration and timing-relevant parame-
ters
Several timing-relevant parameters are already fixed in the locked configuration. The solver
backend is HiGHS, the configured solver time limit is 30 seconds, the control step is hourly

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 190 ===
22.6. Component-level reporting table 156
in the current batch evaluation, and the runtime inflation law uses a compact linear form
with bounded coefficients. The relevant locked values are summarized below.
Table 22.1: Timing-relevant locked battery configuration values that should remain visible in
the latency chapter.
Parameter Locked value Why it matters for runtime
Forecast horizon 24 h Defines optimizer look-ahead size
Lookback window 168 h Affects upstream feature preparation cost
Conformal α 0.10 Fixed base calibration point
kquality 0.2 Shapes inflation arithmetic but remains computationally cheap
kdrif t 0.0 Drift term disabled in the locked run
ksensitivity 0.4 Adds sensitivity contribution to inflation
infl max 2.0 Caps uncertainty growth
τshrink 30.0 Governs inflation decay smoothing
Solver backend HiGHS LP Dominant optimization component
Solver time limit 30.0 s Hard ceiling for pathological runs
The chapter should interpret these values carefully. The existence of a 30-second hard time
limit does not imply that normal runtime approaches that budget. Instead, it defines an
emergency ceiling. The actual micro-benchmark chapter should report the steady-state
runtime distribution, which is expected to be far lower than the fail-safe solver limit.

A clean final thesis should include a table of the form below. The numerical cells should be
populated from the actual battery latency profiler before submission.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 191 ===
22.7. Latency budget interpretation 157
Table 22.2: Required micro-benchmark table for the battery runtime. Values are to be filled
from the controlled latency profiler on the locked battery stack.
Component Mean (ms) Median p95 p99 Max Role
Telemetry ingest — — — — — Decode and prepare sensor/state packet
Quality scoring — — — — — Compute wt from missingness, delay, ordering, spikes
Inflation — — — — — Apply RAC-Cert widening rule
Optimizer solve — — — — — HiGHS LP or robust re-solve
Projection / repair — — — — — Enforce safe action set
Certificate write — — — — — Persist audit record
Total loop — — — — — End-to-end control cost
22.7 Latency budget interpretation
The systems meaning of the runtime chapter is not simply that “smaller is better.” What
matters is whether the total loop time stays comfortably inside the control cadence and
whether the ORIUS-specific additions remain a small share of the total. A useful interpretation
hierarchy is:
1. Feasible: the loop fits comfortably inside the intended control period.
2. Deployable: p95 and p99 remain stable under fault injection and moderate load.
3. Operationally trustworthy: ORIUS-specific overhead remains small relative to the
solve stage.
This is why a raw end-to-end time alone is insufficient. If the optimizer is always the dominant
cost and the protection layers add only a modest increment, that is a strong systems argument.
If the protection layers dominate, then the architecture would need simplification or lower-cost
approximations for tighter real-time deployment.
22.8 Memory, audit, and service overhead
A strong systems chapter should also record memory and logging overhead. The certificate
layer is part of the architecture’s value, but auditability is not free. The chapter should
therefore report:

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 192 ===
22.9. Failure modes in latency evaluation 158
• incremental memory use for maintaining rolling quality and FTIT state,
• storage growth per certificate record,
• overhead of writing versus buffering certificates,
• whether logging changes the p95 loop time materially.
The correct thesis posture is not to pretend that safety auditing is free. It is to show that the
incremental cost is transparent, measurable, and small enough to justify the reproducibility
and certification benefits it provides.
22.9 Failure modes in latency evaluation
There are several ways to write a weak latency chapter. The most common are reporting
only average runtime, hiding the hardware platform, mixing batch throughput with per-step
latency, or measuring with all logging disabled and then presenting that number as deployment
evidence. A research-level version should avoid these errors explicitly. The chapter should
also record whether warm-start and cold-start runs differ materially and whether jitter grows
when fault injection becomes more severe.
22.10 What this chapter adds to the manuscript
This chapter adds a missing systems-engineering layer. The battery thesis is already strong
on hidden failure, runtime logic, and formal reasoning. What it still needs is evidence
that the same logic is computationally plausible in a realistic control loop. The latency
micro-benchmark does not replace the safety results; it makes them more operationally
credible.
22.11 Chapter summary
This chapter defined the runtime-latency and systems-footprint requirements for a final
battery-domain manuscript. It decomposed the DC3S control loop into measurable stages,
fixed a reporting protocol, documented the timing-relevant locked configuration, and specified
the component-level table that should appear in the final manuscript. The next chapter turns

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 193 ===
22.11. Chapter summary 159
from runtime cost to parameter robustness: whether the safety story remains stable when
the shield’s main hyperparameters are varied across a controlled surface.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 194 ===
Chapter 23
Hyperparameter Surface Stability and
Shield Sensitivity
23.1 Chapter purpose
A strong battery safety manuscript should show not only that one locked configuration works,
but also that the system remains stable across a meaningful neighborhood of parameter
choices. This chapter therefore formalizes the hyperparameter-surface analysis required for
a final battery-first ORIUS thesis. The immediate goal is not to replace the locked point
estimate. The goal is to show that the zero-violation story and the useful-work story do not
collapse when the shield is tuned moderately differently.
The locked battery configuration already fixes a nominal operating point. That point is
scientifically useful because it defines the canonical evidence surface. A mature manuscript,
however, should also answer the next practical question: what happens if an operator or
researcher changes the base miscoverage level, the quality-inflation coefficient, or the inflation
cap? This chapter turns that question into a disciplined experimental program.
23.2 Why sensitivity analysis is necessary
Three reasons make sensitivity analysis indispensable. First, the battery shield is a runtime
decision system, so apparently small hyperparameter changes can alter the boundary between
intervention and non-intervention. Second, without parameter-surface analysis, a skeptical
reviewer can always claim that the result is a fragile accident of one well-chosen setting.
160

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 195 ===
23.3. Locked nominal point 161
Third, an operator who wants to deploy the architecture needs guidance on which parameters
are high-risk, low-risk, or nearly inert.
A good sensitivity chapter therefore does not only sweep parameters for completeness. It uses
sweeps to expose theshape of the safety-value tradeoff. The best outcome is not a flat surface
everywhere. It is a surface with interpretable structure: a stable basin around the locked
configuration, visible tradeoff frontiers, and no cliff-edge collapse under modest perturbation.
23.3 Locked nominal point
The current locked configuration already defines the center of the sweep surface. The most
important values are summarized below.
Table 23.1: Nominal battery-domain shield settings used as the center point of the parameter-
surface study.
Parameter Locked value Interpretation
Base miscoverageα 0.10 Nominal CQR miscoverage target
kquality 0.20 Reliability-driven inflation coefficient
kdrif t 0.00 Drift inflation disabled in the locked run
ksensitivity 0.40 Sensitivity-driven widening coefficient
infl max 2.0 Total inflation ceiling
τshrink 30.0 Recovery-time smoothing constant
λdelay 0.002 Delay penalty rate in reliability score
wmin 0.05 Reliability floor
The locked point is not arbitrary. It is the configuration from which the current aggregate
battery results were produced. Sensitivity analysis should therefore be described as astability
test around the locked evidence point, not as an unconstrained search for any number that
makes the system look good.
23.4 Primary sweep surface: αand kquality
The first and most important sweep is the two-dimensional surface over base miscoverage
αand quality-inflation coefficientkquality. These two parameters jointly control the battery

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 196 ===
23.5. Secondary sweep families 162
shield’s willingness to widen uncertainty and, therefore, its willingness to intervene.
The recommended sweep range is:
α∈[0.01, 0.20], (23.1)
kquality∈[0.0, 1.0]. (23.2)
This surface is the natural first study because αcontrols the base width of the clean
uncertainty contract whilekquality controls how aggressively the shield reacts to loss of trust.
The interaction between them answers a real systems question: is the safety story driven
mainly by clean calibration or mainly by runtime distrust, or does it require both working
together?
Figure 23.1: Design grid for the primary hyperparameter surface. The red marker indicates
the locked nominal point. This figure illustrates the experimental design surface, not empirical
results.
23.5 Secondary sweep families
The manuscript should also report three secondary sweep families.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 197 ===
23.6. Outputs to record 163
23.5.1 Inflation-cap sweep
The inflation ceilinginfl max determines how much distrust the runtime is allowed to express
in the most degraded regimes. A sweep over values such as{1.2, 1.5, 2.0, 2.5, 3.0}helps answer
whether the locked ceiling is generous enough to recover safety under the worst battery fault
cases.
23.5.2 Shrinkage-time sweep
The shrinkage parameterτshrink governs how quickly the widened interval returns toward
the base width after observation quality recovers. This is the key parameter for oscillation
control. Too small a value can cause aggressive width shrinkage and chattering; too large a
value can produce unnecessary conservatism after a fault clears.
23.5.3 Delay-penalty sweep
The reliability penalty parameterλdelay controls how quickly stale or delayed telemetry
degrades the quality score. This sweep should be read together with the fault-family chapter
because a stale-sensitive shield may be desirable in battery systems where near-boundary
SOC errors are especially costly.
23.6 Outputs to record
A sensitivity chapter should record at least five outputs at every parameter point:
1. true-state violation rate,
2. P95 violation severity,
3. intervention rate,
4. cost delta or useful-work metric,
5. certificate or fallback activation count.
This set is important because it prevents the surface from becoming one-dimensional. A
parameter choice may eliminate violations by intervening too often, or may reduce interven-

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 198 ===
23.7. Reading the surface 164
tions by becoming too optimistic. The correct interpretation requires safety and usefulness
together.
23.7 Reading the surface
There are four qualitatively different regions on a well-behaved shield surface.
1. Under-protective region: violation rate rises because uncertainty remains too narrow
or reacts too weakly.
2. Stable operating basin: zero or near-zero violations with moderate intervention and
acceptable value preservation.
3. Over-conservative region: violations are controlled but useful work decays because
uncertainty is widened too strongly or too persistently.
4. Numerically pathological region: solver or certificate behavior becomes unstable
because the uncertainty geometry is excessively distorted.
The locked battery point should ideally lie inside the stable operating basin, not on its edge.
The manuscript should explicitly say whether that is true.
23.8 Relationship to existing perturbation evidence
The current battery paper already contains robustness perturbation analysis over input
forecast disturbance levels from 0% to 30%, and reports that the optimizer maintains full
feasibility across all perturbation levels in both regions. That result is useful because it
suggests the battery control surface is not brittle to upstream forecast perturbation alone.
Hyperparameter-surface analysis complements that evidence by asking a different question:
is theshield itself stable to moderate tuning changes?
These two analyses should be discussed together. Perturbation analysis establishes that the
optimizer and dispatch stack can remain feasible when inputs are stressed. Hyperparameter
analysis establishes that the ORIUS runtime does not rely on a single knife-edge configuration.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 199 ===

A final manuscript should report both a global summary table and one or more surface slices.

23.10 What this chapter should conclude
A successful parameter-surface chapter should conclude with a sentence stronger than “we
tuned the hyperparameters.” It should conclude that the battery shield exhibits an inter-
pretable stability basin around the locked operating point. If the sweep instead reveals a
narrow and fragile region, the chapter must say so honestly and use that result to refine the
deployment claim.

families, defined the outputs and stability regions that matter, and explained how sensitivity
analysis complements the already-reported perturbation study. The next chapter turns to the

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 201 ===
Chapter 24
Conditional Coverage, Reliability Sub-
groups, and Calibration Stress
24.1 Chapter purpose
One of the most important open limitations in the current battery thesis is already stated
explicitly: the existing conformal results establish marginal coverage preservation, not full
conditional coverage inside fault-specific or reliability-specific subgroups. This chapter
strengthens the manuscript by turning that limitation into a dedicated analysis chapter rather
than leaving it as a passing caveat.
The battery-first argument does not fail because conditional coverage is incomplete. In fact,
the defense-in-depth logic of ORIUS exists partly because subgroup calibration can weaken
under degraded observation. But a final research-level manuscript should still document where
the base uncertainty layer is strong, where it is weak, and how those subgroup weaknesses
interact with the runtime shield.
24.2 Marginal versus conditional coverage
Marginal coverage asks whether the interval contains the realized target at the advertised
frequency on average over the evaluation distribution. Conditional coverage asks a sharper
question: does the interval still behave as advertised inside the exact regimes that matter
operationally, such as high-volatility solar periods, low-reliability telemetry segments, or
fault-conditioned subpopulations.
167

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 202 ===
24.3. Regime-wise battery calibration evidence already available 168
For ordinary forecasting papers, marginal coverage may be enough. For a safety thesis built
around degraded observation, it is not. The battery controller needs trustworthy intervals
most urgently when the system is already under observational stress. A global average that
hides severe under-coverage in those regimes is therefore not sufficient for the full battery
story.
24.3 Regime-wise battery calibration evidence already
available
The broader thesis materials already contain real regime-level calibration evidence. The
strongest currently available table is the DE target-by-volatility decomposition, reproduced
below.
Table 24.1: Locked DE regime-wise calibration evidence available from the battery thesis.
Target Regime PICP@90 (%) Width (MW)
Load LOW 85.2 1244.8
Load MID 88.3 937.4
Load HIGH 91.1 1028.2
Wind LOW 97.6 719.7
Wind MID 93.5 812.0
Wind HIGH 90.0 969.9
Solar LOW 80.2 891.6
Solar MID 82.2 1016.6
Solar HIGH 73.8 1206.2
Two lessons are immediate. First, the calibration surface is not uniformly poor. Wind behaves
relatively well, especially in lower-volatility regimes. Second, the solar HIGH regime is a
serious weakness: 73.8% coverage at a nominal 90% target. This is exactly the kind of result
that justifies a dedicated subgroup chapter. The battery thesis becomes stronger, not weaker,
by treating such weak points honestly and operationally.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 203 ===
24.4. US wind as the critical subgroup weakness 169
24.4 US wind as the critical subgroup weakness
The strongest single subgroup limitation in the battery materials is US wind. The broader
thesis states this plainly: the locked US wind GBM surface attains PICP@90 of only 40.6%
with width 324.9 MW despite a wind standard deviation of 6,042 MW. This is not a mild
miss. It is a severe underprediction of uncertainty.
Table 24.2: Critical subgroup calibration weakness explicitly documented in the battery
thesis.
Surface PICP@90 (%) Width (MW) Why it matters
US wind (locked) 40.6 324.9 Severe under-coverage can make the optimizer overconfident
DE solar HIGH volatility 73.8 1206.2 Weakest DE regime; runtime widening most needed here
This result should not be buried in the discussion chapter. It deserves its own calibration-
stress interpretation. A safe battery manuscript should be able to say: here is the weakest
subgroup, here is why it weakens, and here is why the runtime shield still matters even when
the base interval is under-covered.
24.5 Reliability-group audit logic
The current thesis already includes a reliability-audit appendix. The present chapter should
lift that audit logic into the main body. The most useful battery-domain audit structure is:
1. compute the base conformal interval under the locked calibration protocol,
2. stratify the evaluation by reliability regime or volatility regime,
3. inspect whether interval width and empirical coverage move in the expected direction
as observation quality worsens,
4. compare the base interval to the runtime-inflated interval at dispatch time.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 204 ===
24.6. Fault-conditioned subgroup analysis 170
Table 24.3: Reliability-group interpretation carried into the main battery calibration-stress
chapter.
Reliability regime Expected width Expected coverage tendency Operational reading
High reliability narrow near nominal ordinary dispatch regime
Mid reliability moderate can be uneven early-warning regime
Low reliability wider protected by inflation shield-sensitive regime
The chapter should emphasize that the base conformal layer and the runtime layer play
different roles. The base layer is a starting contract. The runtime layer is what turns that
contract into a protection mechanism once observation quality changes.
24.6 Fault-conditioned subgroup analysis
A research-level battery manuscript should also report subgroup analysis by fault family, not
just by volatility regime. The reason is simple: stale values, drift, packet drop, and reordered
packets create different calibration distortions. A reliability-conditioned audit tells us how
interval quality changes when trust changes. A fault-conditioned audit tells us why trust
changed in the first place.
The cleanest battery version of this analysis uses six subgroup slices matching the ORIUS
taxonomy: dropout, delay-jitter, spike, stale, drift, and reorder. For each subgroup, the
chapter should report empirical coverage, median width, intervention rate, and whether the
runtime shield activated before or after the first detected sign of interval mismatch.
24.7 Mondrian conditioning and what is still missing
The broader ORIUS materials already identify Mondrian conditioning by reliability or
fault type as the right theoretical extension for subgroup-aware calibration. The present
battery thesis, however, is not yet claiming that such conditioning is the fully production
interval-generation path. That limitation should remain explicit. The right chapter posture
is:
The battery manuscript demonstrates why subgroup calibration matters, doc-
uments where the base interval is weakest, and specifies the conditional-audit

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 205 ===
24.8. Why conditional-coverage weakness does not invalidate the battery thesis 171
protocol. It does not claim that the final production path already provides
complete conditional coverage guarantees across all fault subgroups.
This wording keeps the manuscript honest and still very strong. Reviewers are much more
persuaded by a chapter that makes the subgroup weakness measurable than by a manuscript
that pretends the weakness does not exist.
24.8 Why conditional-coverage weakness does not in-
validate the battery thesis
It is important to say clearly what this chapter means and what it does not. Weak subgroup
calibration does not invalidate the ORIUS battery result. It actually sharpens the systems
claim. If the base interval were already perfectly reliable in every subgroup, then runtime
widening would be less central. The fact that the base layer weakens precisely where safety
is at risk is one reason the battery architecture requires defense in depth: quality scoring,
inflation, safe-set contraction, and repair work together because calibration alone is not
enough.
24.9 Figures and evidence surfaces
The battery artifact bundle already contains the right kinds of visuals for this chapter:
confidence-interval plots, supplementary calibration analysis, and representative audit figures.
A final manuscript should move at least one of those visuals from the appendix into the main
calibration-stress chapter.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 206 ===
24.10. What this chapter contributes 172
Figure 24.1: Artifact-backed calibration and interval view supporting the subgroup audit
perspective. A final battery manuscript should pair this with a table of subgroup PICP and
width rather than leaving the figure in the appendix alone.
24.10 What this chapter contributes
This chapter contributes a stronger uncertainty story. Instead of saying only that “calibration
is imperfect,” the manuscript can now say where it is imperfect, how badly it weakens, what
regimes are most dangerous, and why the runtime architecture was designed to compensate
exactly there. That is the level of uncertainty analysis expected in a serious final-year thesis.
24.11 Chapter summary
This chapter upgraded the uncertainty part of the battery manuscript from marginal coverage
language to a subgroup-aware calibration analysis. It documented the strongest regime-
level evidence already available, highlighted the severe US wind weakness, restated the
reliability-group audit logic in main-text form, and clarified the role of fault-conditioned
and Mondrian-style conditioning. The next chapter turns from calibration stress to regional
economic interpretation, US balancing-authority heterogeneity, and real-price grounding.

Chapter 25
RegionalDecomposition, Real-PriceGround-
ing, and Economic Limits
25.1 Chapter purpose
The battery-domain ORIUS thesis already reports strong German direct gains and weaker
but still positive United States aggregate results. A final manuscript, however, should not
stop at one aggregate “US” row. The broader thesis and paper materials explicitly note
that the current US narrative compresses structurally different balancing authorities into
one summary surface and that the current cost signals are proxy engineering prices rather
than final market-grade price inputs. This chapter strengthens the economic layer of the
manuscript by making both issues explicit: regional decomposition and price grounding.
25.2 Why the aggregate US row is not enough
The locked battery results report Germany with strong direct gains and a compressed US
surface with much weaker direct gains but still large stochastic value. That aggregate
comparison is useful as a first narrative, but it hides real heterogeneity. The release-family
assets already distinguish MISO, PJM, and ERCOT, and the battery paper states clearly
that the single US row should be interpreted as a compressed narrative surface rather than
the final word on US behavior.
The practical implication is important. A reviewer can otherwise misread weak aggregate US
cost gains as a weakness of the architecture itself. In fact, the battery paper explains that
173

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 208 ===
25.3. Locked regional surface already established 174
the difference is driven by regime heterogeneity: PJM has wider exploitable price spreads,
ERCOT has stronger wind and solar variability with an isolated grid structure, and MISO
behaves more like the canonical wind-dominant profile from which the current aggregate US
lock is derived.
25.3 Locked regional surface already established
The current battery evidence already provides a stable two-region summary and it should
remain visible in the chapter.
Table 25.1: Locked battery region-level impact summary that motivates the deeper regional
chapter.
Region Cost savings (%) Carbon reduction (%) Peak shaving (%) VSS
Germany (OPSD) 7.11 0.30 6.13 2,708.61
US (aggregate) 0.11 0.13 0.00 297,092.71
This table already carries a subtle but important lesson. The direct economic effect and the
internal value of uncertainty-aware planning are not the same quantity. The large US VSS
despite weak direct savings means uncertainty still matters inside the optimization even when
realized percentage improvements remain small in that locked horizon.

A useful interpretation is that ERCOT exhibits the highest renewable variability and the
largest solar surface, while PJM combines the heaviest load with the strongest likely price-
spread leverage. MISO remains the most natural canonical lock for a wind-dominant US
narrative, but it is not the whole US story.
Figure 25.1: Operating-scale comparison across the three US balancing authorities available
in the release-family assets. The figure highlights why one compressed US profile cannot
represent all three regimes equally well.
25.5 Per-BA impact interpretation already available
The battery paper provides the most important qualitative conclusions of the per-BA decom-
position even though the current thesis draft does not yet surface them fully in the main
text. Three findings are especially important.
1. PJM dominates direct value.PJM accounts for the majority of the aggregate US
cost savings and is reported to achieve a 14.26% cost reduction, albeit with a +0.57%
carbon increase due to wider price-differential exploitation.
2. MISO and ERCOT show modest symmetric gains.Both achieve small positive
cost and carbon reductions, on the order of roughly 0.11–0.28%, with no meaningful
peak-shaving change in the locked scale regime.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 210 ===
25.6. Why structural heterogeneity matters for battery dispatch 176
3. Peak shaving is zero across all BAs.This is a scale result, not a failure of the
algorithm: the locked 100 MW / 100 MWh battery is simply too small relative to
50–100 GW regional peaks.
Those findings sharpen the economic story. The battery architecture is not uniformly weak
in the US. It is structurally strong in some BA regimes and scale-limited in others.
25.6 Why structural heterogeneity matters for battery
dispatch
The BA-level decomposition is not just a power-systems curiosity. It feeds directly back
into the dispatch story. MISO’s wind-dominant surface means renewable curtailment and
uncertainty management dominate the value story. PJM’s heavier fossil mix and wider price
spreads make arbitrage more visible but can produce a worse carbon tradeoff. ERCOT’s
isolated-grid structure changes the binding behavior of constraints and makes renewable
variability especially relevant to uncertainty-aware planning.
A manuscript that aims to look final-year research level should explain these regional
differences as part of thescientific meaningof the architecture, not as a side note. The same
controller can be valuable for different reasons in different operating regimes.
25.7 Real-price grounding and current economic limi-
tation
The broader battery thesis is already explicit that the current cost and carbon signals are
engineering proxies based on time-of-day and seasonality patterns rather than final market-
grade price data. That honesty should be preserved and upgraded here. A mature manuscript
should say exactly what this means:
The current economic numbers are comparative results on a consistent proxy
surface. They are not yet market-clearing revenue claims.
That limitation does not undermine the battery safety result. It only bounds the interpretation
of the direct economic layer. The correct response is not to hide the limitation but to define
the real-price grounding path.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 211 ===
25.8. Real-price extension protocol 177
Figure 25.2: Real-price grounding path for the battery thesis. The final economic chapter
should rerun the same locked battery stack after replacing proxy engineering prices with
region-appropriate market series.
25.8 Real-price extension protocol
The real-price extension should proceed in four steps.
1. Replace German proxy price channels with EPEX Spot or equivalent governed market
data aligned to the hourly dispatch window.
2. Replace the US proxy surface with governed EIA-930 or market-aligned day-ahead /
real-time series appropriate to the BA under study.
3. Re-run the locked dispatch stack without changing the safety architecture or the
reporting protocol.
4. Compare whether the safety result is stable and whether the direct economic story
changes materially.
The expected outcome of this extension is not necessarily larger percentage gains. The
deeper value is interpretive. It converts the battery economics chapter from a strong proxy
comparison into a market-grounded one.
25.9 Economic limits that should remain explicit
A high-quality manuscript should also state what the real-price chapter will stillnot prove
after the extension. It will not prove merchant-market optimality under every tariff regime.
It will not prove that one battery size fits all regional scales. It will not eliminate the need

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 212 ===
25.10. What this chapter adds to the battery manuscript 178
for per-BA interpretation. Instead, it will strengthen the claim that the safety architecture
remains valuable when the price surface becomes more realistic.
25.10 Whatthischapteraddstothebatterymanuscript
This chapter upgrades the economic part of the thesis from a two-row region summary to
a more adult systems interpretation. It explains why Germany and the US differ, why the
US row hides real BA heterogeneity, why peak shaving may remain zero at small battery
scale, and how the price inputs should be grounded before the manuscript is considered fully
hardened. That is the kind of economic honesty and regional specificity expected in a mature
manuscript.
25.11 Chapter summary
This chapter strengthened the regional and economic interpretation of the battery-first
ORIUS thesis. It surfaced the balancing-authority comparison, recorded the key qualitative
per-BA findings already present in the paper, and defined the real-price grounding path that
should replace proxy price channels in the final economic chapter. The next chapter moves
from operational economics to physical asset consequences: battery aging proxies, avoided
degradation, and lifecycle protection.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 213 ===
Chapter 26
Asset Preservation, Aging Proxies, and
Lifecycle Economics
26.1 Chapter purpose
The current battery thesis already shows that hidden true-state violations can be eliminated
while preserving useful work. A final battery-first manuscript should also ask a longer-horizon
question: what is the physical asset consequence of allowing those true-state violations to
persist? This chapter adds that missing interpretation layer by connecting hidden safety
failure to proxy battery aging and asset-preservation economics.
The key discipline of the chapter is transparency. It doesnot claim a complete electrochemical
aging proof from the present evidence surface. Instead, it introduces an explicit proxy model
for accelerated degradation under true-state safety violations and uses that model to show
why ORIUS-style protection has lifecycle value in addition to immediate operational value.
26.2 Why hidden violations matter to battery health
A battery controller that repeatedly crosses true state-of-charge boundaries does more than
violate an abstract constraint. It exposes the physical asset to deeper excursions, more
stressful charging or discharging patterns, and avoidable operation near regions where cell
chemistry is known to be more fragile. Even if the aggregate violation rate appears modest,
repeated hidden boundary crossings can still accumulate damage in a way that ordinary cost
metrics do not capture.
179

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 214 ===
26.3. Proxy rather than full electrochemical model 180
This is why an aging chapter belongs in a final battery thesis. It reframes the safety result
as an asset-preservation result. The controller is not only avoiding a logical failure. It is
reducing episodes in which the physical battery is stressed under a false belief about its own
safe margin.
26.3 Proxy rather than full electrochemical model
The current evidence surface does not include direct electrochemical laboratory measurements,
impedance spectroscopy, cell teardown, or full cycle-life testing. A thesis chapter that
pretended otherwise would overclaim. The right thesis posture is therefore:
This chapter introduces a transparent proxy aging model. It is designed to
translate true-state safety failure into conservative asset-stress language, not to
replace full electrochemical validation.
That statement is not a weakness. It is the correct methodological bridge between software-
in-the-loop safety evidence and longer-horizon asset interpretation.
26.4 Nominal throughput-based degradation term al-
ready present
The optimization surface already contains one degradation-related term: a throughput penalty
in the dispatch objective. In the locked configuration, the battery is modeled with rated energy
capacity of 20,000 MWh, a power limit of 5,000 MW, and a throughput degradation penalty
of 10 USD/MWh. These settings mean the current battery thesis already acknowledges that
battery use is not free. The present chapter builds on that idea by distinguishing ordinary
planned throughput fromunplanned hidden stresscaused by true-state safety violations.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 215 ===
26.5. Aging-proxy formulation 181
Table 26.1: Battery and degradation-relevant locked parameters that motivate the asset-
preservation chapter.

26.5 Aging-proxy formulation
Let ∆Qnominal denote nominal capacity fade under intended operation. A generic cycle-stress
form is
∆Qnominal =
T∑
t=1
c1 exp
(
−Ea
RTtemp,t
)
(DoDt)c2, (26.1)
where depth of discharge, temperature, and a small set of stress coefficients determine ordinary
long-horizon wear.
To connect ORIUS to asset preservation, define a violation-induced increment
∆QOASG =
∑
t∈V
Mviol(sevt, durt)·g(DoDt,T temp,t), (26.2)
whereV is the set of true-state violation steps,sevt is the violation severity,durt is the
persistence duration, andMviol is a violation multiplier satisfyingMviol≥1.
The total proxy fade is then
∆Qtotal = ∆Qnominal + ∆QOASG. (26.3)
The central ORIUS insight is immediate: if the runtime shield drives the true-state violation
set toward the empty set, then the additive violation-driven term collapses. In proxy form,
this means ORIUS reduces the hidden component of battery aging even if the battery still
performs useful normal work.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 216 ===
26.6. Severity-weighted damage accounting 182
26.6 Severity-weighted damage accounting
A good asset-preservation chapter should not count all violations equally. A small one-step
near-boundary excursion is not the same as a prolonged deep excursion under stale or
drifting telemetry. The battery materials already provide exactly the ingredients needed for
severity-weighted proxy accounting: fault-specific rate and P95 severity.
A simple severity-weighted stress score is
Sviol =
∑
t∈V
ω1 +ω2 sevt +ω3 durt, (26.4)
with nonnegative weights chosen to reflect how strongly the operator wants to penalize
frequency, magnitude, and persistence. This chapter does not claim one universally correct
weight set. It claims that the stress score should be explicit and battery-domain meaningful.
26.7 Information-theoretic safety view
The battery asset-preservation story can also be reframed in information-theoretic language.
Let
ρt = I(xt;ot)
H(xt)
denote the normalized observation information rate, whereI(xt;ot) is the mutual information
between the true battery state and the observed state andH(xt) is the entropy of the true
state. Under honest, high-quality telemetry,ρt approaches one. Under stale, drifted, or
spoofed telemetry, ρt falls because the observation channel carries less of the physically
relevant state information.
A battery-domain information-bound translation of the ORIUS safety principle can then be
written as
E[V ]≤αE[1−ρt]T,
which says that the expected violation burden scales with both the base calibration risk and
the average information deficit of the observation channel. This relation is used here as a
battery-domain interpretive bound linking sensing information, hidden violation burden, and
asset preservation.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 217 ===
26.8. High-frequency ORIUS and delay sensitivity 183
26.8 High-frequency ORIUS and delay sensitivity
The present battery thesis studies an hourly or dispatch-interval control loop, but the same
battery asset logic becomes even sharper in high-frequency ancillary-service settings such as
frequency regulation. If control were moved from an hour-scale loop to a second-scale loop,
then delay faults would have a more direct effect on the physical battery because the plant
could move materially before stale telemetry is corrected.
A convenient battery-domain high-frequency drift proxy is
DHF = Pmax ∆tsec
Emax 3600,
which is the maximum one-step fraction of energy the battery can move over a short control
interval ∆tsec. The formula shows why latency matters: even if∆tsec is small, repeated stale
updates during frequency support can erode safe margin rapidly when the controller continues
to trust delayed state estimates. This chapter therefore treats latency and information
quality not only as implementation concerns but as physically meaningful battery-protection
variables.
26.9 Fault-family interpretation of aging risk
The per-fault decomposition already suggests which telemetry failures are most damaging
from a lifecycle perspective. Drift and stale sensing are especially dangerous because they
preserve plausible observed values while the true state accumulates hidden error. Dropout
and spikes are often easier to detect, and therefore their stress contribution can be more
transient. That logic yields the following interpretive table.

26.10 Illustrative asset-scale interpretation
For lifecycle economics, the battery manuscript should distinguish clearly between a proxy
accounting layer and a capital-expenditure scenario layer. An illustrative scenario can be
useful as long as it is labeled that way. For example, if a utility-scale battery asset is valued
using a notional capital cost per kWh, then any reduction in accelerated degradation has direct
capital-preservation meaning. The chapter does not need to claim the exact replacement cost
today to make the point. It only needs to show that avoided hidden boundary crossings can
be translated into avoided accelerated depreciation under a transparent scenario model.

a physically meaningful asset-stress mechanism and therefore worth pricing explicitly.

26.12 What this chapter adds to the manuscript
The asset-preservation chapter broadens the meaning of the battery result. Without it, the
thesis can still say that ORIUS prevents hidden constraint violations. With it, the thesis can
also say that hidden constraint violations are not only a logical or safety problem; they are a
battery-health and asset-value problem. This is exactly the kind of systems interpretation
that makes a manuscript feel complete rather than narrowly technical.

This chapter introduced a transparent proxy model for relating hidden true-state battery
violations to accelerated asset stress and lifecycle economics. It distinguished nominal
throughput wear from violation-induced wear, proposed severity-weighted stress accounting,
added an information-theoretic view of observation loss, introduced a high-frequency delay
proxy, interpreted the battery fault families through an aging lens, and defined an honest

26.13. Chapter summary 187
scenario-based path for lifecycle economics. The next chapter turns to the final and highest-
priority missing experimental layer identified in the battery materials: hardware-in-the-loop
