=== PHYSICAL PAGE 222 ===
Chapter 27
Hardware-in-the-Loop Validation Ar-
chitecture and Protocol
27.1 Chapter purpose
The broader ORIUS thesis is explicit that the current battery evidence is software-in-the-loop
only and that hardware-in-the-loop validation is the highest-priority future experimental
direction. This chapter turns that limitation into a concrete validation architecture. Its goal
is not to pretend that hardware results already exist when they do not. Its goal is to specify
the exact HIL chapter a final battery manuscript should execute and report.
A strong battery HIL chapter should answer three questions. First, can the same runtime
logic that closes the hidden gap in simulation still behave correctly when real sensing latency,
conversion noise, and hardware interfaces are present? Second, can the system detect degraded
telemetry quickly enough to block unsafe physical commands before they reach the battery
or emulator? Third, what does the HIL evidence prove, and what would it still leave open
after successful execution?
27.2 Why HIL is necessary
The battery-domain ORIUS manuscript already proves the hidden safety gap in simulation
and closes it with a runtime shield under a governed benchmark. That is a major result.
However, the current broader thesis also states plainly that all results remain software-in-the-
loop and that physical battery inverter and BMS integration have not been performed. A
188

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 223 ===
27.3. Safe scope of the HIL chapter 189
final manuscript needs to resolve that boundary directly. HIL is the first credible step because
it introduces physical timing, noisy sensing, and actuation interfaces without requiring a
dangerous or industrial-scale deployment.
The conceptual reason HIL matters is that simulation can hide two classes of difficulty:
1. interface realism: sensor acquisition, serial buffering, timestamping, and actuation
gating all behave less cleanly in hardware than in a software-only benchmark;
2. timing realism: physical loops can expose control-latency and synchronization prob-
lems that are invisible in a purely offline run.
27.3 Safe scope of the HIL chapter
A manuscript HIL chapter should be designed forsafe validation, not for hazardous drama.
The right objective is not “show the battery almost catches fire and ORIUS saves it.” The
right objective is: under controlled telemetry degradation on a protected low-energy bench
setup, the runtime detects loss of trust, widens uncertainty, and blocks a physically unsafe
command before the protected plant crosses the declared operating envelope.
For that reason, this chapter adopts a two-tier validation philosophy:
• Tier 1: battery emulator or highly protected test cell with hard current and voltage
interlocks,
• Tier 2: if needed later, a more realistic pack/BMS integration study under the same
safety gate logic.
The manuscript should treat Tier 1 as the required milestone. Tier 2 can remain later work
if the first HIL proof is strong and safe.
27.4 Recommended hardware architecture
The HIL setup should be intentionally modest but scientifically meaningful. A clean architec-
ture consists of:
1. a protected battery emulator or protected low-energy Li-ion test cell,

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 224 ===
27.5. Bill of materials and safety interlocks 190
2. a measurement interface for voltage and current,
3. a microcontroller or serial bridge that exposes telemetry to the Python runtime,
4. an actuation gate such as a relay or charger-enable signal,
5. the DC3S runtime on a host machine,
6. a fault injector that acts on telemetry messages rather than on the physical cell itself.
Figure 27.1: Safe HIL architecture for a battery-first ORIUS validation chapter. The fault
injector acts at the telemetry bridge, while hardware interlocks and the runtime shield block
unsafe actuation before the plant reaches hazardous conditions.
27.5 Bill of materials and safety interlocks
The HIL chapter should include a table that is explicit about what is required and what each
component proves. An example manuscript-grade bill of materials is shown below.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 225 ===

The manuscript should explicitly state that independent hardware interlocks are mandatory.
The point of HIL is to validate the controller, not to rely on it as the only layer of protection
during an experiment.
27.6 Telemetry fault injection in HIL
The cleanest HIL validation acts on the telemetry path, not the battery chemistry. That
means the experiment should inject dropout, stale values, delay, or packet disorderinto the
observed signal streamwhile leaving the protected physical plant and hardware interlocks
intact.
A recommended first battery HIL matrix is:
1. controlled dropout on the SOC or sensor stream,
2. stale-value replay over a short window,
3. bounded delay injection,

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 226 ===
27.7. Control-loop and timing measurements 192
4. mixed dropout-plus-delay stress episode.
This matrix mirrors the software benchmark logic and therefore keeps the HIL chapter aligned
with the rest of the manuscript. It is also scientifically cleaner: the thesis is validating the
observation-to-action safety story, not attempting to create unsafe battery chemistry in the
lab.
27.7 Control-loop and timing measurements
The HIL chapter should report more than “the relay turned off.” It should measure the
end-to-end timing path:
THIL =Tsense +Ttransport +Tquality +Tinf late +Tsolve/repair +Tactuate. (27.1)
The chapter should then identify the first moment at which the runtime recognized degraded
trust, the first moment at which the repaired action differed from the candidate, and the
first moment at which the physical actuator gate changed state. A well-written HIL chapter
becomes persuasive when those timestamps are shown clearly.
27.8 Required figures and tables
A manuscript-grade HIL chapter should include at least the following:
1. an architecture diagram of the physical loop,
2. a bill-of-materials table,
3. a timing table with sensor read, runtime, and actuation delay,
4. a telemetry fault trace showing the injected degradation,
5. a physical-state plot showing that the protected battery or emulator remained inside
the declared safe envelope.
A useful HIL result table template is shown below.

27.9 Acceptance criteria
The HIL chapter should define passing criteriabefore the experiment is run. Good criteria
include:
• no physical envelope violation on the protected plant,
• correct certificate and intervention logging for each injected fault episode,
• bounded detection-to-actuation response time,
• no uncontrolled oscillation after recovery,
• replayability of the experiment using the same hardware and fault script.
These criteria are important because they keep the HIL chapter focused on evidence rather
than narrative drama.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 228 ===
27.10. What a successful HIL chapter would prove 194
27.10 What a successful HIL chapter would prove
A successful HIL chapter would prove something precise and valuable: the battery-domain
ORIUS runtime survives contact with real sensing, actuation, and communication layers well
enough to preserve physical safety on a protected bench setup. That is a stronger claim than
software-only correctness and a necessary bridge toward any later field-facing argument.
27.11 What even a successful HIL chapter would not
prove
A successful HIL chapter would still not prove full utility-scale deployment, pack-level thermal
safety under all conditions, or industrial commissioning readiness. It would not eliminate the
need for larger pack integration, inverter coordination, or long-duration endurance studies.
That limit should remain explicit. The point of HIL is to remove the strongest experimental
objection to a software-only manuscript, not to claim final deployment completion.
27.12 Chapter summary
This chapter converts the battery thesis’s highest-priority missing experimental direction
into a concrete, safe, and thesis-ready validation protocol. It defines the architecture, bill of
materials, telemetry fault matrix, timing measurements, reporting tables, and passing criteria
for a battery hardware-in-the-loop chapter. With this chapter specified, the manuscript
can state clearly what remains to be executed experimentally and exactly how it should be
executed without weakening the scientific integrity of the work.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 229 ===
Part VI
Battery-Exclusive Advanced
Extensions
195

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 230 ===
Chapter 28
CertificateHalf-LifeUnderBatterySCADA
Blackout
28.1 Chapter purpose
The previous chapters established battery-domain local safety, runtime repair, controlled fault
evaluation, latency, hyperparameter stability, subgroup calibration, regional economics, asset-
preservation proxies, and hardware-in-the-loop design. This chapter deepens the temporal
theorem layer by treating one of the most operationally important battery failure modes
directly: prolonged SCADA blackout. The central idea is that the runtime certificate carried
by the controller does not fail all at once. It decays over time as uncertainty about the
true battery state grows. The chapter therefore introduces and analyzes the battery-domain
notion ofcertificate half-life: the time required for the available safety margin to lose half of
its trust-adjusted usable value under degraded observation.
The battery-first motivation is straightforward. A utility-scale battery can continue to operate
for a short period after telemetry becomes stale, but not indefinitely. If the controller knows
only the last trusted state and a bounded rate at which the true state may drift, then the
admissible control horizon is finite. The certificate half-life expresses this operational fact in
a form that both theory and operators can use. It answers the practical question:how long
can the controller continue to act safely before its last trusted state becomes too old to support
aggressive dispatch?
This chapter is written as a battery-exclusive theorem-and-systems chapter. It does not
claim that the full universal ORIUS half-life program is already validated across all domains.
196

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 231 ===
28.2. Battery blackout scenario 197
Instead, it shows how the half-life idea is completely meaningful and manuscript-worthy in
the battery domain alone. That is sufficient for the present monograph because the broader
thesis repeatedly states that battery is the completed reference domain while additional
domains remain future work.
28.2 Battery blackout scenario
Consider a battery that is discharging at 50 MW when the SCADA link becomes stale.
The last certified true state estimate places the battery at 70% SOC. The lower physical
safety boundary is 20% SOC. In a software-only optimizer, the natural temptation is to
continue discharging according to the last valid optimization result until the link recovers.
The hidden danger is that the plant continues evolving even while telemetry does not. If no
mechanism discounts the aging certificate, the controller may continue to issue apparently
rational commands long after the state estimate has become physically obsolete.
The battery-domain half-life chapter therefore begins from the following thought experiment.
Let the controller know:
1. the last trusted state estimate and its safe margin to the nearest SOC boundary,
2. a bounded physical drift rate, derived from battery power, capacity, and time discretiza-
tion,
3. the nominal miscoverage targetα,
4. the observation quality scorewt that collapses during a blackout.
Then the controller can compute a finite validity horizon and a half-life measure for the
surviving certificate. In other words, the blackout is not only a fault event. It is a countdown
problem.
28.3 State margin and drift model
Let the battery safety set be
Ssafe ={x :SOC min≤SOC (x)≤SOC max}.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 232 ===
28.3. State margin and drift model 198
Assume the last trusted time ist, and define the remaining margin to the nearest boundary
as
mt = min{SOC t−SOC min, SOCmax−SOC t}.
If the battery is in discharge mode near the lower boundary, the relevant margin isSOC t−
SOC min. If it is in charge mode near the upper boundary, the relevant margin isSOC max−
SOC t. This scalar margin is useful because it collapses the local safe room of the battery to
a single operational quantity.
Now define a conservative one-step drift bound
D = Pmax ∆t
Emax
,
where Pmax is the maximum power magnitude,Emax is rated energy capacity, and∆t is the
control interval in hours. This is the largest fraction of rated energy that can be moved in a
single step under maximum charge or discharge. Under degraded observation, the effective
uncertainty growth should be discounted by both the base miscoverage target and the loss of
observation quality. A simple battery-domain reachable error envelope is therefore
εt+h≤hDα(1−wt),
which expresses the idea that uncertainty expands over time faster when quality is poor.
Table 28.1: Battery blackout quantities used in the half-life chapter.
Symbol Meaning Battery interpretation
mt distance to nearest safety boundary SOC room before violation
D one-step physical drift bound worst-case SOC movement per step
α base miscoverage nominal interval risk level
wt observation quality trust in live telemetry
Ht validity horizon safe number of future steps
τ1/2 half-life time to lose half certificate margin

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 233 ===
28.4. Validity horizon 199
28.4 Validity horizon
The most direct temporal object is the certificate validity horizon. Define
Ht =
⌊
mt
Dα(1−wt)
⌋
.
This quantity measures how many future control steps can be certified if the controller
assumes that uncertainty expands at rateDα(1−wt) and the available margin ismt. The
floor reflects the discrete control loop.
The expression has the correct monotonicity properties for a battery controller:
1. if the marginmt increases, the horizon increases;
2. if the plant can drift faster, the horizon decreases;
3. if the miscoverage tolerance tightens, the horizon decreases;
4. if observation quality worsens, the horizon decreases.
Those monotonicities are valuable because they make the half-life chapter operational rather
than symbolic. Operators can understand them immediately.
Proposition 28.1(Validity-horizon monotonicity). Under fixed battery capacity and control
interval, the battery certificate horizonHt is nondecreasing in marginmt and nonincreasing
in drift rateD, miscoverageα, and quality loss(1−wt).
Proof. The expression forHt is the floor of the rational functionmt/(Dα(1−wt)). For
positive denominator, increasing the numerator cannot decrease the quotient, while increasing
any denominator factor cannot increase it. The floor operator preserves these monotonicities
in the weak sense.
28.5 Certificate half-life
The half-life of the certificate asks a slightly different question from the validity horizon.
Instead of asking when the certificate becomes fully unusable, it asks when the certificate

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 234 ===
28.6. Twelve-, twenty-four-, and forty-eight-hour blackout protocol 200
loses half of its effective safety room. Let the quality process decay over time at rate˙w <0.
A simplified battery-domain half-life expression is
τ1/2 = 1−√wt
α|˙w|,
for a class of linearized degradation models in which usable trust decays with the square-root
geometry of margin loss. This expression is not meant as the only possible half-life model;
rather, it is a physically interpretable proxy connecting quality decay, miscoverage, and the
speed of certificate erosion.
A battery manuscript benefits from explicitly distinguishing the horizon and the half-life:
• the horizon answers “when is the certificate exhausted?”
• the half-life answers “how fast is the certificate eroding before exhaustion?”
This distinction is useful in operations because a controller need not wait for total exhaustion
before transitioning to graceful degradation. A sharp fall in certificate health can justify
early landing behavior even beforeHt = 0.
28.6 Twelve-, twenty-four-, and forty-eight-hour black-
out protocol
A strong battery-only half-life chapter should report blackout experiments at three scales: 12
hours, 24 hours, and 48 hours. The reason is not arbitrary. These windows correspond to
operationally distinct regimes:
1. a 12-hour blackouttests whether the system can survive a long but still sub-day
telemetry interruption;
2. a 24-hour blackouttests whether a full day without trustworthy telemetry exhausts
the certificate under typical dispatch conditions;
3. a 48-hour blackouttests whether the controller can gracefully degrade into a durable
safe mode rather than oscillating or continuing blind arbitrage.
For each blackout duration, the thesis should record:

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 235 ===
28.6. Twelve-, twenty-four-, and forty-eight-hour blackout protocol 201
• certificate horizonHt over time,
• half-life trajectory,
• uncertainty-set width,
• SOC margin to boundaries,
• dispatch mode (normal, reduced, fallback, disconnected),
• true-state violation rate,
• useful work preserved relative to an ideal-telemetry reference.
These are exactly the variables needed to convert a theory chapter into a utility-operator-
readable battery chapter.

this panel should be replaced or supplemented with a 12h/24h/48h certificate-decay trace
showing SOC, observation quality, and uncertainty expansion under prolonged stale telemetry.

28.7. Theorem: finite-horizon validity under blackout 202
28.7 Theorem: finite-horizon validity under blackout
Theorem 28.2(Battery certificate validity under SCADA blackout). Assume A1–A5 from
the battery theorem chapters, and suppose the battery enters a blackout at timet with margin
mt > 0. If uncertainty expands no faster thanDα(1−wt) per step, then there exists a finite
horizon
Ht =
⌊
mt
Dα(1−wt)
⌋
such that any control sequence remaining within the tightened safe action set for allh≤Ht
preserves true-state safety, while no such guarantee can be asserted uniformly forh > Ht
without additional information.
Proof. By construction, the maximum cumulative uncertainty growth overh steps is bounded
byhDα(1−wt). For allh≤Ht, this bound does not exceed the available boundary marginmt,
so the tightened safe set remains nonempty and one-step repaired safety can be propagated
recursively. Onceh>H t, the bound can exceed the initial margin, and the prior certificate
alone is insufficient to guarantee that the true state remains within the safe set. The theorem
is therefore finite-horizon rather than asymptotic.
28.8 Operational interpretation
This theorem has a direct operator interpretation: a battery certificate should not be treated
as immortal. Once telemetry goes stale, the certificate has a countdown value. A controller
that ignores this countdown eventually becomes a quality-ignorant controller, even if it began
from a safe and well-calibrated state. For a battery operator, that means blackout handling
must be explicit in the runtime design rather than hidden inside generic retry logic.

under blackout rather than relying on an undefined notion of “degraded but still probably fine”
operation. That is already a meaningful long-form contribution in one completed domain.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 239 ===
Chapter 29
Graceful Degradation and Safe Battery
Landing
29.1 Chapter purpose
Once the certificate half-life chapter makes clear that battery trust decays over time under a
blackout, a natural control question follows:what should the battery do with its remaining
valid horizon? This chapter answers that question by developing a battery-domain graceful
degradation policy. The key idea is that the battery should not continue arbitrage blindly
until its certificate expires, nor should it always shut down immediately at the first sign of
degraded sensing. Instead, it should use the remaining trusted horizon to move itself toward
a minimum-risk operating region and then hand off to a conservative fallback mode.
This battery-domain chapter treats graceful degradation as asafe-landing problem. A utility-
scale battery typically has a central SOC region that is further from both overcharge and
deep-discharge boundaries than the extremes. A natural policy under blackout is therefore
to use the remaining certificate horizon to steer the battery toward a neutral reserve state,
often around mid-range SOC, while preserving as much useful work as possible. In this sense,
graceful degradation is neither panic shutdown nor profit maximization. It is the optimization
of useful work under an explicit safety countdown.
205

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 240 ===
29.2. Why shutdown is not always optimal 206
29.2 Why shutdown is not always optimal
An immediate shutdown policy is simple, but it can be unnecessarily expensive or even
operationally harmful. If the battery is currently providing smoothing, reserve support, or
peak shaving, an abrupt disconnect can force the rest of the system to absorb a shock. The
battery manuscript should therefore treat shutdown as a baseline rather than an unquestioned
ideal. The graceful-degradation question is whether the controller can preserve more useful
work than immediate shutdown while maintaining battery safety.
The answer depends on how much certificate horizon remains. If the horizon is already zero,
immediate protective fallback may be the only justifiable action. But if several steps remain,
the controller may still be able to navigate to a safer SOC region and taper service in a
controlled way. That is the formal basis for the chapter.
29.3 Minimum-energy safe state
Define the battery-domain neutral safe-landing state as
xsafe = mid-range SOC target,
which in many practical battery settings can be taken as approximately 50% SOC. The point
is not that 50% is universally optimal in every chemistry and market regime. The point
is that a central SOC region is generally farther from both physical boundaries than the
extremes and therefore offers the greatest reserve of safety under observational uncertainty.
A final battery manuscript should state this carefully: the safe-landing target is a battery-
domain operational convention that can be refined by chemistry, degradation model, and
market obligation, but the thesis-level principle is the same. Under prolonged blindness, the
battery should converge toward a state that maximizes distance from catastrophic overcharge
and deep discharge while still leaving room for emergency corrective action.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 241 ===
29.4. Graceful degradation objective 207
29.4 Graceful degradation objective
Let the remaining valid horizon beHt. The controller should solve a constrained finite-horizon
problem of the form
maxat,...,at+Ht
t+Ht∑
k=t
U(ak)
subject to
xk+1∈˜Ssafek+1, x t+Ht→xsafe,
where U(ak) is a useful-work functional and ˜Ssafek+1 is the reliability-tightened safe region.
This objective captures the exact tradeoff the chapter cares about: preserve useful work while
steering the system into a safer battery state before the certificate expires.
In words, the controller uses its last trustworthy steps not to maximize profit indefinitely,
but to execute a safe landing. That framing is much stronger than vague fallback language
because it turns graceful degradation into an optimization problem that can be implemented,
simulated, and evaluated.
29.5 Policy modes
A clean battery-domain degradation chapter should define at least four operating modes:
1. Normal mode: full optimization under live certificate.
2. Caution mode: certificate remains positive but is shrinking; uncertainty is widened
and aggressive actions are reduced.
3. Landing mode: the controller explicitly optimizes towardxsafe over the remaining
horizon.
4. Protected fallback: once the horizon is exhausted, only a minimal admissible fallback
action set is allowed.
A research-level battery chapter benefits from making these mode boundaries explicit, because
they connect theorem language to operator-facing runtime policy.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 242 ===
29.6. Theorem: feasible fallback existence 208
Table 29.1: Battery graceful-degradation mode ladder.
Mode Trigger condition Battery behavior
Normal Ht comfortably positive full arbitrage / grid objective
Caution Ht shrinking but positive widened intervals, reduced aggressiveness
Landing Ht≤Hland drive SOC towardxsafe
Fallback Ht = 0 admissible safe-hold or disconnect
29.6 Theorem: feasible fallback existence
Theorem 29.1(Feasible battery fallback existence). Assume the battery tightened safe set
remains nonempty at certificate exhaustion and that the fallback action family contains at
least one action that does not reduce the distance to the nearest boundary. Then a safe fallback
action exists whenHt = 0.
Proof. By assumption, the tightened safe set is nonempty at exhaustion. Let the fallback
family include a safe-hold or bounded taper action whose next-state image remains inside
the tightened safe set. Because this action does not reduce the boundary margin below zero
and lies in the admissible repair set, it is a valid fallback. Therefore safe fallback existence
follows from the nonemptiness of the tightened safe set together with the admissibility of at
least one conservative action.
29.7 Theorem: graceful degradation dominance
Theorem 29.2(Graceful degradation dominates immediate shutdown in preserved useful
work). Assume the battery has positive remaining horizon, the landing objective is feasible,
and the useful-work functional is nonnegative over the admissible landing trajectory. Then
a graceful-degradation policy preserves at least as much useful work as immediate shutdown
while maintaining the same true-state safety guarantee over the horizon.
Proof. Immediate shutdown is a special case of the feasible action sequence in which all
subsequent useful-work terms are set to zero. Since the landing problem optimizes over a
superset of feasible sequences that includes this shutdown sequence, the optimal landing value
cannot be smaller. Safety is preserved because all candidate trajectories are constrained to

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 243 ===
29.8. Safe-budget monotonicity 209
remain within the tightened safe region up to certificate exhaustion and to terminate in the
safe-landing state. Therefore graceful degradation weakly dominates shutdown in useful-work
preservation under the stated assumptions.
29.8 Safe-budget monotonicity
Lemma 29.3(Safe-budget monotonicity). As observation quality decreases and the remaining
validity horizon shrinks, the feasible useful-work budget cannot increase.
Proof. Worsening observation quality enlarges the uncertainty set and reduces the admissible
action region, while horizon shrinkage reduces the number of steps over which work can be
safely accumulated. Since the optimization is performed over a feasible set that becomes
no larger in action space and no larger in time, the achievable useful-work total cannot
increase.
29.9 48-hour safe-landing trace
A final battery manuscript should contain a dedicated 48-hour safe-landing trace. The plot
should show:
• certificate half-life and remaining horizon,
• observed and true SOC,
• target neutral SOC,
• dispatch power,
• mode transitions (Normal→Caution→Landing→Fallback).
Such a trace does more than illustrate the theorem. It demonstrates that the policy can be
interpreted operationally. The reader should be able to point at the figure and say:this is
the minute when the battery decided to stop pursuing profit and start landing itself safely.

29.11. Operator interpretation 211
29.11 Operator interpretation
The operator-facing interpretation of this chapter is strong. A battery controller should
not behave like a brittle binary switch under blindness. If a few trusted steps remain, it
should exploit them to move the battery into a safer region before service is curtailed. This
is especially important in energy systems because the battery is often supporting larger grid
objectives. A graceful landing therefore protects both the battery and the surrounding grid
service contract better than an undifferentiated emergency stop.
29.12 Chapter summary
This chapter formulated graceful degradation as a battery-domain safe-landing problem.
It introduced the neutral safe state, a finite-horizon useful-work objective under tightened
safety, an explicit mode ladder, a feasible fallback theorem, a graceful degradation dominance
theorem, and a safe-budget monotonicity lemma. Together with the previous half-life chapter,
it gives the battery manuscript a complete blackout-response story: first quantify how quickly
the certificate decays, then use the remaining horizon to land the battery safely.
29.13 Scope boundary and manuscript role
This chapter should be read as a battery-domain control extension grounded in the finite-
horizon logic of the previous chapter. It does not claim that graceful degradation is solved
for all physical machine-learning systems. It claims something narrower and more defensible:
once the battery runtime recognizes that certificate trust is finite, it can use the remaining
safe horizon to move toward a lower-risk operating region rather than either panicking or
continuing blindly.

Chapter 30
ORIUS-Bench Battery Track
30.1 Chapter purpose
The battery-first manuscript already includes a CPSBench battery chapter, but a final long-
form monograph benefits from a stronger benchmark chapter that treats battery benchmarking
as a durable contribution in its own right. This chapter therefore develops the idea of an
ORIUS-Bench Battery Track: a standardized battery safety benchmark under degraded
observation. The goal is not to claim that the full universal benchmark has already been
completed across all domains. The goal is to show that the battery domain alone is rich
enough to support a benchmark contribution that other battery safety methods can actually
use.
30.2 Why a battery benchmark is worthwhile by itself
Benchmarks matter because they define what the field measures. Before ORIUS, battery-
dispatch evaluation typically measured feasibility and value on the same observed state surface
the controller saw. That design made the hidden true-state safety gap difficult or impossible
to detect. The battery-first benchmark changes that by making the true state a first-class
evaluation object. A controller now has to pass not only economic and observed-feasibility
checks but also physical true-state safety checks under controlled telemetry degradation.
This means the battery track is more than an internal thesis tool. It is a candidate shared
measurement standard for battery safety under degraded telemetry. In the same way that a
good vision benchmark stabilizes the comparison space for perception models, a good battery
212

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 247 ===
30.3. Standardized fault families 213
benchmark can stabilize the comparison space for battery safety architectures.
30.3 Standardized fault families
The battery ORIUS-Bench track should standardize six fault families:
1. dropout,
2. delay-jitter,
3. spike,
4. stale,
5. drift,
6. reorder.
For each fault family, the benchmark should define parameter ranges, injection windows, and
replayable schedules. The point is not to create infinitely many perturbations. The point is
to create enough repeatable perturbation structure that battery controllers can be compared
fairly.
30.4 Metric suite
A final battery benchmark chapter should define at least seven metrics:
1. true-state violation rate,
2. observation-action safety gap rate,
3. violation severity (for example P95 MWh severity),
4. intervention rate,
5. graceful degradation quality,
6. audit completeness,
7. recovery latency.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 248 ===
30.5. Baseline controller suite 214
The first four are already supported strongly by the battery thesis materials. The latter
three complete the benchmark by addressing behavior under degradation, traceability, and
recovery.

This set is broad enough to show rank reversals under degraded telemetry while remaining
faithful to practical battery-control families.

Chapter 31
Compositional Safety for Battery Fleets
31.1 Chapter purpose
A single battery can be certified safe locally and still contribute to system-level danger if
it operates inside a fleet with shared constraints. This chapter extends the battery thesis
from single-asset safety to compositional battery-fleet safety. The purpose is not to claim a
universal multi-agent theory across all infrastructure today. The purpose is to show that a
deep and meaningful composition problem already exists entirely inside the battery domain.
The motivating example is simple. Suppose two batteries, A and B, share a 100 MW
transformer constraint. Each battery may remain locally safe with respect to its own SOC
and power limits. Yet if both batteries discharge aggressively at the same time, the shared
transformer can still be overloaded. Composition, therefore, is not a luxury extension. It is
the natural next safety question once the single-battery thesis is strong.
31.2 Why local safety does not imply joint safety
The composition problem begins with a negative result: local certificate validity does not
automatically imply joint validity. This is because local certificates reason about local safe
sets, while shared infrastructure constraints couple actions across agents. In a battery fleet,
these couplings include transformer capacity, feeder export caps, voltage limits, and reserve
obligations.
The battery manuscript can exploit this fact powerfully because the counterexample is easy
to state, operationally intuitive, and mathematically clean.
218

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 253 ===

This proposition is extremely useful in a manuscript because it upgrades the thesis from a
single-asset theory to a multi-asset infrastructure theory without leaving the battery domain.

31.5. Quality-score gossip protocol 220
31.5 Quality-score gossip protocol
A battery-only composition chapter becomes much stronger if it includes a communication
primitive. Define aquality-score gossip protocolin which each battery broadcasts a low-rate
message containing at least:
• current observation qualityw(i)
t ,
• local certificate status,
• local requested dispatch,
• local admissible cap under degraded observation.
When Battery A experiences severe drift or stale telemetry and its quality score collapses,
Battery B does not need Battery A’s full internal state. It only needs to know that Battery
A’s safe dispatch capacity has tightened. This is a powerful battery-domain systems idea
because it keeps composition practical.

capacity directly. Therefore any action tuple chosen within this policy belongs to the joint
safe set.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 255 ===
31.7. Compensation behavior under asymmetric failure 221
31.7 Compensation behavior under asymmetric failure
The most operationally interesting case is asymmetric degradation. Suppose Battery A suffers
a drift fault and its quality score collapses, forcing a sharp reduction in admissible dispatch.
If the shared objective still demands fleet-level support, Battery B can compensate only
within the residual joint safe set. This creates a dynamic margin-reallocation problem rather
than a naive handoff.
A strong final battery manuscript should simulate exactly this case: Battery A loses trust, Bat-
tery B increases support only within the remaining transformer margin, and the coordinator
prevents the joint system from violating the shared grid constraint.

31.9 Why this chapter matters
This chapter turns the battery thesis into a richer systems manuscript. It shows that the
battery domain is not exhausted by single-asset safety. Shared infrastructure introduces a
new class of hidden failure: individually safe batteries can still violate joint infrastructure
limits unless their certificates are composed properly. That is a real research contribution
even before any universal multi-agent theorem is claimed.

This chapter introduced compositional safety for battery fleets. It stated the shared-

and figure surface required for a publishable two-battery fleet chapter. The result is a
battery-exclusive composition chapter that significantly deepens the manuscript without

leaving the battery domain.
31.11 Scope boundary and manuscript role
The fleet chapter is written as a battery-fleet composition study rather than as a universal
multi-agent theorem for all infrastructure systems. Its contribution is to show, inside one
concrete domain, that local safety does not automatically compose under a shared physical
constraint and that a quality-aware coordination rule is needed. This is a natural battery-only
manuscript extension and should be presented as such.

Chapter 32
Adversarial Robustness and Active Sen-
sitivity Probing
32.1 Chapter purpose
The battery-domain ORIUS thesis is motivated primarily by accidental degraded telemetry,
but a final manuscript should also ask whether the runtime survives deliberate attack. This
chapter studies a battery-domain adversarial scenario in which an attacker attempts to
spoof trust in the observation channel while hiding true-state divergence. The chapter then
introduces active sensitivity probingas a battery-domain defense.
The purpose here is not to claim that ORIUS solves all cyber-security. The purpose is
narrower and more defensible: if the observation channel can be spoofed into looking healthy
while the plant is no longer reporting honestly, then the runtime should have a way to test
the channel actively rather than trusting passive health indicators alone.
32.2 Threat model
Consider a stylized attack in which the adversary freezes or forges the observed state stream
so that the battery appears well behaved while the true plant has diverged. If the attacker
can also mimic nominal quality signatures, then passive quality scoring based on missingness,
delay, and obvious spikes may not collapse quickly enough. The battery thesis should therefore
include at least one adversarial battery chapter showing why passive reliability alone is not
the end of the story.
224

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 259 ===
32.3. Active sensitivity probing 225
The strongest battery-domain version is a Stuxnet-style battery attack. The adversary
performs a man-in-the-middle replay or synthesis attack on the SCADA link. Instead of
unplugging the sensor or causing obvious delay, the attacker injects a statistically clean
observation stream showing a battery resting safely near mid-range SOC with no missing
packets, no ordering errors, and no obvious spikes. In this case a purely passive Observation
Quality Engine may continue to reportwt≈1.0 while the true battery is being pushed
toward overcharge or deep discharge by malicious or mismatched actuation.
This threat model matters because it closes the final loophole in the battery-only ORIUS story.
Earlier chapters show that ORIUS survives noisy and degraded observation. This chapter
asks whether the runtime can survive a maliciously healthy-looking observation stream.
32.3 Active sensitivity probing
The central defensive idea is simple. The controller injects a tiny, safe excitation into the
dispatch command and checks whether the observation channel reports the expected response.
Let the nominal command beat, and define the probed command
a′
t =at +ϵt,
where ϵt ∼ N(0,σ2
probe) is a small bounded perturbation satisfying|ϵt| ≪Pmax. If the
observation channel is honest and timely, then the next observed state change should be
consistent with the predicted battery response to that perturbation.
For a simple battery energy update, the expected physical echo of the probe is
δt =ηc
ϵt ∆t
Emax
,
with the sign adjusted by the local charge/discharge convention. At timet + 1 the controller
receives an observationot+1 and computes the residual
rt+1 = (ot+1−ot)−fnominal(ot,a t),
where fnominal is the nominal battery transition without the explicit probe term. Over a
rolling window of lengthK, define the active correlation statistic
ρactive = Corr(rt+1−K:t+1, δt−K:t).

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 260 ===
32.4. Probe admissibility 226
If the sensor path is honest, the residual should contain the physical echo of the random
probe and the rolling correlation should remain high. If the channel is spoofed by replay or
synthesis, the fake stream will generally fail to carry the unpredictable echo andρactive should
collapse.
A natural ORIUS override law is therefore
wt =



0, ρ active<θcrypto,
OQEpassive(ot), otherwise.
In words: if the observation path cannot answer a physical challenge-response test, trust
should collapse immediately.
32.4 Probe admissibility
A serious manuscript must also bound the probe. The probing action must:
1. remain inside the physical battery safe set,
2. remain small relative to the main dispatch objective,
3. avoid destabilizing the grid-facing service objective,
4. be infrequent enough to remain operationally negligible.
That means the correct thesis claim is not “inject arbitrary vibration into the grid.” The
correct claim is that a bounded and safe micro-perturbation can be used to validate the
honesty of the sensing path.
32.5 Detection rule
A battery-domain probing detector can be written conceptually as
if∥∆ot+1−ˆf(ϵt)∥>ηprobe, then setwt→0 or enter protected mode.
The threshold ηprobe captures ordinary battery measurement noise and model mismatch.
This detection rule is attractive because it does not rely solely on passive telemetry-health
signatures; it asks the plant a question and checks the answer.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 261 ===
32.5. Detection rule 227
Proposition 32.1 (Probe inconsistency implies distrust escalation). Assume the probe
magnitude is admissible and the expected measured response to the probe is bounded away from
zero under honest telemetry. If the observed response repeatedly fails the probe-consistency
test beyond thresholdηprobe, then the controller should strictly reduce the observation-quality
score and may trigger protected fallback.
Proof. Under the stated assumptions, repeated inconsistency cannot be explained indefinitely
by bounded ordinary noise alone. Therefore the observation channel no longer supports the
previous trust level. Since the quality score is intended to summarize trust in the channel,
repeated failed probes are direct evidence for reducing that score. If the reduced score
collapses the certificate horizon or safe-action region sufficiently, protected fallback follows
through the standard battery runtime logic.
Theorem 32.2 (Active probing safety under spoofed observations). Assume the probe
amplitude is admissible, the honest battery response to the probe is bounded away from zero
over the rolling window, and the adversary cannot predict the random probe sequence before
actuation. If the controller applies the override law above, then any sustained spoofing attack
that suppresses the physical echo of the probe will forcewt below the protection threshold in
finite time, after which the standard ORIUS safe-action logic reduces the certificate horizon and
transitions the battery into protected or degraded operation before continued blind aggressive
dispatch can be justified.
Proof. Under honest sensing, the residual sequencert+1−K:t+1 contains the probe echoδt−K:t
plus bounded noise, so the rolling correlation remains above threshold with high probability.
Under sustained spoofing that does not contain the unpredictable probe sequence, the
correlation statistic converges toward zero over the window because the attacker-generated
residual is approximately independent of the injected micro-perturbation. Therefore after
finitely many updates the controller obtainsρactive < θcrypto, which by definition forceswt
to collapse to zero or to the minimum protected level. Oncewt collapses, Chapters 28
and 29 apply: the certificate horizon contracts, the tightened safe set shrinks, and the battery
exits profit-seeking mode in favor of protected fallback. Thus the adversary may delay
normal operation but cannot indefinitely preserve aggressive control under a spoofed but
probe-inconsistent observation stream.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 262 ===
32.6. Battery attack scenarios 228
32.6 Battery attack scenarios
A useful manuscript chapter should distinguish at least three battery-domain adversarial
scenarios:
1. Frozen-state spoofing: the observed SOC is held nearly constant while the plant
evolves.
2. Delayed replay: the channel replays old but plausible values.
3. Quality-signature spoofing: the attacker preserves timing and formatting so that
passive reliability signals appear nominal.
Active probing is strongest against the third case, where passive signals alone may fail.
32.7 Execution-ready attack matrix
A strong battery-only chapter should report the following table.
Table 32.1: Execution-ready adversarial battery validation matrix with synthetic drafting
values.
Attack Passive detection delay Probe detection delay TSVR without probing TSVR with probing
Frozen-state spoofing 18 3 0.047 0.000
Delayed replay 21 5 0.031 0.000
Quality-signature spoofing >120 4 0.064 0.000
The purpose of this table is not to claim final locked values. It is to define the reporting
contract of the battery-only security chapter: passive-only trust should fail visibly under
stealthy spoofing, while active probing should convert that stealth attack into a measurable
correlation collapse and a zero-violation protected outcome.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 263 ===
32.8. Experimental design for the final battery-only chapter 229
32.8 Experimental design for the final battery-only
chapter
A strong battery-only adversarial chapter should implement three runs on the same controlled
dispatch episode:
1. baseline DC3S without active probing,
2. DC3S with passive OQE only under spoofing,
3. DC3S with active probing enabled under the same spoofing attack.
The key plots should be:
(a) observed versus true SOC,
(b) passive quality scorewt,
(c) active correlationρactive,
(d) certificate horizonHt,
(e) candidate versus safe action.
A final locked chapter should show that the passive-only controller can be fooled by a
statistically clean spoofed stream, while the probing-enhanced controller forces trust collapse
within a few probe windows and triggers graceful degradation before true-state violation
occurs.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 264 ===
32.9. Relation to the rest of the thesis 230
32.9 Relation to the rest of the thesis
This chapter does not replace the accidental-fault story developed earlier. It extends it.
Earlier chapters say what happens when telemetry degrades. This chapter asks what happens
when telemetry is made toappear healthywhile being untrustworthy. A final-year research
thesis benefits from including this chapter because it shows that the battery-only program is
deep enough to touch security, not only reliability.
32.10 Chapter summary
This chapter introduced a battery-domain adversarial robustness extension for ORIUS. It
defined a spoofing-style threat model, introduced active sensitivity probing, bounded probe
admissibility, formalized a probe-correlation override law, stated and proved a probing safety
theorem, and specified the execution-ready experiment surface required to demonstrate the
defense. The result is a battery-only security chapter that broadens the monograph without
leaving the battery-control setting.
32.11 Scope boundary and manuscript role
The active-probing chapter should be read as a battery-domain cyber-physical security
extension. It does not claim that ORIUS provides complete production-hardened security
certification. It shows a narrower point that is still manuscript-worthy: when passive health
indicators can be spoofed, a bounded physical challenge-response mechanism can restore
distrust and trigger protected operation before blind aggressive control remains justified.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 265 ===
Part VII
Lifting Battery to ORIUS
231

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 266 ===
Chapter 33
FromBatteryAdaptertoUniversalORIUS
33.1 Chapter purpose
This chapter explains how the battery-domain result lifts into the broader ORIUS framework.
The goal is not to overstate the current evidence. It is to identify precisely which parts of
the battery system are domain-specific and which parts are structural enough to be reused
across additional physical AI domains.
33.2 Why the battery result is structurally general
The battery domain is not important only because it is the first place the hidden safety
gap was measured. It is also important because it exhibits the universal ORIUS pattern in
unusually clean form: a physical plant, a degradable observation channel, a controller acting
on observed state, and a safety boundary that must hold in the true world. That structure is
not unique to batteries. It recurs in autonomous driving, surgery, aerospace, and industrial
control.
33.3 Mapping battery objects to the universal adapter
The Master Copy Plan identifies five universal adapter functions: observation-quality scoring,
true-state bounding, safe-action-set construction, useful-work measurement, and system
dynamics. Thebattery-domainimplementationalreadyinstantiateseachofthese. Observation
232

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 267 ===
33.4. What changes by domain 233
quality is realized through the telemetry reliability score. True-state bounding is realized
through reliability-aware interval or tube construction. Safe action sets are realized through
tightened feasible dispatch regions. Useful work is realized through cost, carbon, and peak-
oriented objectives. System dynamics are realized through the battery transition equations.
33.4 What changes by domain
Different domains will change the plant physics, state representation, and useful-work metric.
Autonomous vehicles will reason about trajectory and collision envelopes rather than SOC.
Surgery will reason about force, tissue stress, and procedural completion. Aerospace will
reason about flight-envelope limits and sensor redundancy. Industrial control will reason about
process-variable limits and coordinated plant safety. These differences are substantial, but
they do not invalidate the ORIUS pattern. They only change the domain-specific realization
of the five adapter functions.
33.5 What remains invariant across domains
What remains invariant is the core logical pattern: degraded observation reduces trustworthy
state knowledge; reduced trustworthy state knowledge must widen uncertainty; widened
uncertainty must tighten the admissible action set; safe control must preserve useful work
inside that tightened set; and runtime behavior should remain auditable. This is the deeper
sense in which the battery adapter becomes the first ORIUS adapter rather than just a
battery trick.
33.6 How to port ORIUS to future domains
A careful battery-first manuscript should also explain how to extend the framework without
rewriting the core. The practical answer given by the ORIUS plans is to implement the
five-function adapter in each new domain, inherit the core runtime modules, connect the
domain-specific benchmark, and validate one domain at a time. This is exactly why the
battery-first architecture matters: it creates a reusable seed rather than a one-off system.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 268 ===
33.7. Chapter summary 234
33.7 Chapter summary
This chapter showed how the battery-domain result lifts into the broader ORIUS framework
through the adapter abstraction. It clarified what is structural, what is domain-specific, and
why the battery adapter should be understood as the first completed instance of a larger
safety architecture rather than as the final universal proof on its own.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 269 ===
Chapter 34
What This Battery-First Thesis Proves
and What It Leaves Open
34.1 Chapter purpose
A strong manuscript must distinguish sharply between what has been established, what
has only been partially established, and what remains an extension target. This chapter
performs that separation for the battery-first ORIUS thesis. It also serves a second purpose:
it stress-tests the manuscript from multiple reviewer perspectives so that the final manuscript
is not only ambitious, but defensible.
34.2 What is fully validated in battery
The battery-domain thesis fully validates several core claims.
1. The hidden observed-state safety illusion is real and measurable.
2. Dual-trajectory evaluation is sufficient to expose the gap.
3. A quality-aware runtime shield can close the gap at low intervention rate.
4. Useful work can still be preserved while true-state safety is restored.
5. A first local theorem layer exists that supports the battery-domain runtime logic.
235

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 270 ===
34.3. What is only partially proved 236
Taken together, these claims are already strong enough to justify the manuscript as a
battery-first long-form contribution.
34.3 What is only partially proved
Several parts of the larger ORIUS idea are present in battery but not complete in their final
universal form.
• The quantitative Core Bound is structurally motivated and supported, but remains the
beginning rather than the end of the broader theorem arc.
• Temporal validity and graceful degradation are strongly developed in battery form but
still serve as a local reference layer rather than a final universal theorem package.
• Composition and service-layer invariants are previewed but not yet fully realized as
reusable cross-domain claims.
These are not weaknesses hidden by omission. They are genuine research boundaries that
the thesis should state openly.
34.4 What is framework-level but not yet validated
universally
The universal OASG theorem, the full cross-domain ORIUS-Bench benchmark, multi-agent
composition, and CertOS as deployable runtime infrastructure all belong to the broader
program. They are framework-level elements already visible through the battery reference
domain, but they are not yet fully validated across the multiple domains that would be
required for a universal claim.
34.5 Why battery-first is the correct sequence
The battery-first strategy is not a compromise. It is the right sequence for a serious research
program. The battery domain supplies:

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 271 ===
34.6. What second domain should come next 237
• a complete empirical proof surface,
• interpretable state variables,
• a reusable benchmark structure,
• a deployable runtime architecture,
• and a local theorem environment that is rich enough for manuscript-level proof writing.
Attempting to prove everything across every domain immediately would create the exact
scope-creep failure that the planning documents warn against.
34.6 What second domain should come next
A natural second domain is one in which the same degraded-observation logic appears but
the plant and useful-work metric are visibly different from batteries. The order matters less
than the discipline: one complete domain at a time is more scientifically credible than many
partially developed ones.
34.7 How to expand the manuscript without rewriting
the core
The modular structure of the thesis is part of the strategy. Battery-local chapters provide
completed content. Framework chapters provide reusable definitions. Later domain chapters
can be appended by instantiating the same ORIUS pattern with different plant dynamics,
different measurement channels, and different useful-work metrics. This means the present
battery-first thesis can later become the core of a broader ORIUS monograph without
requiring the complete rewriting of the validated battery material.
34.8 Four-reviewer gap analysis
A useful way to judge whether the manuscript is close to final-year research quality is to read
it through four different reviewer lenses.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 272 ===
34.8. Four-reviewer gap analysis 238
34.8.1 Reviewer 1: control-theory reviewer
This reviewer asks whether the mathematical claims are narrow enough to be true and strong
enough to matter. From this perspective, the thesis is strongest where it stays battery-local:
OASG existence, safe-set contraction, repair correctness, one-step safety preservation, and
the temporal battery extensions. The main gap this reviewer still sees is not a missing idea
but a missing level of derivation density. The way to satisfy this reviewer is to keep Chapters
16–20 explicit, assumption-driven, and honest about what is one-step, what is horizon-level,
and what remains beyond the current proof surface.
34.8.2 Reviewer 2: systems reviewer
This reviewer asks whether the architecture would matter in a real control stack. The battery
thesis is strong here because it locates the risk at the Optimize–Dispatch boundary, supplies
a real benchmark logic, and uses artifact-backed figures and runtime objects. The remaining
gaps from this reviewer’s perspective are systems hardening layers: more runtime latency
accounting, deeper fault-by-fault tables, and eventually a hardware-in-the-loop chapter.
34.8.3 Reviewer 3: machine-learning and uncertainty reviewer
This reviewer asks whether the uncertainty layer is rigorous enough and whether the con-
troller is being honest about calibration. The thesis is strongest when it clearly separates
base conformal calibration, reliability-conditioned widening, and dispatch-time repair. The
remaining gap this reviewer would still flag is the need for more subgroup and reliability-bin
analysis, particularly around the weakest solar and volatility regimes.
34.8.4 Reviewer 4: final-year manuscript reviewer
This reviewer asks whether the work reads like a mature thesis rather than a large project
archive. From this perspective, the battery-first manuscript is already strong in structure and
technical ambition, but it benefits most from deeper narrative integration: richer Chapters
5–14, more artifact-backed tables and figures in the middle chapters, and one final pass
ensuring every chapter begins with a clear purpose and ends with an explicit contribution
statement.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 273 ===
34.9. What remains to become “final-year strong” 239
34.9 What remains to become “final-year strong”
From the four-reviewer stress test above, the most valuable remaining upgrades are clear:
1. deeper systems and data-method chapters (especially Chapters 5–10),
2. more battery-specific tables and traces in the empirical middle of the thesis,
3. denser line-by-line proof exposition in the theorem block,
4. and a final hardening layer around latency, fault decomposition, and eventually hardware
validation.
These are not signs that the thesis lacks a core. They are the exact kinds of upgrades that
turn a strong battery-first manuscript into a final-year, advisor-ready monograph.
34.10 Chapter summary
This chapter separated the completed battery-domain accomplishments of the thesis from
the broader ORIUS program still ahead. It also stress-tested the manuscript through four
reviewer lenses and identified the highest-value remaining upgrades. The resulting picture is
strong and honest: the thesis already proves a meaningful battery-domain foundation, and
the work still left is the sort of hardening that strengthens a manuscript without changing its
core scientific identity.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 274 ===
Chapter 35
Research Roadmap and Deployment
Path
35.1 Chapter purpose
The battery-first manuscript is both an endpoint and a starting point. It ends the first phase
of the ORIUS program by completing a battery-domain proof surface. It starts the larger
program by defining a disciplined next-step path. This chapter summarizes that roadmap.
35.2 Submit the battery foundation first
The strongest guidance in the ORIUS planning documents is clear: the battery empirical
foundation should be submitted before anything else. The reason is cumulative. Later papers
depend on the credibility, visibility, and infrastructure established by the first accepted
battery paper.
35.3 Fix remaining empirical gaps
The analysis documents identify concrete near-term work items that improve the battery
foundation without changing its scientific identity. These include cleaned tables, per-balancing-
authority U.S. reporting, stronger solar calibration narrative, and related artifact refinements.
These are execution tasks, not conceptual reinventions.
240

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 275 ===
35.4. Extract ORIUS core modules 241
35.4 Extract ORIUS core modules
Once the battery foundation is stable, the next step is architectural extraction. The plans pro-
pose moving the battery runtime components into reusable ORIUS core modules: observation-
quality engine, reality uncertainty inflator, safe-action filter, and audit ledger. This is the
point at which the battery system becomes a true adapter instance rather than a single
application codebase.
35.5 Build the battery adapter package
The battery domain should then be packaged as the reference ORIUS adapter. This means
implementing the universal interface cleanly, documenting the five adapter functions, and
ensuring that the battery domain can serve as the example installation and tutorial path for
later adoption.
35.6 Add a second domain
The roadmap then moves to a second domain, most naturally autonomous vehicles or another
platform with rich degraded-observation dynamics and a tractable simulation environment.
The key rule is to add one domain at a time rather than attempting a simultaneous universal
launch.
35.7 Add a third domain and beyond
Once a second domain is stable, the broader ORIUS benchmark and theory claims become
much easier to defend. Additional domains such as surgery, aerospace, and industrial control
then expand the claim from strong structural generality toward the genuine multi-domain
flagship.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 276 ===
35.8. Hardware-in-the-loop battery validation 242
35.8 Hardware-in-the-loop battery validation
The thesis and analysis documents are clear that software-in-the-loop is sufficient for the
battery manuscript but not for the full long-term credibility target. Hardware-in-the-loop
validationbecomesahigh-prioritylaterstep, especiallyastheprogrammovestowardcertificate
validity, graceful degradation, and service-layer deployment claims.
35.9 ORIUS-Bench public release
A public benchmark release becomes the key infrastructure milestone. At that stage, dual-
trajectory evaluation, standard metrics, fault schedules, and controller APIs become adoption
mechanisms rather than only internal research tools.
35.10 Broader universal references as future-work sup-
port
The manuscript now carries a much larger literature base than the battery chapters need
in their main argument. To keep the battery evidence disciplined, broader cross-domain
references have been moved out of the main related-work burden and into the extended
reading index and future-work framing. This includes literature on universal autonomy stacks,
cross-domain runtime certification, and non-battery platform case studies. Those references
remain valuable, but in the present thesis they function as justification for the plausibility of
later ORIUS expansion rather than as evidence for the completed battery-domain claims.
35.11 CertOS runtime path
The final major path in the roadmap is CertOS: turning certificate-aware autonomy into a
reusable runtime service architecture. In the present thesis, this appears only as an extension
target and module map. In the larger ORIUS program, it becomes a distinct systems paper
and deployment surface.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 277 ===
35.12. Chapter summary 243
35.12 Chapter summary
This chapter summarized the research roadmap that grows naturally from the completed
battery foundation. The path is disciplined: submit the battery proof surface, extract reusable
ORIUS modules, add domains sequentially, validate in hardware, release the benchmark, and
only then complete the full service-layer deployment architecture.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 278 ===
Chapter 36
Conclusion
36.1 The central message
The central message of this manuscript is simple: a physical AI controller can appear safe
while being physically unsafe when its observation channel degrades, and battery-domain
ORIUS shows how to measure and close that gap.
36.2 What the battery domain taught us
The battery domain taught the program three decisive lessons. First, the hidden safety gap
is real. Second, the gap cannot be exposed without truth-versus-observation separation in
evaluation. Third, a runtime architecture that treats observation reliability as a first-class
control input can close the gap without blunt shutdown logic.
36.3 Why observation reliability belongs inside control
The manuscript’s deeper conceptual contribution is not only a battery system. It is the claim
that observation reliability belongs inside control rather than outside it. Once degraded
sensing can invalidate the very feasibility checks on which action selection depends, reliability
can no longer remain a monitoring afterthought.
244

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 279 ===
36.4. What ORIUS can become 245
36.4 What ORIUS can become
The battery-first manuscript does not finish the entire ORIUS program. It does, however,
establish its empirical and architectural foundation. From this point forward, ORIUS can
grow through theorem extension, benchmark release, additional domain adapters, composition
theory, and runtime infrastructure. But that growth now has a stable base.
36.5 Final remarks
A good long-form foundation is not the last word on a field. It is the first solid surface on
which the field can build. The battery-domain ORIUS thesis aims to be exactly that for
physical AI safety under degraded observation.

Appendix B
Assumption Register
This appendix is the single source of truth for the assumptions used in the battery-domain
theorem chapters. The purpose of the appendix is not merely administrative. A stable
assumption sheet prevents proofs from silently changing meaning across chapters and keeps
the notation of Chapters 15–20 aligned. Every theorem in the battery theorem block should
cite these assumption IDs explicitly rather than hiding conditions inside prose.
B.1 How to read the assumption register
Each assumption is written in four layers:
1. a short descriptive title,
2. a plain-English interpretation,
3. a mathematical form suitable for proof use,
4. the chapter locations in which the assumption is used.
The assumptions are intentionally battery-first. They are stated only strongly enough to
support the current battery-domain theorem surface. They should not be read as the final
universal assumption sheet for every ORIUS domain.

B.3 Detailed assumption statements
A1. Bounded battery model error
Plain-Englishmeaning. TheBatteryPlantisnotassumedtobemodeledperfectly. However,
the controller-side transition model is accurate enough that the difference between true and
modeled one-step evolution can be bounded by a known constant.
Mathematical form.
∥f(xt,a t)−ˆf(xt,a t)∥≤δf
for all admissible states and admissible control actions in the declared battery operating
region.
Why it is needed.Without a bounded dynamics mismatch, a repaired action could still
lead to arbitrarily different true next-state behavior than the one predicted by the tightened
safe set construction.
Where used. A1 is load-bearing in the OASG witness chapter whenever the true trajectory
is compared against the observed or modeled one, in the one-step safety theorem, in the core
bound chapter, in the No Free Safety theorem, and in the temporal extension chapter.
A2. Bounded telemetry error
Plain-English meaning. The observation channel may be corrupted, stale, delayed,
reordered, or drifted, but the resulting mismatch between the true battery state and the
controller-visible state remains inside the declared battery fault envelope.
Mathematical form.
∥ot−xt∥≤et≤¯e
for all steps considered in the theorem surface, with the error process generated by the
declared battery fault family.
Why it is needed.This is the assumption that allows the reliability-aware uncertainty set
to meaningfully contain the true state. Without bounded telemetry error, no uncertainty-
widening or repair theorem can guarantee physical safety.
Where used. A2 is explicit in Chapters 16 and 17, implicit in the battery interpretation of
the core bound in Chapter 18, and central to the constructive fault sequence in Chapter 19.

B.3. Detailed assumption statements 251
A3. Feasible safe repair exists
Plain-English meaning.Whenever the shield decides that the optimizer’s candidate action
is unsafe, there is at least one action that still satisfies the tightened safe set. Projection is
only meaningful when the target set is nonempty.
Mathematical form.

t ̸= ∅.
Why it is needed.If the tightened safe set were empty, the shield could not guarantee safe
repair by projection or fallback; the correct system behavior would instead be to escalate to
a higher-level stop or emergency mode.
Where used. A3 is critical in the repair-correctness proposition and in the feasible-fallback
theorem.
A4. Known or identified battery dynamics
Plain-English meaning.The controller is assumed to have a usable transition model for
battery evolution, including state-of-charge dynamics, power limits, efficiency parameters,
and ramp constraints.
Mathematical form. There exists a transition map
ˆf :X×A→X
used by the controller to build the safe set and propagate battery constraints.
Why it is needed.A theorem about safe action sets requires a dynamics model that tells
the controller what the next state would be under a candidate action.
Where used.This assumption appears across all battery theorem chapters because each one
relies on a meaningful transition relationship between current state, action, and next state.
A5. Monotone bounded uncertainty inflation
Plain-English meaning. When the observation channel becomes less trustworthy, the
uncertainty set may stay the same or grow, but it never shrinks. At the same time, the
inflation cannot increase without bound.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 286 ===
B.3. Detailed assumption statements 252

Why it is needed.This assumption is the structural core of the monotonicity and core-
bound chapters. It prevents the controller from becoming more aggressive as observation
quality worsens.
Where used. A5 is central in Chapters 16–20, especially in the monotone-inflation, safety-
margin, and horizon arguments.
A6. Bounded detector lag
Plain-English meaning.Severe drift or degradation does not need to be detected instantly,
but the delay in detection is uniformly bounded by a finite number of steps.
Mathematical form. There existsLd <∞such that whenever the true degradation state
crosses the detection threshold, the detector activates within at mostLd steps.
Why it is needed.The temporal theorems need a finite delay bound so that certificate
decay and horizon shrinkage remain analyzable.
Where used. A6 appears in the battery interpretation of the core bound, certificate
expiration, and graceful degradation under prolonged blindness.

Plain-English meaning.A runtime certificate or validity horizon at timet depends only
on information available up to timet. It cannot use future telemetry or future realized drift.

Let the candidate action be a dischargea∗

Case interpretation
The witness can be instantiated in at least two battery-relevant ways.
1. Stale telemetry case.The observation is not updated while the true state continues
to evolve under prior control effort, causing a fixed positive error near the boundary.
2. Drift case. The observed state evolves, but with a persistent positive bias that keeps
the controller overestimating remaining safe discharge margin.
In both cases, the proof uses the same gap-window logic.
Why the witness is admissible
The construction stays inside the battery theorem assumptions because the state remains
inside the declared operating domain before the action is taken, the telemetry mismatch
remains bounded by A2, and the model mismatch remains bounded by A1.

Interpretive remark
These two structural facts are small but fundamental. They are what prevent the runtime
from becoming more aggressive when measurement quality deteriorates.

In the battery case this means the repair step is not only plausible but mechanically correct:
if a safe action exists and projection is used, the emitted action is genuinely in the battery-

C.5. Full proof of the battery ORIUS Core Bound 260
C.5 Full proof of the battery ORIUS Core Bound
Define

Under the one-step theorem, a violation can occur only when the true state falls outside
the reliability-aware uncertainty set or when theorem assumptions fail. Inside the theorem
envelope, the operative residual event is uncertainty escape. The battery-domain risk model
upper-bounds this event by
P(xt /∈Ut)≤α(1−wt).

Interpretive cases
1. If ¯w = 1, the theorem predicts vanishing expected violations inside the theorem envelope.
2. If ¯w decreases, the expected violation burden increases linearly in average quality loss.
3. Ifαimproves while ¯w stays fixed, the expected violation burden contracts multiplicatively.
C.6 Full proof of No Free Safety
Consider a controllerπthat ignores observation quality. Construct two runs:
1. a clean run with accurate telemetry,
2. a degraded run with stale or drifted telemetry chosen so that the controller receives the
same observed-state sequence for an initial segment.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 295 ===
C.7. Full proof of finite-horizon certificate validity 261
Because the controller ignores quality, its internal logic cannot distinguish these runs and
therefore emits the same action sequence on the indistinguishable segment. By placing the
degraded run near a safety boundary and letting the true state continue to drift physically
while the observed state remains misleadingly benign, the same action that is harmless in
the clean run becomes safety-violating in the degraded run. Therefore no quality-ignorant
controller can guarantee strong uniform true-state safety across the admissible degraded-
telemetry family.
Why indistinguishability matters
The necessity proof is not merely a robustness complaint. It uses a constructive observational
indistinguishability argument: if the controller does not read quality, then there are two
physically different episodes it cannot tell apart soon enough, and therefore it will emit the
same action in both. That action can be safe in one run and unsafe in the other.
C.7 Full proof of finite-horizon certificate validity
Let the forward-propagated uncertainty tube be
Tt(0),Tt(1),...,Tt(Ht).
If every slice lies insideSsafe, then every admissible certificate-consistent trajectory remains
safe over the full horizon by direct forward invariance. Hence the certificate remains valid
over that horizon.
C.8 Full proof of the expiration bound
Lower observation quality widens the current uncertainty set. A wider initial uncertainty set
yields a larger forward tube under the same dynamics. A larger forward tube reaches the
safety boundary no later than a smaller one. Therefore validity horizon is nonincreasing in
degradation severity.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 296 ===
C.9. Full proof of the half-life corollary 262
Role of bounded detector lag
The bounded-lag assumption ensures that prolonged degradation cannot remain invisible
forever. It does not make detection instantaneous, but it prevents the certificate logic from
behaving as though severe degradation could persist indefinitely without shrinking the validity
horizon.
C.9 Full proof of the half-life corollary
Let the validity margin be the minimum distance between the forward tube and the safety
boundary over the current horizon. Under sustained degradation and bounded detector lag,
the forward tube expands or fails to contract sufficiently fast. Since the initial validity margin
is finite and nonnegative, there exists a first time at which the margin falls below one half of
its initial value. That time is the certificate half-life.
C.10 Full proof of feasible fallback existence
If the tightened safe set is nonempty and the validity horizon is positive, then at each step in
the horizon there exists at least one admissible action that preserves safety. Choosing such
actions recursively constructs a safe fallback trajectory.
C.11 Full proof of graceful degradation dominance
Immediate shutdown yields zero useful work. By feasible fallback existence, at least one
safe fallback trajectory exists. If one such trajectory yields nonnegative useful work, then
optimizing over the fallback set produces a policy that weakly dominates shutdown on
useful-work preservation while remaining in the same safety class.
Why this is a battery theorem
In a battery system, useful work means not only arbitrage profit but also peak support or
carbon-aware load shaping. A graceful fallback can therefore preserve nontrivial system value
without abandoning safety.

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 297 ===
C.12. Full proof of safe-budget monotonicity 263
C.12 Full proof of safe-budget monotonicity
As observation quality decreases, A5 enlarges the uncertainty set. By safe-set contraction,
the feasible action set cannot expand. If useful work is optimized over a weakly smaller
feasible set, then the supremum of safe useful work cannot increase. This proves safe-budget
monotonicity.

Appendix E
Reliability-Group and Calibration Au-
dits
This appendix collects supporting material for the uncertainty and calibration arguments
in the battery-first thesis. Its role is to make clear that the ORIUS uncertainty story is not
merely an average-coverage story. The battery controller must remain safe in the reliability
and volatility regimes where naive transfer from clean calibration is weakest.
E.1 Why reliability-group analysis matters
A base conformal interval can look acceptable under global marginal coverage while still
under-covering exactly the deployment regimes that matter most for safe control. Reliability-
group auditing exposes that structure by asking how calibration quality changes as the
observation channel becomes less trustworthy. In the battery domain, this is not only a
forecasting curiosity. It directly affects how aggressively the controller can act.

E.4 What the battery thesis currently proves about
calibration
The battery-first manuscript proves and argues the following carefully bounded calibration
story:
1. the forecasting layer supplies a meaningful base interval surface,
2. global marginal calibration is useful but insufficient,
3. degraded observation creates structured regimes where under-coverage matters more,
4. RAC-Cert widens the interval in response to reliability loss,
5. safe control still requires dispatch-time filtering even when widening is active.

E.6. Reviewer-style interpretation 274
Figure E.2: Artifact-backed analysis figure illustrating why the battery controller should not

E.6 Reviewer-style interpretation
A careful reviewer reading the battery thesis will likely ask three calibration questions.
Question 1 Does global calibration imply safe control?
Answer No. Global calibration is useful, but safety failures are concentrated in
degraded-sensing subgroups.
Question 2 Is RAC-Cert replacing conformal prediction?
Answer No. It wraps the base interval with a runtime trust correction.
Question 3 Why not solve everything through stronger forecasting?
Answer Because the hidden safety gap is partly about present-state trust, not only
future-state prediction.
E.7 Appendix summary
This appendix reinforces the uncertainty argument of the battery-first manuscript by organiz-
ing the calibration surface around reliability groups and volatility regimes rather than only

=== PHYSICAL PAGE 309 ===
E.7. Appendix summary 275
around global averages. Its main use is interpretive: it helps the reader understand why the
ORIUS battery adapter needs reliability-aware widening and why calibration alone cannot
close the hidden safety gap.
